About this document

This document constitutes a notice under section 22A(6) of the Water Industry Act 1991. It
sets out our decision to impose a financial penalty on Thames Water as a result of its
contraventions of the Urban Waste Water Treatment (England and Wales) Regulations 1994,
section 94 of the Water Industry Act 1991 and Condition P of its Licence. It also includes an
enforcement order under section 18 WIA91 requiring Thames Water to remedy the
contraventions that we have identified.

This decision follows the publication, on 6 August 2024, of a consultation on our proposal to
impose an enforcement order and a financial penalty on Thames Water. This document
summarises the evidence that we have gathered and sets out our findings in relation to the
issues under investigation, including why we believe that Thames Water has contravened its
statutory duties and the obligations of its Licence. It summarises the responses we received
to the draft decision and how we have taken those into consideration in reaching our final
decision.




                                                 2
         Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                             on Thames Water


Executive summary

This notice describes the findings of our investigation in relation to Thames Water’s failings
and how these have occurred. The failings relate to the management, operation,
maintenance and performance of its wastewater treatment works (WWTW) and the
collecting systems (that is, its sewer networks – the network) that are used to supply them.
These are vital assets that are used to clean and treat sewage (or wastewater 1) and which
have a direct impact on the environment, particularly bodies of water, such as rivers or
streams, into which wastewater is released.

It follows a lengthy investigation during which we made multiple detailed requests for
information and data to Thames Water. In addition, we have reviewed environmental
performance and spills data published by the Environment Agency and also considered
relevant information provided to Ofwat by stakeholders.

Having reviewed this information, we have identified that most of Thames Water's WWTW
have storm overflows associated with them that spill regularly to the environment. In 2021,
for example, over 70% of these storm overflows spilled on 20 or more occasions, with almost
30% spilling on more than 60 occasions. Thames Water has failed to demonstrate that these
spills resulted from exceptional circumstances, or that it would have been excessively costly
to address the spills from these storm overflows. Indeed, the information that we assessed
showed that at the majority of its highest spilling storm overflows, operational and asset
maintenance issues were identified as the reason for the high spills.

These high spill levels result from Thames Water not having built and appropriately operated
its WWTW, which is reflected in the number of its WWTW that failed to meet the operational
expectations of their environmental permit requirements in relation to flow to full treatment
(157 WWTW or almost two thirds of Thames Water's WWTW with FFT permits or Temporary
Deemed Consents); dry weather flow (ten WWTW) performance; or which were found to have
insufficient storm tank capacity. While the number of high spilling storm overflows on
Thames Water's network was much lower, it emerged that Thames Water had failed to
identify over 300 storm overflows on its network which had not been monitored, and in many
instances, were also unpermitted. We consider the problems identified to be systemic across
the company's asset base, and that they have been occurring since at least 2017.

The information we have gathered also showed us that Thames Water did not have
appropriate and robust processes and controls in place to ensure that it was able to properly
deliver its wastewater business in line with its legal obligations. It lacked adequate processes
to enable it to properly monitor and assess compliance with its legal and regulatory


1
    Wastewater and sewage may be used inter-changeably in this document.



                                                   3
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

obligations and, even when it was aware of potential compliance risks across its WWTW
estate and on its network, it failed to take action to address those risks effectively and with
sufficient urgency. In addition, Thames Water's senior management and Board had
inadequate oversight in relation to the compliance of its assets.

We published a draft decision for consultation in August 2024, setting out these findings and
proposing to issue the company with an enforcement order and a financial penalty. (We had
previously provided Thames Water with a copy of our provisional findings in December 2023
and given it an opportunity to comment on these and to provide any additional evidence that
it considered relevant to our investigation). In response to our consultation, we received a
detailed response from Thames Water as well responses from a number of other
stakeholders. A summary of these responses is set out in Appendix A2.


Having reviewed the information obtained during the course of our investigation and having
considered the comments we received in response to the consultation on our draft decision,
we have now reached a final decision in this case.

We have concluded that Thames Water failed to meet the following duties and obligations:

   •   to ensure its WWTW perform sufficiently in terms of design, construction, operation
       and maintenance so that spills only occur in exceptional or unforeseeable cases
       (accounting for seasonal variations), subject to best technical knowledge available not
       entailing excessive cost (BTKNEEC), under Regulation 4(4) of the Urban Waste Water
       Treatment (England and Wales) Regulations 1994 (UWWTR);


   •   to ensure its collecting systems (network) are constructed, operated and maintained
       according to BTKNEEC, under Regulation 4(2) and Schedule 2 UWWTR;

   •   to provide effectual drainage of its local areas and to effectually deal with the contents
       of its sewers, under section 94 of the Water Industry Act 1991 (WIA91); and


   •   to ensure as a company that it is run efficiently and effectively in compliance with its
       statutory duties, under Condition P of its instrument of appointment (Licence).


Based on the evidence provided to us, these failures to comply were caused by the company:

   •   failing to operate and maintain its assets adequately, and to upgrade them where
       necessary, to ensure they provided sufficient performance in accordance with
       BTKNEEC.

   •   failing to put in place adequate processes, resources and systems to enable it to
       routinely monitor and maintain operational capacity and performance and hence to



                                                 4
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       ensure sufficient performance in accordance with BTKNEEC at its WWTW and to ensure
       that its sewer networks comply with the requirements of Schedule 2 UWWTR.

   •   failing to put in place adequate processes to identify and address emerging and
       present compliance risks and failures effectively and in a timely manner and
       responding to changes over time in the catchments served by its WWTW and network.

   •   failing put in place adequate processes and systems to ensure that its data gathering,
       verification and analysis processes were fit-for-purpose to make a reasonable
       assessment of compliance and respond to variances in data quality across its asset
       base.

   •   failing to ensure its Executive and Board received and/or sought sufficient information
       and assurance on the operational performance of the company's wastewater assets
       and their compliance with relevant legal obligations. This lack of adequate oversight
       resulted in the company's management failing to be aware of and/or failing to address
       compliance failures and risks.


Thames Water’s failure to operate and manage its WWTW and network appropriately has
meant that it has not minimised the adverse effects its disposal of wastewater can have on
the environment. This failure has resulted in unpermitted and premature spills of wastewater
from its WWTW and network - where wastewater has not gone through all of the treatment
processes it is supposed to before being released into the environment. Such spills impact on
the environment and can cause harm to the health and biodiversity of water bodies, the
ability of customers to use water bodies recreationally in a safe way, and on customer and
public trust in Thames Water, the wider water sector and its regulatory regime.

Thames Water has developed plans to restore compliance at 157 of its WWTW that it has
identified as potentially non-compliant with FFT and other environmental permit conditions
(what Thames Water terms its 'sites of concern'). These plans are expected to be delivered by
the end of AMP8, that is, by 2030. The company has also accelerated the roll-out of new
monitors and has implemented new tools to enable it to have a more timely view of what is
happening at individual sites. It has introduced new operational and governance
arrangements to ensure greater oversight of what is happening across its WWTW estate and
network; has made improvements to the way in which it identifies and manages risks; and
has taken steps to ensure a clearer line of sight for its Executive and Board in relation to the
performance of its wastewater assets and the company's compliance with its environmental
obligations.

Notwithstanding the measures that Thames Water has already introduced, or is proposing to
introduce, Ofwat considers that there are additional steps that the company needs to take in
order to ensure it is compliant with its legal and regulatory obligations. We also consider that
the contraventions that have taken place are sufficiently serious to warrant the imposition of
a significant penalty in this case. Ofwat has decided, therefore, to:



                                                 5
         Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                             on Thames Water

     •     Issue an enforcement order under section 18 WIA91, requiring Thames Water to take
           particular steps to facilitate its compliance with UWWTR, Section 94 and Licence
           Condition P.
     •     Impose a financial penalty under section 22A WIA91 equivalent to 9.0% (£104.5
           million) of the company's relevant turnover from its wastewater business.

In reaching this decision we have taken account of Thames Water's financial position and
wider operational challenges in the context of our section 2 WIA91 duties. We do not consider
that any of the exceptions to our duty to make an enforcement order, as set out in section 19
WIA91, apply. We have formulated our remedies to be proportionate to achieving compliance
as efficiently and effectively as possible to the benefit of consumers and the environment and
the resilience of Thames Water's network, whilst taking account of affordability,
financeability and deliverability. It is open to Thames Water to propose alternative payment
terms for the financial penalty, that we must consider under section 22A(7) WIA91.

Thames Water is also subject to ongoing investigations by the Environment Agency in relation
to non-compliance with environmental permits. The Environment Agency is the responsible
authority in England for issuing environmental permits for WWTW and storm overflows; for
monitoring compliance with those permits; and for taking enforcement action against
companies for breaches of permit conditions.

For the avoidance of doubt, Ofwat’s findings in this notice are purely about the obligations in
respect of which Ofwat has jurisdiction. We are not seeking to make findings about
environmental permit failures or whether the acts of Thames Water or its employees, were
criminal in nature. These matters are currently being dealt with by the Environment Agency.

1.        Introduction

1.1       This document constitutes a notice issued by the Water Services Regulation Authority
          (Ofwat) to inform Thames Water Utilities Limited (Thames Water) 2 that it has
          decided to issue an enforcement order under section 18 WIA91 and impose on it a
          penalty under sections 22A(1) and (2) WIA91.

1.2       The enforcement order and penalty relate to Thames Water:

             •   breaching its duties under Regulation 4(4) UWWTR and Regulation 4(2) and
                 Schedule 2 UWWTR. The UWWTR supplement the duty imposed on every
                 sewerage undertaker by section 94 WIA91 by obliging companies to meet
                 certain minimum standards for its WWTW and collecting systems. Any
                 contravention of those provisions of the UWWTR is treated, for the purposes of
                 the WIA91, as a breach of that duty. 3
             •   breaching its general duty under section 94 WIA91 by systemically breaching
                 its UWWTR duties and thereby failing effectually to provide drainage and deal
                 with the contents of its sewers.
             •   breaching Condition P12 of its Licence in relation to the adequacy of its
                 resources and systems of planning and internal control to enable it to carry out
                 its regulated activities, particularly in relation to its obligations under the
                 Regulation 4 and Schedule 2 UWWTR.

1.3       Ofwat considers that a penalty of 9.0% of Thames Water’s relevant wastewater
          business turnover for 2023-24 is appropriate in all the circumstances of this
          investigation. This amounts to £104.5 million.

    1.4   This document provides more details of the enforcement order and financial penalty
          referenced above, together with details of the contraventions to which it relates, in
          Sections 2 to 6 of this notice. This notice is structured as follows:

             •   Section 2 sets out some relevant background on the operation of wastewater
                 network and treatment works, the environmental permits under which Thames
                 Water's WWTW and network assets operate and the relevant legislative
                 framework.
             •   Section 3 explains the nature of our investigation and summarises the
                 evidence we have considered.

       financial penalty, and the amount of that penalty.

2.        Relevant background

2.1       This section provides background information on the provision, operation and
          maintenance of typical WWTW and sewerage systems; the relevant legislative
          framework for our investigation; and our approach to enforcement of the relevant
          provisions.

2.2       The provision, operation and maintenance of WWTW and sewerage systems is subject
          to a range of legal obligations which are linked to the overarching requirements of
          Regulation 4 UWWTR. These include requirements from the environmental permitting
          regime which are aimed at ensuring that assets operate within the parameters of the
          UWWTR and do not have an unacceptable impact on the environment. In England, the
          Environment Agency is responsible for setting, checking, and enforcing compliance
          with permits and has powers to take enforcement or other regulatory action with
          respect to permit compliance. The permit sets out aspects of how the asset should
          operate and conditions around discharges. The requirements are specific to the asset
          being permitted as the nature of the receiving water bodies will vary from place to
          place, as will the volume and characteristics of the wastewater the asset is dealing
          with.


Sewerage Systems

2.3       Sewerage systems collect foul sewage from bathroom and kitchens in homes and
          offices and wastewater from industrial processes. Sewerage systems have developed
          over many years and can also collect rainwater from varying sources such as roofs and
          roads. Therefore, the flows of wastewater in sewerage systems vary substantially
          between different systems, throughout the day, and with changes in weather.

2.4       There are two broad types of sewerage systems:

      •     A fully separate sewer system has two sets of pipes for foul sewage and rainwater
            with no interconnections. Some rainfall will infiltrate into the foul sewer system,
            however, from groundwater or via manhole covers.
      •     A combined sewer system has a single set of pipes that collect both foul sewage and
            rain from roofs, patios and driveways, etc.

2.5       The catchments of WWTW are made of one, or both, of these types of systems. Older
          urban catchments typically consist of a central combined sewer system that has been
          expanded by the connection of separate sewer systems over time. In England and
          Wales most of the sewer systems operate as combined sewer systems.

2.6       Sewerage systems transport flows of sewage and wastewater to a WWTW for treatment
          before it is discharged to the environment. Sewerage systems may have storm

                                                    9
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       overflows, which enable the discharge of untreated sewage directly into a watercourse
       before it reaches the WWTW during unusually heavy rainfall. This prevents the WWTW
       from receiving more sewage than it can cope with and being overwhelmed and/or the
       sewage flowing back into people's homes. The sewage is a mixture of foul sewage and
       rainwater and is often termed storm sewage.

2.7    Storm overflows in the sewerage system are, in essence, a weir in the sewer pipe. The
       level of this weir should be far above that of the level of flow in the sewer that is due to
       foul sewage only, that is when it is not raining (termed dry weather flow - DWF). In a
       storm, flows in the sewer will increase as rainwater drains into it. If the rainfall is
       unusually heavy the flow will increase to above the level of the weir and the excess
       storm sewage will be discharged to the watercourse. Storm overflows are typically
       designed and operated to 'pass-forward' a set level of flow before such discharges
       begin. The pass-forward flow (PFF) has historically been expressed as a multiple of the
       flow level during dry weather. This is, in effect, a measure of how much the foul
       sewage has been diluted by rainwater. Where storm overflows have an environmental
       permit (permit), discharges from those storm overflows must comply with
       requirements set out in that permit. As explained below (see paragraph 2.15), these
       permits are issued by the Environment Agency, and set a series of conditions intended
       to protect the environment from harm from discharges. The PFF for that storm
       overflow is typically a condition in its permit.

2.8    Historically, a storm overflow may have been designed and permitted to discharge
       when the PFF exceeded six times the DWF. However, this was only a 'rule of thumb' to
       prompt consideration of whether the potential for environmental harm necessitated
       further action. This historical rule of thumb was replaced in the 1970s by an
       alternative calculation, often referred to as Formula A (set out below). Formula A is
       used today as a basis for setting minimum PFF rates at many storm overflows, often in
       conjunction with a simple estimation of the dilution rate of the storm sewage in the
       watercourse. The formula can be adjusted to suit local conditions, for example if a
       proportion of the catchment is a separate sewer system. More complex procedures,
       such as that set out in the Urban Pollution Management Manual, are also used to
       assess the potential for ecological harm using mathematical simulation of models of
       the river water quality and of the sewerage system.4



                     Formula A overflow setting (litres/day) = DWF + 1360P + 2E

                     And, DWF = PG + I + E

                     Where,
                     P – population (head)
                     G – water consumption per person (litres/ day)

4 Urban Pollution Management Manual, 3rd Edition, updated 7 January 2019.


                                                 10
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

                         I – sewer infiltration rate (litres/day)
                         E – average industrial effluent (litres/day)



2.9    Storm overflows in the sewer network can operate inappropriately, causing
       environmental harm. This can result from:

       •     Population growth, which can result in increases in flow, potentially beyond the DWF
             level set in the permit.
       •     Urban creep, a term that refers to the increase in impermeable areas as, for
             example, gardens are paved over, which can result in more rainfall draining to
             sewers.

2.10       If a sewer's design is not increased to reflect this higher flow level, over time both of
           the above factors can contribute to an increase in the frequency and volume of
           discharges from storm overflows.

2.11       In addition, storm overflows can operate inappropriately due to inadequate
           maintenance, with the result that they are not operating to their designed capacity.
           For example, deposits of silt or fats and oils can build up in sewers. These deposits can
           restrict the ability of the system to pass forward sufficient flow. This can result in
           discharges from the storm overflow outside of unusually heavy rain. In severe cases,
           discharges can occur even when it is not or has not recently been raining. Such 'dry-
           day' discharges 5 of foul sewage can be highly polluting as the flow is not diluted by
           heavy rain.

2.12       If the operation of the storm overflow is unsatisfactory then improvements can be
           made to either reduce the frequency of discharges or to reduce the pollutants in the
           discharge. One way in which the frequency of discharges can be reduced is by
           providing a tank that stores the excess flow so it can be passed forward for treatment
           when the storm has passed. Where a permit requires that such storage is provided,
           the minimum volume of the storage tank is set out in the environmental permit. 6

2.13       Sewerage systems, in general, operate via gravity. Where this is not feasible, sewage
           pumping stations (SPS) are used to forward flows and the flows are pumped under
           pressure through rising mains. SPS in combined systems can also have storm
           overflows. There are many similarities in the operation of storm overflows at SPS and
           on the sewerage network. However, the satisfactory functioning of SPS storm



5
  The Environment Agency currently defines dry day spills as spills occurring on any day when there
has been less than 0.25mm of rainfall in the upstream catchment on that day and the preceding 24
hours.
6
  Companies can voluntarily decide to build storm tanks and may choose to build tanks with larger
volumes than the level set in the permits.

                                                     11
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       overflows is also dependent on maintaining the pumping assets to ensure appropriate
       PFF levels are met.

2.14   SPS may also have emergency overflows. These overflows can be used when the SPS
       fails to function for reasons beyond the control of the operator, often to avoid flooding
       properties with sewage. Emergency overflows should not discharge due to rainfall,
       although it is possible that a single overflow pipe can function both as an emergency
       and a storm overflow.


Wastewater treatment works

2.15   Discharges from WWTW must comply with requirements set out in legislation and
       environmental permits. An Environment Agency permit sets out aspects of how the
       works should operate. Among other things, it sets out the volume of flow that can be
       discharged following treatment on a dry day; the maximum concentration of specified
       pollutants in such discharges; and the peak flow capacity of the works. The permit
       conditions are specific to each WWTW as the nature of the receiving water bodies will
       vary from place to place, as will the volume and characteristics of the flows received
       by the works.

2.16   WWTW which serve smaller populations (typically a population equivalent (PE) 7 lower
       than 250) generally have descriptive permits which set out, in qualitative terms, how
       the WWTW should be maintained and operated. WWTW serving five or fewer properties
       may be operated under 'General Binding Rules' regulations.8

2.17   WWTW, except those serving very small populations, continually receive wastewater,
       and thus discharge treated effluent (the final effluent or FE). The permit sets a limit on
       the volume of fully treated effluent that can be discharged when flows are not
       influenced by rainfall and/or snowmelt, termed the DWF. This permitted flow volume,
       together with permit conditions around the quality of the final effluent, seeks to set an
       appropriate pollutant load that can be discharged to the receiving water without
       causing environmental harm.

2.18   A minority of WWTW do not have a DWF permit condition but instead a condition that
       sets a maximum discharge of wastewater per day. In regulating WWTW using a DWF


7
  The capacity of a WWTW is often expressed in terms of its population equivalent. The flow to a WWTW
can contain wastewater from factories and business, alongside domestic properties. The PE metric
seeks to combine these into a common factor. In determining the PE, non-domestic flows are
converted to an equivalent domestic population, based on the pollution load from the business and
that typically produced by a person in their home. The total PE is then the sum of the domestic
population in the WWTW's catchment and the equivalent population estimated from all the businesses
in the catchment.
8
  Environment Agency Guidance, General binding rules: small sewerage discharge to the ground, 16
June 2015 (updated 2 October 2023).

                                                 12
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       limit, the Environment Agency is ensuring that it does not dis-incentivise a WWTW
       operator to treat and discharge rainwater through the final effluent route. Provided
       water quality constraints are met, there is no upper flow limit on final effluent
       discharges when flows are influenced by rainfall (or snow melt).

2.19   The DWF level takes account of the population within the catchment, the number and
       type of businesses that are also generating wastewater and the volume of water
       infiltrating into the sewer systems from the groundwater table.

2.20   DWF can be determined in two different ways 9 depending on whether it is for an
       existing WWTW with a flow meter, or is being estimated, for example in order to
       determine the impact of future population growth or when designing a new WWTW. In
       the latter case, DWF can be estimated using the equation DWF = PG + I + E, as used in
       determining storm overflow settings with Formula A (set out at paragraph 2.8 above).

2.21   Where flow metering data is available, DWF can be estimated from a statistical
       analysis of daily flow readings over a year. Research concluded that DWF can be
       estimated as the non-parametric 20-percentile value of a time series of measured
       total daily volumes (TDV). This is known as Q80. Q80 is calculated by taking the TDV
       for each day of the year and placing these flow numbers in ascending order. Q80 is the
       flow on the day that is lower than 80% of total flows recorded, that is, the 20-
       percentile (or seventy-third day), and is intended to reflect the average dry day. Using
       statistical approaches introduces some uncertainty so another measure, Q90, is used
       by the Environment Agency to assess compliance with DWF permit conditions. A site
       will breach its permit where it fails to achieve its Q90 three or more times in a five-
       year period 10. Q90 is calculated in the same manner as Q80 and represents the flow on
       the day that is lower than 90% of total flows recorded. The measured Q90 will always
       be lower than the measured Q80, and it provides greater allowance for year-on-year
       variability in the flow rates. Research has shown that if Q90 is above the DWF permit
       discharge limit, then one can have 95% confidence that the WWTW is operating above
       the DWF permit limit.

2.22   To continue to serve their intended purpose of protecting the environment, WWTW and
       sewerage system assets and their associated permit conditions must be maintained
       and kept under review to reflect any changes in the catchment they serve. Companies
       should plan ahead and identify where the risk of change may necessitate additional
       treatment or capacity. For example, when population growth due to new housing
       occurs a WWTW's capacity for DWF and its associated permit conditions on DWF may
       need to increase. The final effluent water quality permit conditions may
       correspondingly need to become more stringent to ensure the pollutant load

9 Environment Agency Guidance, Calculating dry weather flow (DWF) at waste water treatment works, 8

May 2018.
10
   While individual permit conditions vary, we understand that this is the approach that will be formally
used by the Environment Agency from 2026.

                                                   13
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       discharged to the watercourse remains within acceptable limits given the increased
       flow volumes.

2.23   Infiltration of groundwater into sewers can be a significant part of DWF. The infiltration
       occurs when the groundwater table is higher than any gaps in the pipe joints and
       manhole chambers in the sewerage system. Infiltration can also occur where sewer
       pipes are fractured and have not been repaired. As the groundwater table varies with
       the weather, infiltration rates vary accordingly. Sewerage systems should be designed
       to cope with such variations that occur under normal local climatic conditions, 11 and
       maintained to ensure infiltration is as low as practicable.

2.24   As set out in the previous section, flow in a sewerage system, and thus the amount of
       flow a WWTW receives, can vary substantially over time. On a dry day, flow at a WWTW
       typically has two peaks, one in the morning as people get up and use the bathroom,
       and then again in the evening as people cook and put on their dishwashers, etc.
       WWTW must be able to cope with these peaks, and varying amounts of rainfall.

2.25   Permits thus can have a condition to ensure that the peak flows of foul sewage are
       always fully treated.12 This is the Flow to Full Treatment (FFT) requirement. This is
       distinct from the DWF in that it aims to ensure that there is sufficient treatment
       capacity to deal with peak flows coming to the works as opposed to stipulating the
       total volume of flow that can be discharged in a day.

2.26   WWTW serving fully separate sewerage systems may not have any assets dedicated to
       the treatment of storm sewage. This is because fully separate systems are expected to
       have only a minor response to rainfall. These WWTW fully treat all flows they receive.

2.27   The physical configuration of WWTW can differ from site to site. Storm sewage arriving
       at a WWTW is typically managed in the three broad configurations set out below.
       However, other systems such as land treatment are also used.

         1. WWTW with a storm overflow at the inlet only.

         2. WWTW with a Settled Storm Overflow (SSO) after storm tanks,
         3. WWTW with both a storm overflow at the inlet and an SSO.



11 The estimation of Flow to Full Treatment requirements includes the maximum infiltration that has

been determined from flow measurement taken, preferably over several years. (See Environment
Agency Guidance, Water companies: environment permits for storm overflows and emergency
overflows, 13 September 2018, section Design standards for storm overflows). Companies should have
systems of planning in place to allow infiltration to be proactively managed in the face of challenges
such as climate change.
12
   Implicit within the estimation of DWF is an allowance for light rainfall. (See Environment Agency
Guidance, Water companies: environment permits for storm overflows and emergency overflows, 13
September 2018, subsection Flow to full treatment settings at waste water treatment works.)

       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

2.28   All WWTW can have emergency overflows, for the same reasons as set out previously
       for SPS.

2.29   Storm overflows at the inlet of WWTW operate, in essence, the same way as storm
       overflows on the sewerage network. Storm overflows at the inlet of WWTW are typically
       designed with a PFF set at six times DWF or Formula A. The discharged storm sewage
       has no further treatment, other than screening to remove coarse solids. At WWTW with
       an inlet storm overflow only, all flows up to six times DWF/Formula A are fully treated.
       The full peak capacity of the WWTW will, thus, only be utilised in heavy rain.

2.30   WWTW with storm tanks operate differently. The main purpose of storm tanks is to
       store storm sewage and return it for treatment after the rainfall has subsided. Thus,
       the treatment process does not receive very dilute flows and the peak capacity (FFT
       level) can be set at lower than six times DWF.

2.31   At WWTW with storm tanks, when it is raining, and flows to the WWTW are in excess of
       the permitted FFT level, works operators have the option within the WWTW permits to
       divert flow to the storm tanks. The diversion of flows can continue until the sewers
       have drained down to the extent that flows are lower than the FFT level. At this point
       the storm sewage in the tanks will be transferred back to the works for treatment.
       Provided the volume of diverted flow does not exceed the capacity of the storm tanks
       no discharge to the environment will occur. If the storm is intense or sustained,
       generating more storm sewage than the tanks can cope with, the permit allows the
       operator to discharge to the environment.

2.32   The point at which flows are diverted to storm tanks from the main treatment process
       is normally after screening and the removal of grit (often termed preliminary
       treatment). Further settlement of pollutants can occur in the storm tanks, and
       therefore discharges via this route may be considered partially treated. However,
       during extremely heavy storms any settled pollutants may be flushed into the river.
       These overflows are often termed settled storm overflows to differentiate them from
       other types of storm overflows. As noted above, it is also possible that storm overflows
       located in the network are provided with storm tanks.

2.33   Operators must provide storm tanks of sufficient storage capacity to protect the
       environment and minimum volumes are typically specified in environmental permits.
       All other things being equal, a WWTW with a lower storm tank volume will have a
       higher frequency of settled storm overflow spills, and thus potentially environmental
       harm.

2.34   Current Environment Agency guidance states that storm tanks "must settle out solids
       and have a minimum capacity of 68 litres per head served or a storage equivalent of




                                                 15
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       two hours at the maximum flow rate to the storm tanks".13 In our investigation we have
       benchmarked storm tank provision to a volume equivalent to six hours of DWF for the
       WWTW. This is derived from the historical asset standard which formed the basis of
       the current guidance.14 It can be applied simply to all WWTW; although it may be an
       underestimate of required provision at many WWTW, it is accepted for some WWTW it
       may be an overestimate.

2.35   For a WWTW with storm tanks, the FFT requirement is typically stated notionally as
       three times the DWF, that is the peak flow rate is taken as three times the average flow
       rate over a day, as shown in the equation below. As set out previously, the FFT level
       reflects variations in the flow rate of domestic and industrial wastewater. Infiltration is
       not expected to show such daily variation and is relatively constant, hence it is not
       subject to the multiplier. In most cases FFT is actually around 2.5 to 2.7 times DWF.



                      Flow to Full Treatment Rate (litres/day) = 3PG + Imax + 3E

                      Where,
                      P – population (head)
                      G – Water consumption per person (litres/head day)
                      Imax – Maximum sewer infiltration rate (litres/day)
                      E – Average industrial effluent (litres/day)



2.36   This 'rule of thumb' of FFT being equal, notionally, to three times DWF is suitable for
       most catchments and makes some allowance for rainwater. However, the ratio of peak
       to average flow will vary from catchment to catchment depending mainly on the size of
       the catchment, topography, and the population within it.

2.37   It takes time for sewage to flow from homes to the WWTW. In large catchments the
       peak flows from houses close to the WWTW will reach it sooner than the peak flows
       from houses further away. As the peak flows from across the catchment do not
       coincide, as they may do for a smaller catchment, the maximum peak flow will be
       lower, on a per property basis, but flows will be elevated for a longer time. Therefore,
       for large WWTW with storm tanks the ratio of FFT to DWF is often set lower than three
       times DWF. 15 However, lower ratios of FFT to DWF can result in higher levels of storm
       overflow spills as there is less capacity to treat storm sewage. In accepting a lower FFT


13 Environment Agency Guidance, Water companies: environment permits for storm overflows and

emergency overflows, 13 September 2018, section Storm sewage requirements. This asset standard is
based on a government report: Ministry of Housing and Local Government (1970). Technical Committee
on Storm Overflows and the Disposal of Storm Sewage. Final Report. HMSO London (Technical
Committee Report).
14
   Technical Committee Report, paragraph 283, page 63.
15
   In addition, the 3*DWF does not take in account the fact that infiltration does not have a diurnal
pattern. Therefore, in practice peak flow rates may be set without multiplying that element of DWF.

                                                 16
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

           to DWF ratio, it is essential to determine if the storm overflow's performance remains
           satisfactory. The meaning of 'satisfactory' in this context is further detailed below (see
           paragraphs 2.53 to 2.61).

2.38       Similarly with smaller catchments, many flows from properties can arrive
           simultaneously and create peak flows higher than the notional three times DWF. At
           these works the FFT level can be substantially higher, occasionally up to nine times
           DWF. Such works may have a tank at the WWTW inlet to balance flows, that is, to store
           peak flows for treatment later. These tanks are distinct from storm tanks as they take
           all flows and are not just utilised in response to rainfall.

2.39       If a water company is diverting flows to storm tanks, or the environment, before
           reaching, or whilst not sustaining, the FFT level required by its permit, it could be
           breaking the conditions of that environmental permit. 16 A WWTW may fail to achieve or
           sustain FFT permit requirements either because the assets themselves are not fit for
           purpose, because they have not been built with sufficient capacity, or because they
           have not been appropriately operated or adequately maintained.

2.40       Potential causes of a WWTW failing to meet performance requirements include the
           following factors.

       •     Poor site flow control, for example, assets at the WWTW that help control flow, such
             as overflow weirs, penstocks and valves, can be incorrectly set by operators or via
             remote controls and/or assets on the sites can be operated incorrectly. There may
             be limited capability to manage, or balance flows coming from the pumping stations
             preceding the WWTW.

       •     Incorrect or lacking site operating data, for example, meters may give inaccurate
             readings due to faulty installation and/or a lack of calibration. Telemetry systems
             may fail to report and/or store data results, and insufficient data may be available to
             support operations.

       •     Inadequate operational maintenance of the WWTW may result in blockages due to
             rag and/or other debris including grit build-up that can lead to a loss of the WWTW's
             intended hydraulic capacity.

       •     Inadequate capital maintenance resulting in the failure of aged and deteriorated
             assets and assets becoming obsolete or no longer fit for purpose, for example,
             screens incapable of passing flow.




16
  While a spill to storm tank in such circumstances may not result in harm to the environment as flows
may be later returned to treatment, this action will reduce the available storage that could be utilised
to avoid sewage spills.

                                                     17
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

       •     Inappropriate operation of treatment processes at a lower hydraulic capacity to
             safeguard final effluent water quality.

       •     Insufficient hydraulic capacity of assets, such as pipework, distribution chambers,
             pumping plant and treatment process units, to meet the flows coming to the WWTW
             and/or its permit conditions (even when appropriately maintained). Poorly designed
             assets can be insufficiently resilient due to a lack of asset and/or process stand-by
             capacity.



Monitoring the performance and compliance of sewerage
system assets
2.41       The conditions in any catchment change over time, putting different demands on the
           sewerage systems and WWTW serving them. The capacity and operation of these
           assets, and their associated environmental permits relating to flow management,
           need to be kept under review and changed as appropriate. Systems of monitoring
           need to cover static factors (such as storm tank volumes), factors that continually
           change (such as flow rates), and the impact of the discharges on the environment.

2.42       Figure 2.1 below shows the various stages of a typical sewerage system, from the point
           waste leaves people's homes, to entering the WWTW, to the point at which treated
           wastewater is discharged. It also shows the point at which flow may be diverted to
           storm tanks and, if the capacity of these tanks is exceeded, the flow may be
           discharged to the environment.

2.43       While sewerage systems can vary significantly, Figure 2.1 also shows points within the
           system where monitors may typically be installed. These monitors, which provide data
           on flows at various stages of the treatment process, can help companies to manage
           the performance and compliance of their assets.




                                                     18
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

Figure 2.1: Representation of a typical sewerage system




2.44   Instrumentation, including flow monitoring, is a highly desirable tool in correctly
       managing and optimising the operation of a WWTW. However, such operational
       monitors may not always be suitable for assuring compliance as they may not be in an
       appropriate location, nor measure and record the right things to a sufficient level of
       accuracy. Companies should have in place robust processes to ensure their own
       compliance. This should likely include using flow metering and other monitors unless
       compliance can be robustly assured by other means.

2.45   WWTW with a DWF greater than 50 m3 per day, and numeric quality limited, typically
       have a condition within their permits requiring final effluent flow to be monitored at
       least every 15-minutes to MCERTS Standard. 17 These meters are situated, typically but
       not always, at the point of the final effluent discharge, and record the total daily flow.
       This daily flow data, for a calendar year, is analysed to determine the Q80 and Q90 flow
       values used for establishing DWF levels. The data is reported annually to the
       Environment Agency as part of its process to monitor compliance with DWF permit
       requirements.



17 The Environment Agency established its Monitoring Certification Scheme (MCERTS) to deliver quality

environmental measurements. The scheme is based on international standards and provides for the
product certification of instruments, the competency certification of personnel and the accreditation
of laboratories.
The MCERTS scheme requires an audited management system to ensure the quality of the data from
the flow meters is independent assessed. Under the MCERTS scheme a flow monitoring system must
meet an uncertainty of +/-8%.

                                                  19
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

2.46   The Environment Agency required water companies to install Event Duration Monitors
       (EDM) on all storm overflows by December 2023. 18 The EDM records when a discharge
       to the environment starts and ends, but not the flow rate or volume. It does this,
       typically, by monitoring the flow level at the overflow weir. The number of spills and
       the total duration for which spills occur in the year, for each storm overflow, is
       reported annually to the Environment Agency and made publicly available.19 The
       number of spills is calculated according to the '12/24 spill count' method. This method
       counts multiple smaller spills that occur in quick succession as a single spill event, but
       longer duration spills as multiple spills. This ensures that the data properly reflects the
       significance of longer-duration spills, while recognising that multiple small events in
       close proximity may relate to waves of flow arriving at the works as rainfall travels over
       a catchment area.

2.47   As set out previously, WWTW have the option to spill to storm tanks when the
       permitted FFT level is reached or exceeded. To best establish therefore that a spill is
       permitted under the conditions of its permit, requires a flow monitor downstream of
       the point at which flows might spill (that is, to measure the flow going to treatment)
       and an EDM to record when a spill to the storm tank, or the environment for an inlet
       storm overflow, is occurring. With this instrumentation in place, for all the times a spill
       is indicated on the EDM, the flow meter must be reading a value equal to, or greater
       than, the FFT requirement.

2.48   Whilst the FFT requirement in permits is not new, the Environment Agency has more
       recently been working with water companies to further develop the permit conditions
       for FFT to provide additional assurance around the monitoring of FFT compliance (that
       is, the reporting requirements under the permit, rather than the requirement itself to
       meet FFT levels). For the purpose of monitoring compliance, the conditions stipulate
       that permit compliance can be considered to be achieved when 95% of the individual
       flow values exceed 92% of the overflow setting (that is, the FFT limit) when the works
       is discharging to the storm tanks, or to the environment if there are no storm tanks.
       This proxy is used for monitoring purposes in recognition of there being some margin
       of error (here, +/- 8%) in the accuracy of meter readings. Nevertheless, companies are
       expected to meet their FFT permit conditions all of the time.

2.49   Meeting this monitoring requirement necessitates the commissioning of
       instrumentation to continually monitor flows. The required instrumentation, and any
       associated civil engineering works, at many WWTW were included in the Water

storm-overflows/.

   The 12/24-hour spill count methodology starts counting when the first discharge occurs; any
discharges in the first 12-hour block are counted as one spill; any discharges in the next, and
subsequent 24-hour blocks are each counted as one additional spill per block and counting continues
until there is a 24-hour block with no discharge.

           Industry National Environment Programme (WINEP) for companies to deliver, and
           funded within the allowances companies are allowed to recover through customer bills
           following Ofwat's PR19 price review process. The investments were defined with
           reference to the WINEP driver codes given below and expected to be delivered on or
           before March 2025.

       •     U_MON3 – “Install EDM on WWTW overflows to storm tanks at those WWTW where
             we can’t use existing monitors to be confident that the permitted FFT setting is
             being complied with.”
       •     U_MON4 – “Install MCERTS flow monitoring as close to the overflow as practicable
             to record FFT at WWTW where the existing DWF MCERTS flow monitoring, or other
             installed flow monitoring, cannot be readily used to confirm the permitted FFT
             setting is being complied with when the overflow to storm tanks operates.”

2.50       As set out previously, the majority of WWTW have a TDV meter. These are typically at
           the point of final effluent discharge and not immediately downstream of any storm
           overflow weir to storm tanks or a storm overflow at a WWTW inlet. As such, they may
           not be used to fully evidence compliance with a FFT permit requirement but can give
           an indication of whether the WWTW is likely to be complying. This is because the flow
           pattern can be substantially altered by treatment processes between the storm
           overflow weir and the point of discharge.

2.51       Where existing flow meters were in place companies were funded at PR19 to
           investigate if these meters could be used for FFT compliance monitoring, under the
           driver code below. Where the meter could not be used, companies were expected to
           submit investment plans for funding suitable monitoring at the PR24 price review
           process.

       •     U_INV2 – “Investigation to confirm if any existing front end flow monitor or the
             back end MCERTS flow monitor can be used to measure pass forward flow to full
             treatment at a WWTW."

2.52       The roll-out of these meters will give companies and regulators greater transparency
           on the performance of WWTW and greater oversight on flow compliance. However, it is
           important to stress that, regardless of whether companies have or have not had
           access to data from flow meters, they have still had an obligation to ensure they were
           complying with requirements arising from legislation and their environmental permits
           with regards to flows and discharges, and the onus is on companies to ensure that
           they had systems and processes to enable them to do so.

       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

Monitoring of storm overflows

2.53   Water companies are expected to classify their storm overflows.20 There are three
       classifications; satisfactory, substandard and unsatisfactory. In summary, an
       unsatisfactory storm overflow is one that spills when it has not been raining, causes or
       significantly contributes to environmental harm or operates in breach of permit. A
       substandard storm overflow is one that has a satisfactory environmental impact but
       does not meet current engineering standards, for example with respect to screening
       requirements.

2.54   The Environment Agency can review permits or take enforcement action when a storm
       overflow becomes unsatisfactory. Where necessary, an improvement condition can be
       set to ensure appropriate standards are met. These storm overflows should be
       addressed as soon as practicable, normally within three years. The Environment
       Agency applies water quality design standards when it issues permits for all new,
       improved or altered storm overflows, that depend on the potential environmental
       impact of the discharge.

2.55   Companies are also required to address substandard storm overflows over the medium
       to long term through their routine maintenance and renewal activities. Companies are
       expected to proactively undertake activities that prevent a storm overflow becoming
       unsatisfactory.

2.56   A storm overflow can become unsatisfactory when new legal requirements come into
       effect which it does not meet. In these instances, companies, in collaboration with the
       Environment Agency, should develop a solution for inclusion in the WINEP. Ofwat
       allows water companies funding to deliver these solutions within the price review
       process.

2.57   The Environment Agency and water companies developed the Storm Overflow
       Assessment Framework (SOAF), 21 which was finalised in June 2018, to prioritise the
       investigation and remediation of those storm overflows spilling the most and
       contributing the most harm. It formed the basis for identifying storm overflow work
       programmes for inclusion in PR19 business plans – with investigations included within
       WINEP for PR19, and resulting remediation solutions intended to be included in either
       PR19 or PR24. The SOAF states that it is intended "to address the problems caused by
       discharges from storm overflows considered to operate at too high a frequency. The
       framework will ensure that the water industry is proactively monitoring and managing
       the performance of its overflows in light of the pressures of growth, urban creep and




20
   Environment Agency Guidance, Water companies: environmental permits for storm overflows and
emergency overflows, updated 13 September 2018.
21
   Environment Agency, Storm Overflow Assessment Framework. Version 1.6. June 2018.

                                                 22
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       changing rainfall patterns. It is also intended to demonstrate that sewerage systems
       are compliant with relevant legislation such as the UWWTR." 22

2.58   The SOAF sets out processes for investigation and remediation. It consists of five
       stages.

       Stage 1 - Identifies storm overflows that have exceeded spill frequency triggers 23 and
       assesses whether this is due to exceptional rainfall; due to maintenance issues; or due
       to insufficient hydraulic capacity. More specifically:
            • Exceptional rainfall – this does not refer to individual rainfall events, but
                rather the rainfall across the reporting year.
            • Asset maintenance 24 – this applies where the storm overflows and potentially
                parts of the upstream and downstream sewer network have not operated as
                designed/expected. Those storm overflows for which maintenance issues are
                identified as the cause of the high spill frequence are removed from the next
                stages of the SOAF assessment as these issues should instead be resolved
                through operational maintenance.
            • Hydraulic capacity - if the reason for a high spilling storm overflow is neither
                exceptional rainfall, nor asset maintenance, then it is classified under this
                category. This indicates there may be insufficient capacity (conveyance or
                storage) to cope with the sewage flow plus typical rainfall entering the
                network.

       Stage 2 - For the storm overflows where hydraulic incapacity is identified as the
       cause, the environmental impact of the discharges is quantified. This consists of two
       stages. Firstly, there is an aesthetic assessment, which considers amenity value,
       customer complaints and historical pollution incidents. Secondly an assessment of
       ecology is undertaken using invertebrate sampling. Where invertebrate sampling is
       not possible water quality is assessed through mathematical modelling.

       Stage 3 - The costs and benefits of improvement options for the storm overflow are
       determined. This identifies the environmental, social and economic benefits of
       overflow improvement to determine the most economically efficient improvement
       option.



22 SOAF, page 1, paragraph 4.
23
   Within the 2018 SOAF these investigation triggers are based on the number of years of EDM data
collected, more than 60 spills for one year of data; an average of more than 50 spills per year over two
years of data; and an average of more than 40 spills per year over three years of data. The Environment
Agency is in the process of updating the SOAF to use lower thresholds.
24
   The Environment Agency lists a number of different asset maintenance categories or "Performance"
reasons that companies can identify. These include: partial/no capacity due to blockage or restriction;
sewer collapse (partial/full); groundwater inundation; infiltration; asset power failure; pump
failure/issue; other maintenance/capital works; asset configuration (e.g. pumping station/rising
main/storm tanks); data collection – EDM non-representative location.

                                                   23
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       Stage 4 – A decision is made on whether an improvement option can be found where
       the costs are not disproportionate to the benefits, or, if not, the 'Do-nothing' is the
       preferred option.

       Stage 5 - Delivery of the most cost beneficial solution to reduce environmental impact
       and/or reduce the frequency of discharges.

2.59   Companies are required to submit annual returns to the Environment Agency, which
       include information on any action taken or planned for those storm overflows which
       are high spilling due to hydraulic capacity and the status and timeframe for those
       actions. This information typically indicates which stage of the SOAF investigation
       process the site has reached and, if appropriate, whether it is being investigated or
       already due to be improved as part of another programme such as WINEP.

2.60   The SOAF applies to storm overflows discharging to rivers. It does not apply to storm
       overflows discharging to lakes, estuaries or coastal waters. It also does not cover the
       requirements to meet other legal obligations relevant to storm overflows such as the
       Bathing Water Regulations 2013 and the Shellfish Water Protected Area Directions
       2016 (Shellfish Directions).

2.61   As a result of the above approach the SOAF can help companies to consider whether
       spills from their storm overflows are only occurring in exceptional circumstances
       subject to BTKNEEC. They should similarly consider this for storm overflows not subject
       to SOAF (that is, those discharging to waters other than rivers).



Regulatory developments relevant to storm overflows since the
establishment of the SOAF

2.62   The SOAF methodology, as well as the WINEP commitments referred to above,
       informed the approach water companies adopted to request funding during the PR19
       price review. As set out below, since PR19, further initiatives, policies and legal
       obligations have arisen with respect to the way the companies must or can address
       issues relating to the sufficient performance of their assets and the reduction of storm
       overflow spills.

2.63   Shortly before the start of the PR19 period,25 in December 2019, the UK Government's
       new Environment Bill was first announced in the Queen's Speech. Among other things,
       the Environment Bill was focused on the setting of long-term environmental targets for
       water and water resources management. The Storm Overflows Taskforce 26, an inter-

2.64   In July 2020, the Department for the Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra), the
       Environment Agency, the Drinking Water Inspectorate, Ofwat and the Consumer
       Council for Water announced the Green Economic Recovery Programme (Green
       Recovery), aimed at accelerating and bringing forward planned investment, and
       implementing new innovative ideas to benefit customers and the environment. 27 The
       final decisions regarding the proposals submitted by companies were published in July
       2021, making available additional funding for infrastructure improvements and
       projects related to storm overflow spill reduction.

2.65   In March 2021, the 2020 EDM return data showing the number and duration of spills
       from storm overflows was published by the Environment Agency for the first time. As
       the installation of EDMs was becoming more widespread, the data highlighted a
       greater than expected scale of storm overflow discharges into watercourses to both
       the industry and the general public. The EDM return data has since been published
       annually.

2.66   Following the publication of the EDM return data, we wrote to water companies in June
       2021 regarding the growing concern about their use of storm overflows. We told
       companies that Ofwat expects them and their boards to have a strong understanding
       and oversight of their duties and storm overflow assets and performance.

2.67   In July 2021, Defra consulted on the new Strategic Policy Statement (policy
       statement) for Ofwat, which was published in its final form in February 2022. The
       policy statement made clear that water companies must take steps to significantly
       reduce storm overflows and that Ofwat should drive companies to be more ambitious
       in their actions to protect and enhance the environment and challenge them to meet
       the requirements of all environmental legislation. Further to the publication of the
       policy statement, in October 2021, the UK Government introduced amendments to the
       Environment Bill to include a duty for companies to ensure that they secure a
       progressive reduction in the adverse impacts of discharges from storm overflows. The
       Environment Act 2021 received royal assent in November 2021.

2.68   In March 2022, we wrote to the companies to remind them of the new duty under the
       Environment Act 2021 to reduce harm from storm overflows, and the UK Government's
       expectations described in its policy statement for Ofwat. We asked companies in
       England to set out their plans to reduce harm to rivers and made clear that they 'must



government representatives and regulators. The taskforce is chaired by Defra and its objective is to
recommend actions to achieve the long-term aim of eliminating harm from storm overflows in
England.
