                                            on Thames Water

       act now' as there was nothing in the regulatory regime that prevented them from
       tackling the issues immediately.

2.69   In August 2022, the UK Government published its Storm Overflows Discharge
       Reduction Plan (SODRP). The development of the SODRP commenced with the
       publication of the Storm Overflows Evidence Project in November 2021. This was
       prepared to provide the Storm Overflows Taskforce with evidence regarding the extent
       of harm that storm overflow discharges cause inland rivers and potential solutions.
       The SODRP introduced specific, time-bound targets for companies to deliver as a
       minimum in relation to storm overflows, including, amongst others, a headline target
       that storm overflows will not be permitted to discharge above an average of 10 rainfall
       events per year by 2050.

2.70   In October 2022, Defra commenced the Accelerated Infrastructure Delivery Project by
       inviting water companies to propose accelerated infrastructure delivery schemes for
       2023-24 and 2024-25. 28 Final decisions on the companies' proposals were published by
       Ofwat in June 2023. All wastewater companies submitted proposals, and the projects
       for which funding was agreed included those aimed at addressing storm overflow
       spills.

2.71   Most recently, our methodology for the PR24 price review was finalised in December
       2022, with the draft version published and publicly consulted on between July and
       September 2022. The methodology again set out our expectations of water companies
       in light of the UK Government's policy statement and their statutory duties under
       section 94 WIA91 and Regulation 4 UWWTR, which we confirmed they had always had
       to comply with.



The relevant legislative framework


Introduction

2.72   The previous subsections provided details on the regulatory framework and context to
       our investigation. The following subsections describe the legal provisions at the centre
       of our investigation. As further detailed below, these are: section 94 WIA91 in general;
       Regulation 4(2) and 4(4) UWWTR, which are enforceable through their inclusion in
       section 94(1)(a) and (1)(b) WIA91 respectively; and Condition P12 of a water company's
       licence. These are separate legal provisions with specific requirements but there is a
       degree of overlap between the three in how they apply to the findings of our
       investigation.



28 Ofwat, Accelerated Infrastructure Delivery Project.


                                                   26
          Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                              on Thames Water

2.73      The following subsections also describe the practical implications of these provisions
          for how the companies should have been operating their businesses and how we
          expect companies to act in the future.



General duty under section 94 WIA91

2.74      Under section 94(1) WIA91, wastewater companies have a general duty to:

        (a) provide, improve, and extend such a system of public sewers (whether inside their
            area or elsewhere) and so to cleanse and maintain those sewers, and any lateral
            drains which belong to or vest in the undertakers, as to ensure that that area is and
            continues to be effectually drained; and

        (b) make provision for the emptying of those sewers and such further provision
            (whether inside their area or elsewhere) as is necessary from time to time for
            effectually dealing, by means of sewerage disposal works or otherwise, with the
            contents of those sewers.

2.75      As further detailed below, the duty imposed on every wastewater company by section
          94 WIA91 is supplemented by the UWWTR, which obliges companies to meet certain
          minimum standards for WWTW and collecting systems. Any contravention of
          Regulation 4 UWWTR is treated, for the purposes of the WIA91, as a breach of section
          94. 29

Duties under Regulation 4 UWWTR

2.76      The permit regime described in paragraphs 2.15 to 2.61 above is aimed at ensuring
          that wastewater assets are operating within the parameters of the UWWTR in certain
          respects specified in Regulation 6(2). In addition, companies remain directly subject
          to the obligations imposed by Regulation 4 UWWTR, in relation to which Ofwat is the
          enforcing authority. The aim of the UWWTR is to protect human health and the water
          environment from being damaged by untreated urban wastewater and certain
          industrial discharges. Regulation 4 UWWTR sets out specific requirements for the
          provision, maintenance and operation of WWTW and the sewerage systems which feed
          into them, in order to ensure a minimum standard of environmental performance that
          supports that aim.

Regulations 4(4) and 5 UWWTR

2.77      Regulation 4(4) UWWTR supplements a wastewater company's general duty under
          section 94(1)(b) WIA91 to effectually deal with the contents of its sewers (see more


29
     Regulation 4(1) UWWTR.

                                                    27
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       below). It relates primarily to the manner in which companies operate each of their
       WWTW. It provides:

       "The duty imposed by [section 94(1)(b)] shall include a duty to ensure that
       urban waste water entering collecting systems is, before discharge, subject to
       treatment provided in accordance with regulation 5, and to ensure that—

       (a)   plants built in order to comply with that regulation are designed (account
             being taken of seasonal variations of the load), constructed, operated and
             maintained to ensure sufficient performance under all normal local
             climatic conditions;

       (b)   treated waste water and sludge arising from waste water treatment are
             reused whenever appropriate; and

       (c)   disposal routes for treated waste water and sludge minimise the adverse
             effects on the environment."

2.78   Regulation 5 UWWTR sets minimum treatment requirements according to the size of
       population served by the relevant WWTW. 30

Regulation 4(2) and Schedule 2 UWWTR

2.79   Regulation 4(2) UWWTR supplements a wastewater company's general duty under
       section 94(1)(a) WIA91 to provide and maintain a system of sewers, that is its sewerage
       network (collecting systems). Regulation 4(2) UWWTR provides:

       "Subject to paragraph (3) below,[31] the duty imposed by subsection (1)(a) of the
       said section 94 shall include a duty to ensure that collecting systems which
       satisfy the requirements of Schedule 2 are provided—

       (a) where the urban waste water discharges into receiving waters which are a
           sensitive area, by 31st December 1998 for every agglomeration with a
           population equivalent of more than 10,000; and

       (b) without prejudice to sub-paragraph (a) above—


30
   Regulation 5(1) sets out the dates by which secondary treatment is to be provided depending on the
relevant agglomerations' population equivalent and type of receiving waterbody. Regulation 5(7)
explains that, notwithstanding the above, appropriate treatment should be provided by 31 December
2005 with respect to agglomerations with the population equivalent of less than 2,000/10,000 (for
discharges to freshwaters and estuaries and coastal waters respectively).
31 Regulation 4(3) UWWTR allows the Environment Agency and the Secretary of State to exempt

specific "agglomerations" from the requirement to have a collecting system in limited circumstances.
Paragraph (3) is not relevant to this investigation.

                                                 28
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

            (i) by 31st December 2000 for every agglomeration with a population
                equivalent of more than 15,000; and

            (ii) by 31st December 2005 for every agglomeration with a population
                 equivalent of between 2,000 and 15,000." 32

2.80   Schedule 2 UWWTR provides:

       "1. Collecting systems shall take into account waste water treatment
           requirements.

       2.   The design, construction and maintenance of collecting systems shall be
            undertaken in accordance with the best technical knowledge not entailing
            excessive costs, notably regarding—

            (a) volume and characteristics of urban waste water;

            (b) prevention of leaks; and

            (c) limitation of pollution of receiving waters due to storm water
                overflows."

2.81   The requirement at paragraph 2 of Schedule 2 UWWTR for the design, construction,
       operation and maintenance of collecting systems to be carried out according to the
       best technical knowledge available not entailing excessive costs, notably
       regarding the matters in (a) to (c), is referred to as the BTKNEEC requirement or
       'BTKNEEC test'.



Further guidance on Regulation 4 UWWTR from case law

2.82   In Commission v United Kingdom, 33 the Court of Justice of the European Union (ECJ)
       provided further context and detail on the interpretation of the Urban Waste Water
       Treatment Directive (the Directive).34 The UWWTR is the domestic equivalent of the
       Directive in England and Wales. 35 In its judgment, the ECJ examined the provisions of
       the Directive but, in the interest of clarity, equivalent provisions of the UWWTR are
       referred to below instead.




32
   This investigation, therefore, focuses on those collecting systems which are provided for
agglomerations with a population equivalent of at least 2,000.
33 ECJ judgment of 18 October 2012 in Case C-301/10 EU Commission v UK.
34
   Council Directive 91/271/EEC concerning waste water treatment, adopted on 21 May 1991.
35
   The Directive is implemented in England and Wales by the UWWTR.

                                                   29
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

2.83   The ECJ explained that to meet the objective of protecting the environment the
       concept of ‘sufficient performance’ referred to in Regulation 4(4) UWWTR (which
       applies to WWTW) must be understood to mean that under usual climatic conditions,
       and accounting for seasonal variations, all urban wastewater must be collected and
       treated. It clarified that non-compliance with the requirements of Regulation 4(4) in
       respect of the WWTW and Regulation 4(2) in respect of collecting systems could only
       be tolerated in exceptional or unforeseeable circumstances, such as 'unusually heavy
       rainfall'. The term 'unusual' is of key importance and means, again, that failure to
       collect or treat wastewater should not occur in normal circumstances.

2.84   The ECJ noted that the UWWTR do not prescribe a numerical spill threshold that would
       amount to a contravention. Accordingly, any discharge of untreated urban wastewater
       into the environment has the potential to constitute a breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR.
       From a practical perspective, Advocate General Mengozzi 36 noted in his Opinion on the
       case that the Commission had “on several occasions […] both during the pre-litigation
       phase and before the Court” indicated that “as a rule, exceeding the limit of 20
       overflows a year would be a cause for concern, suggesting a possible failure to fulfil
       obligations”. 37 While reiterating that “the Court cannot lay down numerical values
       which the legislature considered it appropriate to avoid”,38 he nevertheless expressed
       the opinion that “it may be entirely reasonable for the Commission to draw up
       quantitative guidelines on which to base its own monitoring activity”, 39 and that
       “[d]espite all its limitations and without prejudice to the need for a case-by-case
       assessment, a numerical criterion of that nature may be reasonable and
       acceptable.” 40

2.85   Within the UWWTR, the concept of BTKNEEC is only referred to expressly in relation to
       collecting systems (Regulation 4(2) UWWTR and paragraph 2 of Schedule 2 UWWTR).
       The ECJ clarified that BTKNEEC is also inherent in all of the UWWTR provisions which
       intend to secure the objective of protecting the environment. It therefore extends to
       WWTW in the context of controlling adverse environmental impacts arising from any
       discharges of untreated wastewater. BTKNEEC should be seen as a concept that
       enables compliance with the duties under the UWWTR without imposing unachievable
       obligations that could only be fulfilled at a disproportionate cost.

2.86   In Commission v United Kingdom, the ECJ held that: “[s]hould it prove impossible or
       very difficult to collect and treat all the waste water, it will be for the Member State
       concerned to demonstrate that the conditions for applying the concept of BTKNEEC are


36 Advocate Generals are advisors to the ECJ who assist the court by writing impartial and independent

opinions on cases which the judges consider before giving judgment.
37
   See paragraph 48 of the opinion of Advocate General Mengozzi dated 26 January 2012 in Case C-
301/10 EU Commission v UK.
38
   Opinion of Advocate General Mengozzi dated 26 January 2012 in Case C-301/10 EU Commission v UK
39
   Opinion of Advocate General Mengozzi dated 26 January 2012 in Case C-301/10 EU Commission v UK
40
   Opinion of Advocate General Mengozzi dated 26 January 2012 in Case C-301/10 EU Commission v UK

                                                  30
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       met.” 41 It summarised the approach it should take in considering whether the UK was
       in compliance with the Directive as follows: "For the purpose of examining the present
       action, the Court must, first of all, examine whether the discharges from the collecting
       systems or the treatment plants of the various agglomerations in the United Kingdom
       are due to circumstances of an exceptional nature, and then, if that is not the case,
       establish whether the United Kingdom has been able to demonstrate that the
       conditions for applying the concept of BTKNEEC were met." 42

2.87   Advocate General Mengozzi said in his Opinion in the same case that "[BTKNEEC]
       constitutes a mechanism which operates by way of exception and which may not be
       employed in order to undermine the principle (…) that, as a rule, the collection and
       treatment processes must cover all waste water” 43 and "there must be a
       comprehensive assessment of the circumstances of each case". 44

2.88   That does not mean that the concept of BTKNEEC can only be engaged ‘exceptionally’,
       in the sense of ‘rarely’: whether or not it is engaged will depend on the facts of each
       case. 45 But it does mean that the concept must be invoked and justified as a departure
       from the default position, and that disproportionate costs under BTNKEEC cannot be
       assumed or simply asserted without evidence; they must be proven by the party
       seeking to rely on that conclusion as a reason why spilling outside exceptional
       circumstances does not amount to a breach of the UWWTR.

2.89   Licence Condition P 46 of water and wastewater companies' instrument of appointment
       (that is, their licence) requires that the companies have adequate resources in place
       to fulfil their regulated activities, both currently and for the foreseeable future. It also
       sets out certain requirements of companies' boards.

2.90   It requires that, to enable it to carry out the regulated activities (including to meet its
       legal and regulatory obligations such as its duties under the UWWTR and section 94

Practical implications of the legal framework for water
companies' operations and our approach to our investigation

2.93   The subsection below sets out some of the practical implications of the legal
       obligations set out above and what might reasonably be expected of a company to
       ensure that it is in compliance. It explains the approach we have taken to assessing
       the conduct under investigation. For the avoidance of doubt, the below is not an
       exhaustive discussion of all issues relevant to water companies' duties.

2.94   There is a considerable overlap between the requirements of Regulation 4(2) and 4(4)
       UWWTR, section 94 WIA91 and Licence Condition P12. We have therefore sought to
       indicate how each of the provisions may be relevant to the issues we discuss from our
       investigation.

2.95   What follows are practical implications of the various legal requirements and the
       approach that companies should be taking to compliance. These are relevant for our
       investigation's findings. How we have assessed Thames Water's conduct for the
       purpose of considering penalties in relation to breaches of these obligations is
       explained separately in Section 6.

Compliance with Regulation 4 UWWTR

2.96   Regulation 4(2) and 4(4) UWWTR require companies to "ensure" that their collecting
       systems and WWTW are providing sufficient performance, subject to the BTKNEEC test,
       which cannot be assumed or asserted but must be evidenced. This means that a
       company should not have any assets which do not meet this standard, and also that it
       should take active steps where appropriate to satisfy itself that its assets are
       compliant.

2.97   Ensuring that its assets are compliant means that a company should take ownership of
       monitoring its obligations under Regulation 4 UWWTR and adopt an inquisitive stance
       with respect to the performance of its assets. Where it becomes aware of indicators
       that an asset may not be performing sufficiently, for instance because spill data
       suggests that the asset is spilling outside of exceptional circumstances, Regulation 4
       requires the company to actively consider and investigate potential issues and not
       simply assume that the asset is compliant until proven otherwise. If there is spilling
       outside of exceptional circumstances, the ECJ has made clear that a BTKNEEC
       exception cannot be relied upon unless it has been made out in the specific case.

2.98   In our investigation we have considered both the extent to which the company's
       assets comply with the requirements of Regulation 4 UWWTR (that is, in terms of
       whether they are providing sufficient performance), and the extent to which the
       company has met its obligation under Regulation 4 to ensure that its assets continue



                                                 33
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       to meet the relevant standards (that is, how it has gone about ensuring that they
       provide sufficient performance).




Spill prevention and mitigation

2.99   A company must ensure that wastewater flows to the environment (either directly or
       via storm tanks) which have not been fully treated only occur in exceptional or
       unforeseeable circumstances. Where it appears to a company that an asset may be
       spilling outside of exceptional circumstances, for instance because of the frequency of
       spills, the company should investigate the circumstances of the spill(s). If the
       discharges cannot be explained by exceptional circumstances (of whatever nature),
       the company must then mitigate any harm to the environment resulting from them to
       the extent that there is a remedy available which meets the BTKNEEC test. That
       remedy may be a change to operational processes or maintenance repairs (we note
       that under the SOAF, companies are required to resolve any operational or
       maintenance issues identified at Stage 1), or it could require physical works to change
       the capacity of the asset.

2.100 Non-compliance with these requirements might amount to a breach of Regulation 4(2)
      or 4(4) UWWTR (depending on whether the source of the discharge was the network or
      a WWTW), and consequently of section 94 WIA91. This non-compliance with Regulation
      4 is also interrelated with the general duty under section 94 WIA91 to provide and
      effectually deal with the contents of sewers and obligations as to the adequacy of a
      company's resources and systems of planning and internal control, and may be the
      result of shortcomings in these processes. A company in breach of Regulation 4
      UWWTR might therefore also be in breach of the broader duties under section 94
      WIA91 and Condition P. When assessing its compliance, a company should consider a
      number of factors, in particular the ones we identify below. These have been key
      considerations in our investigation.

2.101 The starting point to our assessment of compliance with Regulation 4 UWWTR is that
      all urban wastewater must be collected and treated under usual circumstances (in
      particular, in usual local climatic conditions). The circumstances in which spills might
      be tolerated at the first stage of the analysis are only truly unforeseeable or
      exceptional events, rather than routine departures from the norm such as seasonal
      heavy rainfall, seasonal fluctuations in the non-residential and/or tourist populations,
      or seasonal changes in local economic activity.

2.102 Spill performance of a WWTW or network asset is an important factor in assessing
      compliance. We consider that companies should treat 20 spills per year as a material

                                                 34
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       indicator of a breach which requires further investigation. This is equivalent to
       approximately one spill every two and a half weeks, which we consider is reasonably
       construed as routine spilling (that is, non-exceptional). Although this is not a binding
       threshold for compliance, as previously explained, we note that this threshold has
       been a rule of thumb used by the European Commission. Any number of spills below 20
       per year may still amount to a breach, in particular where there is an indication or
       evidence of related environmental harm for which there is an available BTKNEEC
       remedy. It is also possible that an asset spilling more than 20 times per year could, on
       investigation, be found to be compliant, for example because of exceptional
       circumstances or because of the application of the BTKNEEC test. Each potential
       breach must be assessed on a case-by-case basis.

2.103 Therefore, as a minimum, if an asset spills 20 or more times per year, in accordance
      with a company's obligation under Regulation 4 UWWTR to "ensure" asset compliance,
      we expect a company to further assess that asset's compliance with Regulation 4
      UWWTR. This would involve: (i) identifying the cause of and circumstances relevant to
      the spills (and whether or not the spills have in fact occurred due to exceptional
      circumstances); and (ii) determining their impact on the environment and whether
      there are improvements consistent with BTKNEEC that would reduce or remove any
      harm (which should then be implemented) or whether it would be disproportionately
      costly to do so. Where a company has been unable to provide evidence showing that
      there have been exceptional circumstances, no environmental harm or a BTKNEEC
      exception in relation to storm overflows having 20 or more spills a year, there is a
      strong likelihood that it is operating in breach of Regulation 4(2) or Regulation 4(4) in
      relation to collecting systems and WWTW respectively. In those circumstances, we
      would also consider that the company has failed in its overarching obligation to ensure
      that its assets are operating in compliance with Regulation 4(2) UWWTR.

2.104 We consider that storm overflows that are discharging 20 times or more where there is
      no evidence of exceptional circumstances and there is a BTKNEEC solution for any
      environmental harm which has not been implemented are clearly in breach of
      Regulation 4(2) UWWTR or Regulation 4(4) UWWTR. Similarly, we will consider the
      company to be in breach where it has identified that the discharges are caused by
      operational or maintenance repair issues, rather than hydraulic capacity issues, but
      these have not been resolved.

2.105 Further, reflecting paragraph 2.96 and 2.97 above, if a company cannot provide
      evidence that it has investigated apparent routine discharges as identified through
      spill data and taken appropriate remedial action where there were compliance issues
      in a timely way, or that it is now in the process of investigating such discharges with a
      view to ensuring compliance with Regulation 4 UWWTR, we will consider that company
      to have breached or to be in breach (respectively) of its obligations to ensure asset
      compliance under Regulation 4 UWWTR.



                                                 35
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

2.106 Other factors which are strongly indicative of a breach include: dry day spills; spills
      constituting Category 1 or 2 pollution incidents; where there was more than one
      discharge from an emergency overflow per year; assets contributing to the failure of a
      water body to meet legal obligations, such as the protection of bathing waters,
      shellfish waters and other designations such as RAMSAR 51 or SSSI 52; and EDMs failing
      to record data for at least 90% of any 12-month period. We have taken these factors
      into account in our assessment of compliance because, without reasonable
      explanation, they are inconsistent with the company actively seeking to ensure that its
      assets are operating in compliance with Regulation 4(2) UWWTR. If any of these
      occurrences are recorded, we would expect a company to conduct further
      investigation into the relevant asset's compliance and in the absence of evidence that
      there have been exceptional circumstances, no environmental harm or a BTKNEEC
      exception, we may find that there was a breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR.

2.107 It is unlikely that the company is meeting its duties under Regulation 4 UWWTR, as
      well as under section 94 WIA91 more generally or Condition P12, if discharges of
      untreated urban wastewater from its WWTW or network occur regularly.



Environmental permit compliance

2.108 All substantiated breaches of environmental permit conditions are subject to
      discretionary action in accordance with the Environment Agency's enforcement and
      sanctions policy.53 The Environment Agency is required to set permits with a view to
      securing compliance with the UWWTR, but permits are set at a point in time, while the
      circumstances applicable to an asset and its performance can change over time.
      Whilst the Environment Agency is the responsible authority in England for issuing,
      monitoring and enforcing compliance with environmental permits, in which Ofwat
      does not have a role, companies remain directly subject to the underlying obligations
      of the UWWTR.

2.109 The indication of potential compliance or non-compliance with a permit is one of the
      evidentiary factors in our assessment of whether a company is meeting the duties
      Ofwat is responsible for enforcing under section 94, Regulation 4 UWWTR and Licence
      Condition P12. As further detailed below, potential failure to comply with a permit
      might be an indication of a breach of the sufficient performance requirement under
      Regulation 4 UWWTR and, by extension, of section 94 WIA91. If it is a symptom of


51 Ramsar Convention on Wetlands. These are wetlands of international importance that have been

designated under the criteria of the convention for containing representative, rare or unique wetland
types or for their importance in conserving biological diversity.
52
   Sites of Special Scientific Interest are areas of land and water designated under the Wildlife and
Countryside Act 1981 (as amended) and typically support many characteristic, rare and endangered
species, habitats and natural features.
53 Environment Agency enforcement and sanctions policy, updated 11 December 2023.


                                                  36
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       broader systemic failures, such as a company's inadequate understanding, planning,
       resources and systems, a company might also be in breach of its broader duties under
       section 94 WIA91 and Condition P requirements. These breaches may occur, however,
       even in the circumstances where a company is in compliance with its environmental
       permits, as explained further below.

2.110 Potential non-compliance with the conditions in an environmental permit is a strong
      factor indicating that duties under Regulation 4 UWWTR are not being met. It may
      point to the asset no longer having the capacity to meet sufficient performance for its
      catchment, or to inadequate operation or maintenance of the asset, such that
      sufficient performance, although theoretically achievable from the asset's design and
      construction, is not being achieved in practice. This is particularly the case with
      evidence that a WWTW is not meeting the DWF and FFT requirements in its permit
      before discharging untreated flows.

2.111 As explained above, SPS or WWTW may have emergency overflows. 54 These overflows
      can be used when a function failure occurs for reasons beyond the control of the
      operator. Permit conditions attached to emergency overflows are necessarily strict,
      particularly so when emergency overflows are connected to foul water only assets.
      Emergency overflows are normally different from storm overflows and should not be
      used for discharges resulting from heavy rainfall or storm conditions. If these
      emergency overflows do not operate as intended, and in particular where spills are
      recorded outside of the limited circumstances in which these overflows are meant to
      be used, this might constitute a breach of Regulation 4(4) or Regulation 4(2) UWWTR
      (depending on where the emergency overflow is located).

2.112 Some permits stipulate the minimum volume that storm tanks should be able to hold.
      In addition, the Environment Agency's asset standards require that storm tanks have
      the capacity equivalent to two hours at peak flow (or six hours of DWF). If a storm
      tank's capacity is below these requirements and/or spills from storm tanks occur, a
      company might not be meeting the sufficient performance standard, in breach of
      Regulation 4(4) UWWTR.

2.113 It is illegal for companies to discharge into surface waters without a permit. The
      existence and operation of significant numbers of storm overflows operating without
      environmental permits, particularly where the company was previously unaware of the
      existence of the overflow on its own network, itself might suggest that the network in
      question has not been constructed or maintained in accordance with the BTKNEEC
      standard to ensure limitation of pollution from storm overflows and that the company
      has not ensured that the asset is performing to the requirements of Regulation 4
      UWWTR. This is especially the case where these storm overflows are not being

54 The Environment Agency will normally permit emergency overflows at SPS on the network up to and

including the WWTW inlet – Environment Agency Guidance, Water companies: environment permits for
storm overflows and emergency overflows, updated 13 September 2018.

                                                 37
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       monitored, which would indicate that a company might not be aware of how and when
       they are in operation and, therefore, might be unable to take any remedial action if it
       is required. Where there is a prevalence of unpermitted storm overflows on the
       network it is therefore likely that the company is in breach of Regulation 4(2).

2.114 A company may comply with its environmental permit but may still be in breach of
      duties Ofwat is responsible for enforcing. For instance, certain indicators, such as
      increased spill levels, may suggest that the sewerage asset in question and therefore
      the conditions in the relevant permit relating to it no longer ensure its sufficient
      performance. This might be, for example, because the demand characteristics of the
      catchment have changed since the asset and associated permit were put in place. It is
      a company's responsibility to ensure compliance with its obligations under the UWWTR
      and, where necessary, it should liaise with the Environment Agency regarding its
      assets and associated permit conditions to achieve this.

2.115 Following review of the relevant evidence, we will find a company in breach of
      Regulation 4 UWWTR 55 if it ignores certain relevant indicators and warning signs that
      suggest an asset is at risk of operating outside of permit conditions in the future or
      that it has ceased to perform sufficiently as required under the UWWTR, even though
      it remains compliant with its permit. This is because a company may have failed to
      "ensure" that its assets are operating to the requirements of Regulation 4 UWWTR. A
      company must have effective means to monitor its compliance with the permits and
      Regulation 4 UWWTR, including sufficient and accurate site operating data and
      insights. The fact that a WWTW is 'capable' of meeting its permit conditions (that is,
      has the required capacity) is not enough and a company should ensure there is
      nothing in the design or configuration of the WWTW, or the operation or maintenance
      of the asset, that impedes sufficient performance. Key examples we have identified of
      issues that companies should be monitoring are as follows:

     (i)     DWF performance - WWTW permits set out DWF limits. As mentioned at
             paragraphs 2.20, 2.21 and 2.45 above, Q90 is used by the Environment Agency to
             assess compliance with permits – a WWTW will typically breach its DWF condition
             where it fails to meet Q90 at least three times in a five-year period. However,
             where a WWTW fails to meet its Q80 metric or where it fails to meet Q90 fewer
             than three times in five years, this may indicate that the WWTW is or will likely
             not be designed, constructed, operated and/or maintained in order to ensure
             sufficient performance and is at risk of breaching its Q90 permit condition and of
             future spills. It is self-evident that sites that are operating with little or no
             headroom are at greater risk of discharging, with a consequent increased risk of
             causing environmental harm. We accept that exceptional weather conditions
             may temporarily result in high infiltration rates, meaning that it may not be

55
  A breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR is itself is a breach of section 94 WIA91 (as well as additional
aspects of section 94 WIA91 and/or Condition P12 where the evidence shows more systemic issues or
inadequate monitoring).

                                                 38
        Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                            on Thames Water

              possible for a WWTW to achieve the Q80 metric at all times, but this occurrence
              may be tolerated only if it is of short duration and is exceptional.56 We expect
              companies to investigate WWTW that fail to achieve Q80 to understand why this
              is the case and whether it is suggestive of the existence of current or likely
              future non-compliance.

      (ii)    FFT performance - As set out in paragraph 2.48 above it is recognised that flow
              meters have a margin of error of plus or minus 8% when measuring the level of
              flow. As a result, to ensure that it only enforces in relation to WWTW that are
              definitely breaching their FFT permit requirements, the Environment Agency has
              a discretion to take action where flows are measured below 92% of the FFT
              threshold. However, the margin of error of the flow meters means that even a
              recording of 107% of the FFT requirement may actually be below the permit
              requirement if the monitoring equipment is overstating the level of flow.
              Companies should also recognise the implications of this potential margin of
              error when monitoring FFT and assessing whether or not a WWTW is or risks
              being non-compliant with Regulation 4(4) UWWTR, by satisfying themselves that
              those assets that are not meeting 108% of their permitted FFT level are
              compliant with Regulation 4 UWWTR.

2.116 By way of an overview of the above, and as an illustrative example, a reasonable
      company would ensure a WWTW is designed and constructed to meet the flows
      received from its catchment under the range of typical local weather conditions. It
      would also be able to demonstrate that it is keeping the capacity of that WWTW under
      review, taking appropriate action to ensure that the WWTW continues to provide
      sufficient performance despite changes in the catchment and/or local weather
      conditions (including, where necessary expanding treatment capacity at the WWTW
      and, in a proactive and timely fashion, engaging with the Environment Agency to
      request that changes are made to the relevant permits), and ensuring that the day-to-
      day operation and maintenance of WWTW enable it to operate to its intended capacity.



BTKNEEC standard

2.117 Where spills are not due to exceptional circumstances companies must ensure that
      they satisfy the BTKNEEC requirement in order to comply with Regulation 4(2) and
      Regulation 4(4). This is a strict standard that requires the company to apply, on an
      ongoing basis, the best technical knowledge to all aspects of its WWTW and networks.




56
  Where infiltration is not of short duration, companies should either reduce infiltration levels or apply
for a higher DWF permit level.

                                                    39
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

2.118 When considering compliance with the BTKNEEC standard, a reasonable company
      would ensure that:

     (i)     The full range of remedial options has been assessed, including steps outside
             the immediate catchment and on a system-wide basis where options may
             resolve multiple concerns that individually may have been prohibitively costly to
             address, and less costly steps which, while they may not completely resolve or
             remedy the environmental harm, can decrease or mitigate it.

     (ii)    In quantifying environmental harm, all relevant issues are considered, including
             those related to public health and amenity, as well as impacts on plant health
             and biodiversity.

     (iii)   Account is taken of the latest technological advances.



Relevance of SOAF evidence

2.119 As explained in paragraph 2.57 above, water companies and the Environment Agency
      have developed the SOAF which sets out a staged approach to prioritise the
      investigation and remedy of high spilling storm overflows to enable their compliance
      with the UWWTR. This has been and continues to be an important means by which
      companies assess their compliance with the UWWTR. However, SOAF should not be
      the only way companies are engaging in ensuring that they are meeting the
      requirements of Regulation 4 UWWTR, and other operational and planning practices
      they have should also put their mind to ensuring compliance. As set out above there
      are a range of indicators that should prompt companies as a matter of course to
      consider whether their compliance is at risk on a routine basis.

2.120 In our investigation, we have taken account of the investigations that companies have
      conducted based on the evidence they have provided to us in relation to their SOAF (or
      similar) assessments of storm overflows. While this is subject to our consideration of
      all circumstances that are relevant to each potential breach, in general where a
      company:

     (i)     Has completed a timely investigation (under the SOAF or similar) and found no
             BTKNEEC solution or has a timely investigation underway and is therefore in the
             process of establishing the reason for the discharge, whether there is adverse
             environmental impact and/or a BTKNEEC solution with respect to a site, we do
             not consider such sites to be in breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR.

     (ii)    Has undertaken a Stage 1 SOAF assessment (or equivalent) and found that high
             spills over the SOAF thresholds are attributable to operational and maintenance
             issues, and has taken steps to reduce the spills such that the site is only spilling

                                                 40
        Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                            on Thames Water

              in exceptional circumstances (which, as set out above, will ordinarily mean that
              there are fewer than 20 spills a year), we consider this to have been a past
              breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR. Where a company has not taken steps to reduce
              the spills such that the site is only spilling in exceptional circumstances, we
              consider this to be an ongoing breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR. As explained
              above, any sites classified as spilling because of operational maintenance issues
              at Stage 1 are removed from the SOAF process and the companies are expected
              to address these issues.

      (iii)   Does not have an investigation in progress in relation to a site with spill levels of
              20 spills or more per year, (beyond having simply carried out a Stage 1 root cause
              assessment), we consider that site to be in breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR. In
              addition, the absence of a timely investigation is likely to constitute a breach of
              Condition P12. 57

      (iv)    Did not progress an investigation in a timely manner when spill levels of 20 spills
              or more were originally identified, or ought to have been identified, but
              subsequently progressed an investigation, we consider this to have been a past
              breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR. It is also likely that this constitutes a past breach
              of Condition P12.

      (v)     Has carried out a SOAF investigation and has identified that a BTKNEEC solution
              exists for a site but has not carried out the actions required to implement that
              solution, we consider this site to be in breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR.



Compliance with the section 94 general duty

2.121 Under section 94 WIA91, the key requirements of a company are to:

      (i)     Comply with its duties under the UWWTR both on an individual storm overflow
              level and across its asset base, including adequately applying the BTKNEEC
              standard to its operations.

      (ii)    Ensure wastewater which has been subjected to full treatment and which is to
              be returned to the environment meets the quality conditions outlined in the
              WWTW's permit.

      (iii)   Ensure other wastewater flows to the environment, either direct or via storm
              tanks, and which have not been fully treated only occur in exceptional or
              unforeseeable conditions.



57 We address the factors relevant to our findings in relation to Condition P12 further below.


                                                    41
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

2.122 As noted above, breaches of Regulation 4 UWWTR are also breaches of section 94
      WIA91. In considering whether a company may have also breached its wider
      obligations under section 94 WIA91, we consider whether there has been a systemic
      failure by the company to comply with its obligations; and whether the actions of the
      company were the actions to be expected of a reasonable company. Where breaches
      or risks of breaches are numerous, widespread or persist over an extended period, we
      are likely to view this as being indicative of a systemic failure to make appropriate
      provision for effectually dealing with the contents of sewers, constituting a breach of
      section 94 WIA91.

2.123 The non-exhaustive list below provides examples of steps and actions expected of a
      reasonable company seeking to comply with its section 94 WIA91 general duty, many
      of which mirror those needed to comply with Regulation 4 UWWTR.

Condition and performance of asset base

     (i)     Monitor the condition and performance of its asset base, identify critical assets
             and intervene through either operational or capital investment measures so that,
             as a minimum, compliance with permits and the relevant regulations can be
             assured. Where a company becomes aware of a risk of non-compliance, it will
             act immediately to address it and identify the underlying cause.

     (ii)    Take a proactive and forward-looking approach to maintenance planning for all
             assets across its asset base. This includes putting in place adequate
             management, resources and systems of planning and internal controls to ensure
             that operational performance, including flow compliance, is routinely monitored
             and maintained at its WWTW and on its network, including identifying and
             addressing compliance risks and failures effectively and in a timely manner and
             responding to changes over time in the catchments served.

     (iii)   Ensure adequate oversight by the management team and company board in
             order for them to be aware, respond and engage effectively with compliance
             failures and risks. This includes management and board receiving and seeking
             sufficient information and assurance on the operational performance of
             wastewater assets and their compliance with relevant legal obligations and
             licence conditions.

Management of emerging and present risks

     (iv)    A company is required to comply with its legal and statutory obligations as a
             minimum. It should take a system-wide approach to understanding, planning
             and managing risks and issues for the delivery of its wastewater service by
             having the right information, systems, processes, governance and capabilities in



                                                 42
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

              place. It should use all available data to assess compliance at all of its sites
              within a routine process.

     (v)      Design, construct, operate and maintain its sites to a high standard within a
              culture of compliance.

     (vi)     Use all relevant data and insights to assess trends in performance and their
              implication for the current and future sufficiency of the company's assets.
              Proactively engage with stakeholders such as the relevant planning authorities
              and the Environment Agency to ensure growth in the catchment is understood
              and the company's assets and permit conditions remain fit for purpose.

     (vii)    Ensure WWTW and network assets have appropriate levels of headroom in
              capacity so operational failures need not lead to non-compliance.

Data gathering

     (viii)   Have data gathering, verification and analysis processes in place that are fit for
              purpose to make a reasonable assessment of compliance, and respond to
              variances in data quality across its asset base. Where data is less reliable, a
              company should respond to this and potentially take a more precautionary
              approach to verification.

     (ix)     Have alternative processes to collect and verify the accuracy of data and
              operational compliance in the absence of MCERTs monitors (such as FFT meters
              and EDM on storm tank inlets). In the absence of data, or to supplement its
              available data, a reasonable company will take steps to ensure compliance
              including temporary flow surveys, visual inspections of WWTW and receiving
              waters, routine checking of weir setting, using other data such rainfall data, and
              level changes in storm tanks to provide an overall view of performance, and to fit
              monitoring equipment where feasible.



Condition P12 compliance

2.124 Companies' duties under the UWWTR and section 94 WIA91 are inherently interlinked
      with issues of adequate financial resources, and facilities, management resources and
      systems of planning and internal control at the board and/or management level in
      which Condition P12 requirements are likely to be engaged. Fundamentally, Condition
      P12 requires companies to be run efficiently and effectively, and in compliance with
      their various statutory duties.

2.125 The importance of preventing and reducing the number of storm overflow discharges
      has been the focus of various regulatory developments in the recent years, in

                                                 43
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

      particular since PR19. As set out above, the SOAF guidance was finalised in June 2018,
      with its opening paragraphs stating that it is "intended to demonstrate that sewerage
      systems are compliant with relevant legislation such as the UWWTR". This has put the
      industry on notice that, at the very least, sites meeting the relevant spill thresholds
      under the SOAF are at risk of not complying with that legislation and therefore require
      attention. A number of subsequent regulatory developments further highlighted the
      importance of reducing the number of potentially harmful storm overflow discharges,
      in particular: the coming into force of the Environment Act 2021; the development of
      the SODRP; the accelerated funding being made available under the Green Recovery
      and Accelerated Infrastructure Delivery Project; the roll-out of EDMs and publication
      of the EDM data; as well as communications from the UK Government regarding
      priorities for regulators and wastewater companies.

2.126 The above developments should have informed, and should continue to inform, the
      way companies ensure they are meeting their obligations. The companies should
      similarly take into account any subsequent developments relating to regulatory
      expectations on compliance.

2.127 In recognition of some of the interlinkages with expectations of Regulation 4 UWWTR
      and section 94 WIA91 set out above, the list below further outlines key aspects of how
      a company would behave to ensure compliance with Condition P12:

     (i)     Have processes in place to understand and routinely assess its compliance with
             its legal obligations at every WWTW and network storm overflow, based on actual
             performance rather than the asset's potential capacity.

     (ii)    Have systems in place to escalate actual or risk of non-compliance to prompt
             timely investigation and resolution and prioritise securing compliance.

     (iii)   Have internal controls that follow-up interventions to ensure effective
             compliance.

     (iv)    Ensure operational staff are enabled, by the right information, processes, and
             culture, to understand and prioritise ensuring compliance and to speak up
             where they identify risks to compliance.

     (v)     Have appropriate checks and balances in place to assure monitoring and
             reporting information from operational teams.

     (vi)    Ensure that executives and Board request, receive and meaningfully engage with
             accurate reports on the company’s environmental compliance position and
             resolution of non-compliance, including metrics that go beyond a narrow set of
             price review performance commitments or Environment Agency Environmental
             Performance Assessment metrics.

                                                 44
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

     (vii)    Ensure that its assets are operating, at least, within the parameters of the
              relevant environmental permits.

     (viii)   Have a minimal number of high spilling sites attributable to operational issues
              (as we expect for these to be dealt with as part of a company's routine asset
              maintenance), and where operational issues arise, these are resolved swiftly.

     (ix)     Review its EDM data at least on an annual basis to understand which storm
              overflows should be subject to investigations (as opposed to, for example, only
              reviewing the data once every five years for price review purposes).

     (x)      Progress SOAF investigations for the sites that meet the trigger thresholds under
              the SOAF through their various stages in a timely manner, including the delivery
              of any BTKNEEC solutions identified (that is, not unreasonably delaying viable
              solutions until the following price review period once they have been identified
              as necessary for compliance with the UWWTR).

     (xi)     Not consider the SOAF and WINEP processes to be the only means of satisfying
              the company's obligations under the UWWTR (that is, taking up other
              opportunities to fund and progress solutions where they are needed to ensure
              compliance).

2.128 As a result of the above expectations and the relevant regulatory developments, the
      key evidential factors we take into account in our assessment of a company's conduct
      under Condition P12 in this investigation are:

     (i)      The coverage and pace of the company's EDM roll-out, and the degree to which
              the EDM roll-out revealed high storm overflow spills numbers, indicating that the
              company's previous processes for designing, constructing, operating and
              maintaining assets was deficient.

     (ii)     The frequency with which the company has assessed its EDM data ahead of and
              during the PR19 period against the thresholds which trigger Stage 1 of the SOAF
              (that is, is the cohort of sites it is progressing through the SOAF investigation set
              at a point in time, or refreshed at least in light of the latest annual EDM data).

     (iii)    The number and proportion of sites identified as high spilling for operational
              purposes at Stage 1, and the pace at which the company resolves or reduces
              spills at these sites.

     (iv)     The number and proportion of sites for which environmental harm is identified at
              Stage 2 of the SOAF.




                                                 45
        Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                            on Thames Water

      (v)      Whether the company has progressed all sites which meet the thresholds for the
               SOAF through all of the SOAF stages, and at reasonable pace given the times at
               which they were triggered.

      (vi)     Whether the company has provided evidence of other means of monitoring and
               investigating sites for which the SOAF does not apply, to satisfy the BTKNEEC test
               (for example, in relation to coastal, bathing, and shellfish waters).

      (vii)    The number and proportion of sites for which the company has identified
               BTKNEEC solutions (through the SOAF and/or other assessment processes), and
               if these have been delivered or are planned to be achieved at reasonable pace
               (for example, the timescales between the completion of Stage 4 and Stage 5 of
               the SOAF).

      (viii)   The extent to which the company has responded to further regulatory
               developments and funding opportunities during PR19 to bring forward further
               investigations and solutions for high spilling sites (for example, Green Recovery,
               Accelerated Infrastructure Delivery Project, or shareholder funding).

      (ix)     The failure of a company to actively investigate and deliver a BTKNEEC solution in
               response to high spilling sites is in itself a failure with respect to Condition P12
               obligations to have adequate resources and systems of planning and internal
               controls to carry out its regulated activities (in relation to its obligations under
               the UWWTR and section 94 WIA91).



Ofwat's powers

2.129 Breaches of the general duty under section 94, Regulation 4 UWWTR, and licence
      conditions are enforceable by Ofwat under section 18 WIA91.58 If we are satisfied that
      there is, or there is likely to be, a breach, Ofwat is under a duty to impose an
      enforcement order. The purpose of an enforcement order is to secure compliance with
      the relevant obligation.

2.130 Section 19 WIA91 provides limited exceptions to the duty to enforce, including where:
      (i) the contravention is of a trivial nature; or (ii) the company has given a formal
      undertaking to take remedial steps which we consider appropriate to secure
      compliance and is complying with that undertaking. In the event that a company fails
      to comply with an undertaking, Ofwat is under a further duty to pursue an

58 Ofwat has been given a general authorisation by the Secretary of State for the purposes of section

94(3)(b) WIA91, originally signed by the Secretary of State on 27 November 1990 for the Director General
of Water Services under the Water Act 1989. There is nothing in section 94 or in the UWWTR which limits
the ability of either Ofwat or the Secretary of State to enforce the obligations under section 94 WIA91 or
Regulation 4 UWWTR.

                                                   46
          Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                              on Thames Water

          enforcement order to ensure the company’s compliance. We provide further
          information on our approach to enforcement orders in Section 5.

2.131 If we are satisfied that a company has contravened or is contravening any condition of
      its licence or a statutory requirement, we may impose a financial penalty under
      section 22A(1) and (2) WIA91. We will consider a number of factors in determining the
      appropriate level of the penalty in the circumstances of each breach, ensuring that
      the amount does not exceed 10% of a company's relevant turnover in the preceding
      year. We explain our approach to calculating financial penalties and the relevant
      statutory provisions more fully in Section 6 and Appendix A7.

2.132 We will consider regulatory settlement where our powers allow us to do so.

2.133 Regulatory settlement may be accepted in lieu of all or part of a financial penalty. It
      can include financial and/or non-financial commitments from the company such as a
      redress package for customers or remediation of a failure. On a case-by-case basis,
      we will decide whether the content of any settlement proposed by a company is
      acceptable. We are open to companies engaging in proactive discussions with us
      about potential settlement.

2.134 Before making an enforcement order or confirming a provisional enforcement order 59
      and/or imposing a penalty, 60 we are required to consider whether it would be more
      appropriate to proceed under the Competition Act 1998 (CA98).

 3. Our investigation

 Background

3.1   On 18 November 2021, Ofwat and the Environment Agency announced investigations into
      all water and wastewater companies in England and Wales. This was prompted by
      companies revealing to the Storm Overflows Taskforce 61 that data from the installation of
      new EDM and flow measurement devices at their WWTW had indicated that they may not
      be complying with the FFT requirement in their environmental permits. As a result, some
      WWTW might not be treating as much sewage as they should be, which could be
      resulting in sewage discharges into storm tanks and/or the environment at times when
      this should not be happening.

3.2   On the same day, we wrote to all water and wastewater companies in England and Wales
      requesting that they provide a range of information to us. This included:

         •   Providing details about the number of WWTW the company had that might not be
             meeting the FFT requirements of their environmental permits, and an estimate of
             the discharges to the environment this might have caused;

         •   If this was happening, explaining the reasons why;

         •   Explaining how the company managed its WWTW to ensure they meet the FFT
             requirements of their environmental permits;

         •   Setting out what role their board plays in ensuring the company is meeting its FFT
             requirements;

         •   Describing how the company’s board considers the company’s environmental
             performance when making its decisions about the dividends and executive pay
             and bonuses the company pays out; and

         •   Setting out their plans and timelines to put right any problems uncovered.

3.3   We received responses from all companies in December 2021.




 61
   The Storm Overflows Taskforce is a joint group which was established in August 2020 with
 membership drawn from the water industry, environmental non-governmental organisations,
 government representatives and regulators. The taskforce is chaired by the Department for the
 Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra). Its objective is to recommend actions to achieve the long-
 term aim of eliminating harm from storm overflows in England.

        Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                            on Thames Water

      as potentially non-compliant with its FFT permit conditions in its December 2021
      response. We also had follow-on questions about:

        •   the methodology which Thames Water had used to assess and identify FFT non-
            compliance and its root causes, and whether this methodology was sufficiently
            robust; and/or

        •   the adequacy of the resources and systems of planning and internal control
            Thames Water had in place to ensure compliance with FFT conditions and its
            duties under the UWWTR and section 94 WIA91.


3.5   In March 2022 we announced that we had opened an enforcement case into Thames
      Water 62 and served it with a formal notice under section 203 WIA9163. In that notice, we
      explained that based on its December 2021 response, it appeared to Ofwat that Thames
      Water may have contravened, be contravening, or be likely to contravene, the following:

        a. Paragraph 12 of Condition P in relation to the adequacy of its resources and
           systems of planning and internal control to enable it to carry out its regulated
           activities, particularly in relation to its obligations under section 94(1)(b) of the
           WIA91; and/or

        b. The requirements of its general duty under Section 94 of the WIA91 to provide a
           sewerage system (in particular Section 94(1)(b) in relation to its duty to make
           provision for dealing effectually, by means of sewage disposal works or otherwise,
           with the contents of the sewers in its sewerage system) and including but not
           limited to breaches of Regulations 4(4) and 5 of the Urban Wastewater Treatment
           (England and Wales) Regulations 1994.

      information and data to Thames Water to enable us to gain an understanding of the
      company's overall compliance position with its obligations under section 94 and

       WIA91 and Condition P of its licence and our proposal to impose a financial penalty in

3.12   Having considered these responses, and having taken account of the updated
       information provided by Thames Water, in the following sections we set out the range of

  The scale of potential FFT non-compliance at Thames Water's
  WWTW

3.13   Within its area of appointment, Thames Water treats and recycles wastewater at 354
       WWTW. Of these, Thames Water told us that 197 have environmental permits which
       include a specific FFT requirement. It also has a further 39 WWTW which operate under
       Temporary Deemed Consents (TDCs) 66. These are sites which, whilst they currently do
       not have a specific FFT requirement, are expected to treat the amount of flow the site
       was designed and funded to treat, but are yet to be updated into an Environmental
       Permitting Regulations (EPR) style permit.

3.14   In response to our section 203 notices, Thames Water provided information on the
       number of its WWTW that it considered could be potentially non-compliant with FFT
       permit requirements. This included the sites with specific FFT permit conditions as well
       as those with TDCs.

3.15   Thames Water explained that in the absence of Spill-to-Storm (U_MON3) and FFT
       (U_MON4) monitors its ability to fully, and accurately, assess FFT compliance at its sites
       has been limited. 67 In the past, it relied on data from TDV or FE flow meters to indicate

  66
     According to Thames Water, TDCs were issued for sites with storm discharges by the National Rivers
  Authority (at the time) at privatisation and did not include specific FFT requirements. Since then,
  these consents have been determined by the Environment Agency, but they have yet to be updated
  into an Environmental Permitting Regulations (EPR) style permit. In the meantime, the Environment
  Agency expects these sites to treat the amount of flow the site was designed and funded to treat. In its
  January 2024 response, Thames Water told us that it is currently liaising with the Environment Agency
  to update certain of these TDCs to new environmental permits which will include a FFT requirement.
  67
     In 2020 Thames Water had 197 WWTW with a FFT permit requirement but none of these had MCERTS
  FFT monitors and only one site had an EDM on the spill to storm tank. As explained in following
  sections, it has now accelerated the roll-out of U_MON3 and U_MON4 monitors at its WWTW.]

                                                     51
          Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                              on Thames Water

       whether a site was likely to be meeting its FFT permit conditions but Thames Water also
       stressed that as there are significant deficiencies in this approach, this method of
       assessing FFT compliance is only an indicator.68 More recently, as EDM data on spills to
       the environment has become available, it has used the methodology recommended by
       UK Water Industry Research (UKWIR) 69 which uses data from both MCERTS TDV
       monitors and EDM spill-to-environment monitors. This is the methodology that Thames
       Water used in assessing the FFT compliance of its sites for its responses to our section
       203 notices.

3.16   In those responses, Thames Water continued to caveat the accuracy of this assessment
       due to limitations with the source data and the methodology used. It told us that the
       outputs of its assessment should be treated with a high degree of caution 70; that until


  68
     The method used took the 99th percentile TDV/FE flow and compared this to the FFT permit limit.
  Thames Water noted that this data describes the flow that has been treated as opposed to the flow
  passed to treatment and is not representative of the actual conditions at the point of storm separation.
  It also noted that in most cases, TDV/FE monitors do not assess FFT accurately as there is a gap in time
  between the point at which flow enters the treatment process and the point at which it passes the
  TDV/FE monitor. The diversion or recirculation of flows within the treatment process can also impact
  accurate FFT monitoring. As such, Thames Water noted, an assessment of FFT compliance that is
  based on FE flow monitor data may be inaccurate. It nevertheless used this data in its 2021 and 2022
  Assessments as it considered this was its only reliable source of flow data. It also noted that since
  Thames Water (along with the rest of the industry) has historically focussed on measuring FE volumes,
  it was unable to confidently assess compliance with the FFT requirements of its environmental permits
  at sites that lacked U_MON3 and U_MON4 monitors. It stated that new flow data will become available
  throughout AMP7 from U_MON3 and U_MON4 monitors, and this will allow for more reliable and
  accurate assessments of FFT compliance.
  69
     UKWIR, Wastewater Treatment Works Flow to Full Treatment-Monitoring and Compliance Assessment
  Method Development, 2019. Using this approach, if a site’s average flow during a spill as a percentage
  of FFT was below 92% (this accounts for the margin of error in the monitor of +/-8%) Thames Water
  flagged it as a potentially non-compliant site. A technical judgement was then made by Thames Water
  on certain sites that were above or below this threshold as to whether they needed to be included on
  the list of potentially non-compliant sites. The following features of the data were considered as part of
  this process:
  • Level of missing data;
  • Obvious erroneous data due to a faulty monitor;
  • Factors which could have skewed the data (low spill counts, one-off events); and
  • Average performance over the length of a spill. For example, taking account of poor performance at
       the beginning of a spill but reaching FFT at the end. This was then assessed against the location of
       the flow monitor to establish if this could be a factor.
  70
     Thames Water told us that 15-minute total daily flow data from MCERTS FE monitors was used to
  assess FFT but that the data describes the flow that has been treated as opposed to the flow passed to
  treatment and is not representative of the actual conditions at the point of storm separation. "It can
  therefore only be regarded as a proxy for FFT. This is because of the length of time between the sewage
  entering the treatment process and exiting, or otherwise being changed by on-site processes (for
  example inter-stage pumping), and the impact this has on establishing the flow at the storm
  separation point rather than the FE point". As such, Thames Water told us that it only able to provide an
  estimate of potential FFT compliance based on best available data. It also noted that the EDM data
  used in the assessment is from the spill-to-environment monitors and therefore does not capture the
  length of time spilling into the storm tank, only out of it.


                                                     52
         Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                             on Thames Water

       U_MON3 and U_MON4 monitors are installed, commissioned and fully operational it will
       not have a complete picture of its compliance position across all of its sites; and, as a
       result, there is still a significant degree of uncertainty over the true scale of its potential
       non-compliance.

3.17   Thames Water's uncertainty over its actual compliance position was also reflected in the
       fact that, over the course of our investigation, the number of sites which it assessed as
       being potentially non-compliant with their FFT permit conditions changed materially. In
       its December 2021 response, for example, it told us it had identified 104 sites of concern
       based on its assessment of data from 2020. In its April 2022 response to our section 203
       notice, this number increased to 137 based on its assessment of data from 2021 and in a
       further response in September 2022 it explained that it was looking again at 20 sites
       which had been identified as potentially non-compliant under its assessment of 2020
       data but not 2021 data. Thames Water told us that in the interests of transparency, and
       to gain a fuller picture of the true scale of its potential non-compliance, it would
       therefore need to investigate a total of 157 sites of concern. This is equivalent to 66.5%,
       or almost two thirds, of all its WWTW with FFT permits or TDCs.

3.18   In undertaking its assessment, Thames Water explained that it had adopted a "cautious
       approach" of including in its numbers of potentially non-compliant sites those it could
       clearly identify to be non-compliant and those where compliance was not fully clear.
       Therefore, in addition to sites which were clearly showing potentially non-compliant FFT
       performance (88 or 37.3% of its WWTW with FFT permits or TDCs), or sites which were
       capable of meeting FFT requirements but were not doing so consistently (41 or 17.4%), it
       also included sites where the data was inconclusive or indicated marginal or one-off
       non-compliance (that is, sites that were not indicating performance that was FFT
       compliant). The breakdown of the 157 sites of concern by these different categories is
       shown in Table 3.1 below.
