3.19    While acknowledging the approach adopted by Thames Water in its assessment of
        whether its WWTW have been meeting FFT permit levels, it is nevertheless evident that
        even on a less "cautious" approach, potential FFT non-compliance is extensive across its
        WWTW estate. It is also evident that, based on its own categorisation, a significant
        number of its sites (88) do not have the capacity to meet FFT permit conditions, while a
        further 69 sites, while they may have the capacity to do so, are not being operated or
        maintained in a manner to ensure that they are meeting FFT permit conditions
        consistently.



  The duration of potential FFT non-compliance

3.20    The information that Thames Water provided on its sites of concern gives a snapshot of
        its compliance position based on its assessment of data for 2020 and 2021. However, it is
        also evident from its responses to our information requests that the company had been
        aware of FFT compliance risks at many of its WWTW for some time. It had also been



                                                         54
         Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                             on Thames Water

       subject to previous enforcement action by the Environment Agency for failings at a
       number of its WWTW.

3.21   According to documents provided to us during the course of our investigation, the
       Environment Agency 71 wrote to Thames Water in 2017 setting out its concerns about
       aspects of its WWTW compliance. This followed Thames Water's prosecution for a series
       of serious pollution incidents over the period from 2012 to 2014 arising from prolonged
       discharges of untreated sewage from its WWTW at Aylesbury, Didcot, Henley and Little
       Marlow, and a large sewage pumping station at Littlemore, Oxford. As a result of these
       incidents and the Environment Agency's prosecution, Thames Water received a record
       fine of £20.3m in 2017. 72

3.22   Thames Water's failure to manage its FFT compliance was a key aspect of these
       Environment Agency prosecutions. It was demonstrated, for example, that at a number
       of its WWTW FFT was routinely reduced and a portion of the flow was sent to storm tanks
       in order to ensure that the quality of the final effluent being discharged from the site
       met permit limits - a practice known as ‘flow clipping' 73. In correspondence with Thames
       Water in July 2017 the Environment Agency also noted that there had been several
       "recent examples", which had been picked up during routine site inspections, where
       Thames Water's WWTW might not be treating the FFT required by its permits on an
       ongoing basis. In addition, a further Environment Agency enforcement case relating to
       serious pollution incidents at Thames Water's Princess Risborough WWTW 74 around the
       same period 75 had also highlighted concerns that its sites might not be set-up to treat
       permitted FFT.

3.23   In its 2017 correspondence, the Environment Agency sought assurances from Thames
       Water that it had appropriate systems and actions in place (or planned) to prevent
       practices such as flow clipping occurring again and to ensure that FFT compliance 76
       would be achieved at each relevant WWTW within a reasonable timescale 77. While the
       Environment Agency acknowledged that this would depend on the scale of works
       required, it nevertheless stressed the need for Thames Water to return its sites to FFT
       compliance without undue delay.

3.24   In its response in November 2017 78, Thames Water acknowledged the Environment
       Agency's concerns and assured it that the company had several initiatives and activities
       underway to improve its understanding of the scale of FFT compliance risk and to deliver
       the necessary changes.

3.25   Thames Water explained that as part of an initiative known as Go to Green (G2G)
       programme, it was undertaking a wholesale review of the capability of the company's
       WWTW, on a site-by-site basis, to highlight any constraints to WWTW performance and
       permit compliance.

3.26   In terms of the timeframe for addressing the identified compliance risks, we understand
       that Thames Water told the Environment Agency that, following an initial assessment, it
       had identified a number of sites which required further investigation to understand the
       root causes of those that were potentially non-compliant, and the steps needed to bring
       them back into compliance. It noted that for some WWTW a relatively simple solution
       might be sufficient to ensure full permit compliance whilst at others capital works might
       be required. As a result, until design and costings had been completed, it informed the
       Environment Agency that it was not possible to indicate how long it would take to deliver
       the full programme but that, given the number of sites on its initial list, it might not be
       possible to complete all of the required works within "the current AMP" (that is,within
       the AMP6 period of 2015-20) 79.

       calculated the 99th percentile of the 15-minute FE (TDV) flow data 88 and compared this
       to the FFT value in the relevant environmental permit to give an indication as to any
       potential compliance concern, using data for the three-year period from 2015 to 2017.
       The results of its assessment for the 250 sites with flow permits showed that at 106 of
       these sites (that is, more than 40%) the actual level of FFT treated was lower than that
       required by the permit. Of these 106 sites, the assessment showed that at 70 sites the
       actual level of FFT treated was at least 10% less than the permit level and at more than
       40 sites, the actual level of FFT treated was at least 20% less than permit levels.89
       Thames Water stressed again, however, that the limited nature of the data set available
       to it at that time did not provide a definitive view of a site's performance, but only an
       indication as to whether there was an FFT compliance concern.

3.35   When challenged as to its plans for the sites which had been identified as having
       compliance risks or which were known to be at risk of becoming non-compliant, but
       which were not part of the priority list (the 'non-priority sites') 96, Thames Water told us
       that it had always intended G2G to be a multi-AMP programme, aimed at investigating
       sites and delivering solutions for the highest risk/prioritised sites as fast as possible
       "within deliverability constraints". It also intended that the G2G risk assessment would
       be repeated periodically as more data became available - with the result that the
       prioritisation of sites might change over time.

3.36   When questioned about its decision making on the speed with which known compliance
       risks would be addressed 97 and the level of funding made available to do this, Thames
       Water told us that it had sought to balance a number of risks and competing priorities
       across its business, including tackling leakage, addressing pollution incidents,
       improving customer service and reducing the risk of asset failure. As a result, and in line
       with its systems and procedures, it noted that its decisions on investment priorities were
       driven by an assessment of available funding and outcomes based on the data available.
       It stressed again that some WWTW, which had been identified as a compliance risk
       under G2G but not prioritised for funding under AMP7, might have received funding via
       its directly managed capital (DMC) programme 98 (intended to fund planned or reactive
       work at its WWTW) to enable the delivery of 'quick-wins' locally 99.



  96

  of the WINEP were also prioritised. According to Thames Water, this provided an opportunity for it to
  address FFT compliance in parallel with the other needs on the site.

3.37    Thames Water did repeat the G2G risk assessment in 2020 using data from its EDM
        monitors and refreshed TDV flow data, as well as updated insight from its operational
        teams. The 2020 G2G assessment also continued to consider other risk factors in
        addition to FFT flow compliance, such as asset health and the sensitivity of the receiving
        water course 100. The results of its 2020 assessment showed that three years after its
        initial G2G assessment (which was based on data from 2015 to 2017), a significant
        number of Thames Water's sites were still rated as red or amber in terms of their
        compliance risks.

  The causes of potential FFT non-compliance

3.43      In addition to information on the number of FFT potentially non-compliant sites, Thames
          Water was also asked to identify the root cause of non-compliance for each site –
          recognising that for some sites, multiple root causes might exist.

3.44      As noted previously, the company's understanding of its compliance position evolved
          during the course of our investigation as it found more sites of concern. Its ability to
          identify the root cause of potential non-compliance at its sites also evolved.

3.45      In its December 2021 section 203 response, for example, of the 104 sites of concern it
          reported at that point, it told us that 15 sites had already been resolved and that of the
          remaining 89 sites, root causes had been identified for 51 sites following a programme of

3.47    Following a further information request, in January 2023 Thames Water told us it had
        concluded its root cause analysis for all 157 sites of concern. The results of this analysis
        are shown in Table 3.3 below using a number of categories which Thames Water
        described as follows:

          •   Balancing tank capacity: The capacity of existing assets to meet the final
              effluent discharge permit when treating FFT. Thames Water noted that in some
              cases, historic improvements to its wastewater pumping network has resulted in
              higher peak instantaneous flows to the WWTW leading to a reduction in capacity to
              balance incoming flow;

          •   Monitor maintenance: Incorrect operation, maintenance and calibration issues
              associated with EDM and flow monitors leading to inaccurate data reads;

          •   Monitor installation: Faulty installation and monitors in sub-optimal locations;

          •   Lack of treatment resilience: Plant configuration with single points of failure,
              poor process capacity including lack of flow balancing, and redundancy. Thames
              Water noted that for sites where the diurnal flow pattern is very pronounced and/or
              some or all of the flow to the works is pumped, it is unable to reduce the variation
              in flow due to lack of flow balancing;

          •   Incorrect site and storm tank return locations: Site returns include liquors
              containing, for example, sludge and backwash from the treatment process being
              returned to the process upstream of flow to treatment measurement, wrongfully
              accounted for in the FFT measurement110;

          •   Poor flow control. Poor flow control which occurs when the physical mechanism
              or control system to ensure the site pass forward flow is accurate (neither passing
              too much or too little flow to treatment) is not working correctly. This could be due

                the flow from one process unit to another not being of the correct size and causing
                the flow to back up and/or overflow; and

          •     Blockages. Blockages or restrictions observed on sites, where for example there is
                an accumulation of rags and/or grit in a pipe or channel, restricting or blocking the
                flow.

  Other indicators of potential permit non-compliance

3.51    As explained above, although our investigation initially focused on Thames Water's
        compliance with its FFT permit conditions and what this indicated for the obligations
        Ofwat is responsible for enforcing, the information provided by Thames Water revealed
        further issues in the way that the company constructs, operates, maintains and
        monitors its WWTW to ensure its compliance with the UWWTR and section 94 WIA91.

3.52    The risk assessment Thames Water undertook in 2017 under its G2G programme had also
        highlighted potential compliance concerns in relation to other aspects of the operation
        of its WWTW (which we consider relevant indicators of the UWWTR compliance). This
        included the ability of its WWTW to treat sewage within permit limits, which, at that
        time, Thames Water acknowledged was "becoming increasingly challenging due to
        population growth, headroom erosion and tightening consents". It also acknowledged
        that "some sites were operating beyond their design capacity, as a result of historic
        growth or asset deterioration, and that a few sites might not be meeting permitted DWF
        levels". 112

3.53    In the following sections, we consider evidence we have obtained about other aspects of
        the company's management and operation of its WWTW.

  Dry Weather Flow

3.54    Data obtained during the course of our investigation confirmed the deterioration in the
        company's DWF compliance position with an increasing number of sites failing to meet
        DWF permit conditions in at least one year and an increasing number of sites seeing an
        erosion in headroom and therefore at greater risk of failing.

3.55    As set out in Section 2, WWTW with DWF environmental permit conditions are expected
        to be able to treat the Q80 113 flow and companies are expected to monitor whether a site
        is achieving this metric and to take action to investigate and remedy if it is not. While
        changes in weather from year to year means that it may not be possible for a WWTW to
        achieve the Q80 metric in each and every year, a prudent company should investigate
        any WWTW that fail to achieve Q80 to ensure that it understands why this is the case and



  112
      From Thames Water's document entitled 'Go to Green Flow Compliance Update, Environment
  Agency, 19 March 2018'
  113
      Q80 is calculated by taking the total flow for each day of the year and placing these flow numbers in
  ascending order. The Q80 is the flow on the day that is lower than 80% of the others, that is, the 20th
  percentile (or 73rd day). Q80 is intended to reflect the average dry day in the UK – if it never rained it
  would be Q50 but as it rains quite often, Q80 is considered to be the average dry day.

                                                      68
          Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                              on Thames Water

        to consider whether the failure may indicate the existence of longer-term problems that
        the company needs to address.

3.56    If a site fails to treat the Q90 114 on a persistent or recurring basis (assessed on a three
        out of five years basis) then it is more likely that the site is unable to meet local demand
        in normal climatic conditions, that is, it does not have adequate capacity to deal with
        the volume of wastewater that it would normally, or routinely, be expected to deal with.

3.57    In response to our section 203 notices, Thames Water provided data on the number of its
        sites with DWF permit requirements and the number of those sites that had failed to
        meet Q80 flow and Q90 flow over the five-year period from 2017 to 2021.

3.58    Thames Water told us that 322 of its 354 WWTW have a DWF permit requirement and it
        provided data for 282 of these. This data showed that 78 of the sites for which Thames
        Water had provided data (28%) had failed to meet Q80 DWF permit conditions at least
        once in the 5-year period. In terms of compliance with Q90 flow levels, 46 of these sites
        (16%) had failed to meet Q90 DWF permit conditions at least once in those five years; 23
        sites (8%) had failed to meet Q90 twice in those five years; and 10 sites (4%) had failed
        to meet Q90 at least three times in that five-year period. 115

3.59    This evidence on the number of sites failing to meet Q80 and Q90 DWF permit limits at
        least once in the five-year period to 2021 is also consistent with concerns being flagged
        in discussions within the company itself. In a February 2022 report relating to DWF
        performance, for example, Thames Water noted that "currently we have 46 failing sites in
        total. If any of these sites fail for a second year then we could have at least 21 sites at
        'failed' status by end of 2025 (unless issues fully resolved)." 116 This position was also
        discussed at a Thames Water's internal Executive Performance Review meeting in
        February 2022, where it was noted that “as investment has not kept up with growth” the
        company could have more than 20 failing sites by 2025117. 118

3.60   In addition to an increasing number of failed sites, the data provided by Thames Water
       also indicated increased pressure on its WWTW and a continued erosion of headroom at
       a growing number of sites. Between 2017 and 2021, 83% of sites reported by Thames
       Water experienced an increase in DWF 119 and, over the same period, the average ratio
       between actual DWF and the DWF permit limit at Thames Water's sites increased from
       69% to 84%.

3.62   As explained above, sites that are failing to meet Q80 permit levels are at greater risk of
       breaching UWWTR obligations relating to discharges as they are operating close to
       tolerance levels, and with little or no headroom. If a site is failing to meet Q80 permit
       levels, therefore, this should act as a trigger to prompt the company to check the
       operation of its assets at those sites and consider whether it has sufficient capacity to
       deal with local demand and also consider whether it needs to renew its assets and the
       associated DWF and FFT conditions within its environmental permit to reflect higher
       levels of demand on the site.




  growth but instead by increases to DWF. However, Thames Water did not provide an explanation of the
  cause of this DWF increase.
  119
      This, according to Thames Water, was due, in part, to the impact of Covid and the fact that people
  were working from home which resulted in increases in flows at some sites beyond normal levels.

                                                                     70
         Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                             on Thames Water

3.63   It is evident that sites that are operating with little or no headroom are at greater risk of
       discharging, with a consequent increased risk of causing environmental harm. Our
       analysis of the DWF performance data provided by Thames Water and the information it
       provided on spills from individual WWTW indicates that sites operating above DWF permit
       levels spill more frequently. In the case of Thames Water, its WWTW operating above
       their DWF permit levels have, on average, 13% more spills than those operating within
       their DWF permit levels.

  Storm tank capacity

3.64   In addition to the evidence about Thames Water's deteriorating position in relation to
       DWF, data provided to us by the company also indicates a deteriorating position in
       relation to the capacity of other assets to deal with the increasing volumes of
       wastewater that they are required to do.

3.65   As indicated in Section 2, in our investigation we have benchmarked the storm tank
       provision to a volume equivalent of six hours of DWF for the WWTW. Storing less than this
       would mean spills to the environment will likely be higher than would otherwise be the
       case as a company may not have sufficient storage capacity to prevent short-term high
       flow levels from leading to discharges to the environment. This in turn could indicate
       that a company is failing to comply with the requirements of the UWWTR as it would not
       be minimising adverse impacts on the environment to at least the degree originally
       expected by its environmental permit.

3.66   In Thames Water's case, in addition to concerns about FFT compliance at 157 of its sites,
       and potential DWF issues at a growing number of sites, in its January 2023 response on
       its root cause analysis, it also identified potential issues with storm tank capacity at
       more than a third of its sites of concern (that is, 57 sites or 36% of the sites of concern).

3.67   Separately, Thames Water also provided data on storage capacity at a number of its
       WWTW (but was not able to do so for all sites). For the 193 sites for which information
       was provided, 62% had storage capacity of six hours or less of permit DWF limit and so,
       are at greater risk of breaching permit conditions and spilling to the environment.



  Potential non-compliance with wider environmental and
  regulatory obligations

3.68   As explained in Section 2, a company must ensure that discharges of untreated
       wastewater to the environment, either directly or via storm tanks, only occur in
       exceptional circumstances unless it can demonstrate that there was no environmental
       harm or that there is no BTKNEEC compatible remedial action it can take. Where a spill
       does occur, a company is expected to mitigate any harm to the environment resulting
       from it, again subject to BTKNEEC. We also explained that non-compliance with these

                                                   71
          Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                              on Thames Water

       requirements might amount to a breach of Regulations 4(2) or 4(4) UWWTR (depending
       on whether the source of the discharge was the network or a WWTW), and consequently
       of section 94 WIA91. How a company considers and responds to its spills performance is
       also relevant to demonstrating the adequacy of its understanding, planning, resources
       and systems for managing its compliance, and therefore its broader duties under
       section 94 WIA91 and Condition P.

  Spills from storm overflows at Thames Water's WWTW and on its network

3.69   Water companies report spills data from the EDMs on their storm overflows to the
       Environment Agency and this is now published annually. Table 3.5 below shows spills
       data for 2020, 2021, 2022 and 2023 reported for storm overflows associated with Thames
       Water's WWTW as well as at its sewage pumping stations and on its network. The data
       does not distinguish between those spills which occurred within or outside of
       compliance with the conditions of Environment Agency permit and/or the UWWTR.

3.70   In its data returns for 2020 and 2021, Thames Water reported that close to 100% of its
       storm overflows had commissioned EDMs in place and that the vast majority were
       providing spills data. In its 2022 return, however, it listed an additional 312 storm
       overflows which had not previously been reported and which did not have EDMs in place.
       The vast majority (307) of these storm overflows were identified as being on Thames
       Water's network and it is understood that a significant number (196 120 or 36.5% of the
       537 network storm overflows that Thames Water listed) also did not have Environment
       Agency permits in place at the time of the reporting to the Environment Agency 121. As a
       result, the proportion of Thames Water's storm overflows with EDMs commissioned and
       reporting data, fell to just 61.8% in 2022 from 100% in 2021.

3.71   The identification of these un-monitored storm overflows (and notwithstanding that
       Thames Water now considers that some of these overflows were not "genuine" storm
       overflows) means that the actual level of spills reported by Thames Water from 2020 to
       2023 is unlikely to provide a full and complete picture of what was happening across its
       network. The actual number and duration of spills could, therefore, have been much
       higher 122.

3.85   Thames Water told us that these estimates were derived by using data from MCERTS FE

       event to determine what proportion of each event was potentially non-compliant but
       stressed that this was likely to be an over-estimation. It noted that, in practice, not all
       spills from a site would have necessarily been non-compliant with its permit and some
       spills might have occurred even if the site was meeting its FFT permit conditions. As a
       result, Thames Water explained that it was unable to definitively say that the spills
       reported had occurred as a result of potential permit non-compliance.

  147
      In assessing compliance, Thames Water told us that the approach used involves determining the
  approximate level at which effluent flow rate ‘plateaus’ during spill events. For an individual discharge
  event to be compliant, the plateau should exceed 92% of the FFT consent, once allowances have been
  made for the lag or delay in flows reaching the final effluent flow measurement location. The duration
  of each individual spill event at a site was analysed to determine potential compliance. For sites with
  an MCERTS flow monitor at the front end of the works, the permit condition of 92% of FFT 95% of the
  time was used to determine “compliance” for the purposes of finding duration of compliant vs non-
  compliant spills. For sites with a FE MCERTS flow monitor, the UKWIR guidance was followed, and
  analysis allowed for a 3-hour consideration of any attenuation through the works before determining
  compliance.

  The reasons for high spills from Thames Water's storm overflows

3.89    As explained in Section 2, there are circumstances in which a site which has recorded a
        high number of spills may still be considered to have complied with UWWTR
        requirements relating to discharges because, for example, the spill took place in
        exceptional circumstances, the spill did not result in environmental harm or the
        company is able to demonstrate that the high spilling site is subject to a BTKNEEC
        exception.

3.90    In the following section, therefore, we consider evidence on the extent to which these
        exceptions may have applied and the extent to which Thames Water has taken all the
        steps it is required to take to remediate high spilling sites in accordance with BTKNEEC.

3.91    Storm overflows which have spill levels above the SOAF thresholds are expected to be
        investigated by the companies. As explained in Section 2, Stage 1 of the SOAF requires a
        company to identify the reasons for the spills (and forms part of a company's annual
        return to the Environment Agency in addition to data on the number and duration of
        spill). These reasons fall into the following categories:

          •   Exceptional rainfall: This does not refer to individual rainfall events, but rather the
              rainfall across the reporting year.

          •   Asset Maintenance: Where the storm overflow and potentially parts of the
              upstream and downstream sewer network have not operated as
              designed/expected, high spill counts (over 60 times per year) can be caused". 148

          •   Hydraulic capacity: If the reason for a high spilling storm overflow (over 60 times
              per year) is neither “exceptional rainfall” nor “asset maintenance” then the reason
              is classified under the “Hydraulic capacity” category. This indicates there is
              insufficient capacity (conveyance or storage) in the sewer network to cope with
              the wastewater flow plus typical rainfall entering the sewer network.

3.92    As set out in Table 3.5, Thames Water has not identified exceptional rainfall as the
        reason for spills above the SOAF threshold at any of its high spilling sites over the period
        from 2021 to 2023. Instead, asset maintenance was identified as the reason for high
        spills at a significant number of its sites - over 60% of high spilling sites in 2021, 38.7%
        in 2022 and 40.4% in 2023.



  148
     The Environment Agency lists a number of different asset maintenance categories or "Performance"
  reasons that companies can identify. These include Partial / no capacity due to blockage or restriction;
  Sewer collapse (partial / full); Groundwater inundation; Infiltration; Asset power failure; Pump failure /
  issue; Other maintenance / capital works; Asset configuration (e.g. Pumping Station/rising main/storm
  tanks); Data collection - EDM non-representative location.

                                                     81
         Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                             on Thames Water

  High spilling sites due to asset maintenance

3.93   Under the SOAF framework, if the cause of spills which was triggered under SOAF (for
       example, the site has recorded more than 60 spills in one year) is identified as being due
       to asset maintenance, the storm overflow is removed from the SOAF process at this
       Stage (Stage 1). The removal of these sites from the SOAF process reflects the fact that
       companies are expected to be able to resolve these issues within the calendar year as
       part of their ongoing operational and maintenance activities (activities that companies
       are already funded for under their price control base allowances).

3.94   The Environment Agency's published spills data would seem to indicate, however, that
       not only was asset maintenance the primary reason for high spills at the majority of
       Thames Water's storm overflows, but that issues at these sites were not resolved swiftly
       and that many of these sites continued to record a significant number of spills in
       subsequent years. For example, of the 84 storm overflows which were reported to the
       Environment Agency as having spilled over 60 times in 2021, at 52 of these sites 149
       Thames Water identified the reason for the spill as being due to asset maintenance
       issues, and of these 52 sites, 41 also recorded 20 or more spills in 2022, 31 recorded over
       40 spills and 15 recorded over 60 spills in 2022.

3.95   In its response to our draft decision, Thames Water told us that whilst asset
       maintenance may have been identified originally as the reason a site had spilled more
       than 60 times in 2021, this categorisaton may have changed subsequently if, as a result
       of further investigation, the actual cause of the spills was found to be due to hydraulic
       capacity issues. Thames Water told us that this was the case for three storm overflows
       associated with its WWTW and one storm overflow on its network150 (that is, so the
       number of high spilling sites spilling due to asset maintenance issues revised down from
       52 to 48).

3.96   Thames Water also told us that as part of our assessment we had "likely overstated" the
       number of its storm overflows which had failed to comply with the UWWTR. This is
       because, under SOAF, while sites which are high spilling due to asset maintenance
       issues are expected to be remediated within the calendar year, where infiltration is
       identified as the reason for the spill (which is also categorised as an asset maintenance
       issue), the SOAF states that "it is recognised that investigation and resolution of
       infiltration issues can be difficult, that solutions may be iterative, and that infiltration
       reduction plans may only succeed over the medium to long-term". 151 If infiltration has
       been identified as the reason for a site spilling more than 60 times in one year, the


  149 Of the 52 storm overflow sites that were high spilling in 2021 due to operational and maintenance

         company is expected to develop an infiltration reduction plan (IRP) to address the
         problem.

 3.97    As part of our investigation, therefore, we sought further information from Thames
         Water on the number of its storm overflows where infiltration had been identified as the
         reason for the site spilling more than 60 times in 2021. We also requested details of
         whether an IRP was in place for the particular storm overflow; the date the plan had
         originally been developed and when it was last reviewed; whether the plan had been
         agreed with the Environment Agency; and information on the company's progress in
         delivering that plan and the actions it has taken to resolve infiltration issues at its sites.

 3.98    Thames Water told us that of the 48 storm overflows which had spilled more than 60
         times in 2021 due to asset maintenance issues, 19 were identified as being due to
         infiltration 152. It also told us that these sites either have an IRP in place, which has been
         agreed with the Environment Agency, or it has a Groundwater Impacted System
         Management Plan (GISMP) which has been developed in collaboration with, and
         reviewed by, the Environment Agency. All of these plans are reviewed annually and
         updated reports are published on Thames Water's website 153.

   High spilling sites due to hydraulic capacity reasons

 3.99    Under the SOAF framework, if the cause of the high spills at a storm overflow is
         identified as being due to hydraulic capacity reasons at Stage 1 of the process, the storm
         overflow continues to Stage 2. This requires the company to investigate and quantify the
         environmental impacts resulting for the high spilling storm overflow (including aesthetic
         impact and the effect on macroinvertebrates, as well as, in some instances, the effect
         on water quality), and then, if appropriate, to identify options to address the cause of the
         high spills and to assess the costs and benefits of those options (Stage 3 of the SOAF
         process). Where a cost beneficial option is found to exist, under the SOAF framework,
         companies are then required to develop plans to prioritise and deliver the necessary
         improvements.

3.100    As part of the company's annual return to the Environment Agency, it is also asked to
         provide information on any action taken or planned for those sites which are high
         spilling sites due to hydraulic capacity issues and the status and timeframe for those
         plans. This information typically indicates which stage of the SOAF investigation process

        shows that at 52 of these sites (84%) the spills had had an environmental impact and at

   Pollution incidents

3.106    As set out in Section 2, in our view, we would expect that a reasonable company would
         have systems in place to enable it to have sufficient oversight of what is happening at its
         WWTW and on its network and the extent to which its assets are operating as they
         should do to minimise any adverse impact on the environment.

3.107    Thames Water has claimed that it was a lack of accurate data on what was happening
         across its network that impacted negatively on its ability to understand its compliance
         position. However, in our view, the data that was already available to Thames Water
         should have highlighted concerns about how its assets were operating and should have
         triggered more rigorous and urgent investigation. This is particularly so given the known
         compliance risks that the company was already aware of as a result of its G2G risk
         assessment and also, given the fact that the company had been the subject of previous
         enforcement action as a result of a series of serious pollution incidents arising from
         failings at its WWTW and on its network 158.

3.108    Despite these past prosecutions, Thames Water's performance in terms of pollution
         incidents has remained a cause of significant concern. In 2021, for example, it was
         downgraded from a 3* to a 2* rated company (that is, one that requires improvement)
         by the Environment Agency in its annual Environmental Performance Assessment
         (EPA) 159. Figures for 2022 and 2023 160 show that its environmental performance
         continued to be disappointing and it remained a 2* rated company.

3.109    The downgrade in the company's EPA rating has been due in large part to the number of
         serious (Category 1 & 2) 161 pollution incidents that the company has been reporting. In
         2021, for example, it recorded 12 serious pollution incidents. Of these, six serious
         pollution incidents were associated with its WWTW and of these, three were at WWTW




   158
       Thames Water has been prosecuted for a number of serious pollution incidents in recent years. In
   2017 it received a record fine of £20.3 million relating to the discharge of untreated sewage into the
   Thames and has also received fines in subsequent years for incidents dating from around the same
   period – the latest being a £3.3 million fine in July 2023. As a result of this latest prosecution,
   Thames Water has been fined a total of £35.7 million since 2017 as a result of its pollution incidents.

3.112      The table also shows that just six of these top 26 polluting sites were included in Thames

           pollutions. It also stressed that all 26 sites listed are subject to further investigation and
           investment as part of the company's Pollution Incident Reduction Plan ("PIRP") 164.
           However, given the latest numbers on pollution incidents being reported by Thames
           Water it would appear that the actions that Thames Water has taken to address and
           reduce incidents have not been effective as yet.

3.116   This again underlines the fact that many of the sites which had been identified as
        having compliance risks under Thames Water's G2G programme – but which were not
        prioritised for investment – are sites which are among Thames Water's sites of concern
        and also among its highest polluting sites.

3.117   While we recognise that there may be multiple reasons why individual pollution
        incidents occur, some of which may not have a direct causal link to a site's failure to
        meet FFT or DWF permit conditions, the fact that many sites have repeatedly been the
        source of these incidents – and over such an extended period of time - suggests that
        Thames Water has failed to address underlying issues with sufficient urgency.


   Stakeholder concerns

3.118   Against this backdrop of ongoing issues in relation to its environmental performance and
        increased pollution incidents, Thames Water has also been the subject of significant and
        sustained criticism from several environmental non-governmental organisations
        (eNGOs) and community groups relating to the frequency and duration of spills of
        untreated sewage to local waterways and the company's alleged failure to invest
        adequately to cater for the growth in population in its region.

3.119   Citizen scientists and volunteers working with Windrush Against Sewage Pollution
        (WASP), for example, have played a key role in raising awareness of longstanding
        compliance issues and advocating for change. This has included collecting and
        analysing data on flow and sewage spills at Thames Water's sites over a number of years.
        WASP claims that its analysis of data from 2017 - which was gathered from Thames
        Water and the Environment Agency using Environmental Information Regulation (EIR)


                                                    91
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

         requests - demonstrates that the company has routinely been discharging untreated
         sewage from its WWTW outside of its permit conditions. This includes spills during dry
         weather (which WASP categorise as "dry spills") or when it has failed to meet its FFT
         conditions (which WASP categorise as "early spills") 166.

3.120    We are aware that WASP has been in contact with Thames Water to raise its concerns
         about these issues for some time and has submitted a number of EIR requests to obtain
         information on the performance of the company's WWTW. WASP has argued that the
         responses to these requests have revealed gaps or inconsistencies in the information
         that Thames Water has been able to provide and have demonstrated the company's poor
         record keeping and its inability to oversee the installation of EDMs to accurately record
         spills of sewage from its sites. As outlined previously, Thames Water has acknowledged
         that there were problems with the installation and maintenance of its EDM monitors,
         which meant that the company could not rely on the accuracy of the data being
         collected from those monitors.

3.121    WASP's analysis has also been shared with Thames Water and has been used as part of a
         campaign to raise public and political awareness of these issues. It is understood that
         there has been some discussion and debate about the accuracy of WASP's analysis and
         its interpretation of what the flow and spills data is showing about Thames Water's
         potential breaches of its permit conditions. Nevertheless, it is clear that local groups and
         eNGOs have been raising serious concerns with the company about discharges of
         untreated sewage from its WWTW for several years and about the impacts that these
         discharges are having on local waterways. It is also clear that at least some of these
         concerns are supported by the evidence set out above about the scale, duration and
         scope of potential non-compliance with environmental permits that Thames Water has
         now identified across its WWTW estate. Indeed, most of the 24 sites that WASP had
         included in its list of sites with unpermitted spills (and which its analysis had indicated
         had recorded "dry" or "early" spills), are also sites which Thames Water's assessment has
         now shown to be sites of concern.

3.122    A number of other organisations have also publicly expressed their concerns about the
         performance of Thames Water's WWTW, about the scale and duration of discharges of
         untreated sewage from its WWTW and about the consequences of these discharges for
         the local environment and communities. In January 2022, for example, the Oxford River
         Improvement Campaign (ORIC) published a report entitled "The rivers around Oxford


   166
      WASP Review of Unpermitted Spills from Sewage Treatment Works, Part 1. October 2021. This
   research highlighted concerns about spills at the following WWTW: Stanton Harcourt, Oxford, South
   Leigh, Mogden, Fairford, Chipping Norton, Carterton, Hogsmill, Princes Risborough, Ampney St Peter,
   Cassington, Aylesbury, Lechlade and Pangbourne. In Part 2 of its review in January 2022, WASP
   highlighted concerns about unpermitted spills at a number of other Thames Water WWTW, namely;
   Bentley, Bledington, Chesham, Chinnor, Church Hanborough, Dorking, East Shefford, Maple Lodge,
   Marlborough and Witney.


                                                     92
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

         2020" in which it drew on information from the Environment Agency, Thames Water and
         the National Rivers Trust to collate and analyse data about 148 WWTW around Oxford.

3.123    In that report, ORIC concluded that untreated sewage is being discharged from Thames
         Water's WWTW "at an alarming rate" and outside of storm conditions, including during
         periods of low or no rainfall events. In ORIC's view, the underlying cause of the
         discharges is not storms or heavy rainfall, but a shortage of capacity at Thames Water's
         WWTW, caused by increasing population and Thames Water's failure to invest in
         significant new capacity over the last 15 years.

3.124    It is understood that Thames Water challenged aspects of the approach adopted by ORIC
         and the conclusions that ORIC reached in its report about the nature and scale of
         discharges of untreated sewage from its WWTW. In its response, for example, Thames
         Water acknowledged that shortage of treatment capacity and a growing population can
         be important factors in causing discharges from its WWTW, but noted that, nevertheless,
         the underlying root cause is ground / surface water and rain entering the sewers 167.
         According to Thames Water, this in turn meant that there were a range of different
         interventions that could be made to reduce the amount of water entering the system
         apart from investment in additional capacity which may not always be the most cost
         effective or efficient solution. Thames Water noted that in seeking to solve the problem
         of excess flows it employs a combination of measures as there is no one ‘correct’ answer
         and each location needs to be assessed separately, looking at local conditions relating to
         infrastructure, topography and geology, and assessing the most cost-effective way to
         solve the problem (though, as explained earlier, it has also acknowledged potential
         issues with headroom capacity at an increasing number of its sites).

3.125    In addition to the concerns highlighted above, other groups have also been sharing their
         experiences about the impact that discharges from Thames Water's WWTW have been
         having on local watercourses. In written evidence for the House of Commons
         Environmental Audit Committee report on water quality in rivers 168, for example, the
         Upper Thames Fisheries Consultative committee (UTFC) pointed to the deterioration in
         water quality of the Cotswold rivers and the loss of biodiversity in terms of fish species,
         riverine invertebrates and in stream macrophytes. UTFC claimed in its evidence that the
         principal cause of this ongoing degradation has been the repeated, increased and
         continuing discharge of untreated sewage into all of the rivers in question – the source
         of which was Thames Water's WWTW. This view was also echoed in its written evidence
         to the Committee by the Thame Valley Fisheries Preservation Consultative, which also




   167
       In its December 2021 response, Thames Water told us that groundwater infiltration and surface
   water ingress account for more than half of the spills from its overflow assets.

        pointed to discharges of untreated sewage from Thames Water's WWTW as the main

3.126   The evidence provided and issues raised by various eNGOs and stakeholder groups has
        been helpful in highlighting areas for our own investigation and information gathering.
        However, our findings rely upon our own analysis of monitoring data and data provided
        by Thames Water.

   Summary of evidence relating to the performance of the company's
   wastewater assets

3.127   As set out in the preceding sections, we have received and considered a range of
        evidence and information on the performance of Thames Water's WWTW and its
        wastewater network. This has revealed a number of concerns, across a range of different
        metrics and indicators that are relevant to understanding whether the company is
        complying with its legal obligations relating to discharges to the environment. In
        summary, the evidence shows that:

          •   Two thirds of all its WWTW with FFT permit conditions or TDC (157 sites) have been
              identified by Thames Water as sites of concern with respect to their permit
              compliance.

          •   88 of these sites were identified as clearly underperforming and appear to be
              incapable of meeting FFT permit conditions, while a further 69 sites, while having
              the capacity to meet FFT permit conditions, were not doing so on a consistent
              basis.

          •   Ten sites have failed to meet DWF permit conditions and Thames Water
              acknowledged that the number could increase to more than 20 by 2025 if issues
              are not addressed.

          •   A growing number of sites have already seen significant headroom erosion and are,
              therefore, at risk of failing to meet DWF conditions. This, in turn, increases the risk
              of unpermitted spills of untreated sewage from these sites.

          •   57 sites (or more than a third of its sites of concern) have been identified by
              Thames Water as having insufficient storage tank capacity, and over 60% of sites
              for which it was able to provide data had storage capacity of six hours or less of
              permit DWF limit. These sites are, therefore, at greater risk of breaching permit
              conditions and of unpermitted spills of untreated sewage.

          •   Thames Water has reported high number of spills from its storm overflows – but
              these numbers are likely to under-report the true scale of spills which have
              actually occurred across its wastewater network.



                                                    94
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       •   In 2021, almost 30% of storm overflows associated with its WWTW spilled more than
           60 times; and almost three quarters (73%) spilled 20 or more times.

       •   Thames Water does appear to have taken steps to investigate the high spilling
           sites which were triggered under the SOAF and for the 25 169 storm overflows
           associated with its WWTW which had spilled more than 60 times in 2021 due to
           hydraulic capacity issues, it appears to have identified and costed remediation
           solutions for 19 of these sites – though those solutions are not due to be
           implemented until AMP8.

       •   In addition, the SOAF investigations also showed that the majority of spills at the
           high spilling storm overflows associated with its WWTW were due to asset
           maintenance issues (over 60% of sites which had spilled more than 60 times in
           2021) and the majority of those sites continued to record 20 or more times in the
           following year – indicating that Thames Water had failed to act swiftly to resolve
           issues as it is required to do under SOAF.

       •   Thames Water has told us that infiltration was the reason for the spills at 17 of the
           sites where asset maintenance was the reason for the site spilling more than 60
           times. Under SOAF it is accepted that it may taken longer to address infiltration
           issues than other asset maintenance issues, nevertheless, companies are
           expected to develop plans to identify and address the causes of infiltration at its
           sites. While Thames Water has infiltration reduction plans in place for all 17 of the
           sites referred to above, at eight of those sites, there was no evidence that it had
           taken any particular action (apart from investigations) to reduce the risk of spills
           from those sites.

       •   In relation to spills at storm overflows on Thames Water's wastewater network, the
           reported number of spills were significantly lower than at its WWTW. However, the
           actual number of spills from these storm overflow could be significantly higher.
           This is because in its 2022 return to the Environment Agency the company listed
           307 storm overflows on its network that had not previously been included in its
           annual return and did not have EDMs in place. It also revealed that 196 of these
           overflows did not have environmental permits. While Thames Water has now
           developed a programme to investigate these overflows and, as a result, has
           reduced its estimate of the number of storm overflows that are actually
           unpermitted, it nevertheless, underlines the fact that, until recently, the company
           did not have sufficient oversight and management of its assets and was therefore
           failing to ensure that its assets were being managed to minimise the risk of any
           adverse impact on the environment.




  As noted previously, Thames Water told us that three storm overflows associated with its WWTW
169

which were originally thought to have spilled due to infiltration and reported in its 2021 EDM return to
the Environment Agency were subsequently recategorized as having spilled due to hydraulic capacity.

                                                   95
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

           •   Despite the fact that up to July 2023 Thames Water had been fined in excess of
               £35million 170arising from a series of serious pollution incidents since 2017 as a
               result of Environment Agency prosecutions, the number of serious pollution
               incidents reported by the company has remained worryingly high and is expected
               to remain high for the remainder of AMP7.

           •   Thames Water has been the focus of increasingly vocal complaint from local
               stakeholder groups and eNGOs about the number and frequency of spills from its
               WWTW and across its network.



   Thames Water's processes and procedures for identifying and
   addressing compliance risks

3.128    Thames Water has highlighted the lack of accurate data as the main reason for its
         failure to identify compliance issues on the scale that it has now done. However, as
         outlined above, there were other indicators and sources of information which could
         have, and should have, alerted the company about the performance of its wastewater
         assets and the extent to which it was operating and maintaining these assets to ensure
         it was able to meet local demand and to minimise adverse effects on the environment,
         and hence to comply with its legal obligations to do so.

3.129    In this section we consider the processes and procedures that Thames Water has told us
         it had/has in place to enable it to identify and address compliance risks.

3.130    Outside of the G2G asset-wide risk assessments that Thames Water undertook in 2017
         and repeated in 2020, it told us that it also had various ad-hoc processes and
         procedures in place to enable it to assess FFT compliance and inform decisions with
         respects to the performance of its WWTW. These processes included site visits combined
         with remote monitoring of key assets, processes, and data. Thames Water claimed that
         these operational processes enabled it to ensure that any anomalies that might arise
         were flagged, investigated and rectified in a timely manner, or, if necessary, escalated
         or used to identify investment needs or additional funding to ensure that such issues did
         not occur in future.

3.131    Thames Water explained, for example, that all WWTW with storm discharge permits
         have had remote monitoring capability which include storm tank levels and key process
         assets. Alarms have been monitored centrally in Thames Water's Waste Operational
         Control Room which has been manned 24/7. Site processes have been audited by
         process scientists with data from larger sites being collated centrally and reviewed to

   170
      See footnote 75. Following a prosecution decision in July 2023, the Environment Agency noted that
   as a result of its prosecutions for pollution incidents, at that point, Thames Water had been fined a
   total of £35.7m since 2017.


                                                     96
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

         ensure sites have been operated and maintained at the required efficiency. Site audits
         have also been conducted by process compliance teams at varying frequencies and the
         completion of operation and maintenance activities have been reported weekly and
         reviewed in performance meetings. Regular reviews have also taken place at local team
         level and key performance indicators (KPIs) have been reviewed at departmental level.
         Thames Water also noted that critical asset availability has been tracked through its
         "Waste Cockpit" system.

3.132    The company also told us that any risks and steps to address issues have been recorded
         in local action plans managed by the site performance team and process
         compliance team. Longer-term issues have been referred to the company's asset
         planning team and plans discussed and escalated, if appropriate, to its Environmental
         Governance Board (EGB). The EGB was set up in November 2019 to provide oversight,
         assurance and challenge on operational performance and environmental compliance 171
         and is chaired by the Head of Environmental Regulation and Quality and attended by
         Directors from Operations, Engineering and Asset, and Sustainability.

3.133    Throughout our investigation, and whilst acknowledging that there were areas for
         improvement, Thames Water has maintained that its processes and procedures have
         been robust and capable of identifying and managing compliance risks. Instead, it
         pointed to a lack of accurate data as the key factor in its failure to identity the full extent
         of potential FFT and other non-compliance at its sites.

3.134    As explained earlier, Thames Water told us that it had previously relied on data from its
         FE/TDV monitors to provide an indication of whether its sites were likely to be meeting
         FFT permit requirements. As spills data from its EDM monitors has become available,
         this has enabled it to get a better understanding of its compliance position and, in turn,
         has revealed more sites of concern.

3.135    It also told us, however, that while it began installing EDMs during AMP6 172 issues with
         the installation of these monitors meant that it could not rely on the accuracy of the data

   •    Installation: installations at incorrect locations that did not accurately represent storm spills to the

        EDM not capable of collecting accurate data from locations such as wet wells and storm tanks.

        Thames Water's systems as data being sent via a 2G mobile network location was impacted by poor
        network connectivity due to the location of the monitor.

        a lack of communications with Operations at the point of asset handover resulted in maintenance
        not consistently being applied as per the Thames Water Asset Standard. In addition, no data
        quality assurance processes were introduced when the monitors were first installed, meaning that
        Thames Water was not able to validate data on a regular basis which in turn lead to delays in the
        identification of suspect and missing data.

3.138    In relation to U_MON 4 monitors, Thames Water told us it had installed 154 monitors to
         September 2024 and that these monitors were operational and had obtained certificates
         of conformity (with 91 monitors having full MCERTS certification). This included monitors
         which the company had planned to install under WINEP for AMP8 but which it had
         accelerated into AMP7.

3.139    According to Thames Water these monitors are subject to rigorous data verification, with
         the U_MON 3 data in particular being checked constantly through the company's new
         Discharge Alert Manager (DAM) tool. In addition, its MCERTS certified U_MON 4
         monitors are managed under the company's MCERTS Quality Management System
         (QMS) which details the maintenance and data handling requirements for these
         monitors. In its September 2024 response to our consultation, Thames Water told us that
         the first external audit of the company's processes to ensure successful and accurate
         calibration of its U_MON monitors had been completed and no major defaults had been
         identified. This audit is scheduled to occur annually.

3.140    In addition to the accelerated roll out of monitors, Thames Water also informed us that it
         has invested in the development of digital tools such as its DAM. This, it claims, will give
         the company near real-time visibility of spills to the environment, spills to storm tanks
         and a view of FFT and environmental compliance more broadly. In its September 2024
         response, Thames Water told us that the DAM tool is now fully operational and is being
         used across the company's operational monitoring base. It also noted that it is gathering
         feedback continually in order to enhance the existing features of the tool and enable its
         continuous development.

3.141    As a result of the implementation of the DAM tool, Thames Water told us that it will be
         able to collate and centralise data received from the company's monitors, harnessing FE,
         EDM and U_MON data to gauge widescale compliance across its wastewater network,
         with any anomalies being flagged to operational teams for investigation. It also noted
         that reports are being built for operational teams to have direct insight into performance
         and to allow Thames Water to generate weekly or monthly summaries of alert volumes,
         by type, region and site. This will also enable teams to investigate the circumstances of a
         reported spill and to escalate within the company if necessary 176.

3.142    The DAM tool is also being enhanced to allow compliance forecasting. According to
         Thames Water, the tool can, for example, integrate data from the company's sewer

3.144   In relation to compliance beyond FFT permit conditions, Thames Water told us that it has
        also implemented a Compliance Assessment System (CAS) with the objective of
        providing enhanced transparency on its compliance position against all permit
        conditions, to escalate potential non-compliance, and to provide oversight of related
        improvement programmes. According to Thames Water 177 the CAS:

          •   is an interactive tool to allow “self-serve” reports on compliance position;
          •   identifies areas of greatest permit compliance risk;
          •   improves oversight of improvement schemes to address risk;
          •   implements a process to provide regular re-assessments of compliance position;
              and
          •   helps deliver the objectives of the Turnaround Plan.

3.145   In an update provided in September 2024, Thames Water told us that the CAS tool had
        completed its assessment across all WWTW for key permit conditions, and all sewage
        pumping stations for all permit conditions and that an assessment of storm overflows
        was also due to commence that month. It also noted that, as with the DAM, the CAS is
        intended to be a programme subject to continual development, with the business
        currently assessing the implementation of a digital tool to accelerate assessments at a
        higher frequency across all permit requirements and asset types.

3.146   In addition to the development of these new tools and processes to enable it to monitor
        and manage the performance of its sites more effectively, Thames Water told us it has
        taken steps to enhance the knowledge, skills and capabilities of its front-line teams
        (areas, which as explained below, its own internal reviews had highlighted as risks). This
        is to ensure that teams understand the importance of maintaining Thames Water's
        monitors, operational responses to potential FFT non-compliance and the regulatory

         framework surrounding FFT and storm spills 178. It told us that it will drive this
         enhancement through its Environmental Management System (EMS) via training, new

3.147    Thames Water has also told us that it has substantially upgraded training programmes
         throughout the business as a key output of the company's Turnaround Plan. This
         includes, for example, rolling out additional mandatory training for pollution awareness
         at WWTW, including mandatory training for front-line managers in relation to the
         management of WWTW (including incidents at WWTW); mandatory training on pollution
         and permits for new starters; and mandatory pollution awareness training across the
         company's waste treatment site teams. In addition, it has also consolidated its internal
         programmes relating to employee culture, such as the company's “Speak Up”
         whistleblowing programme and has taken steps to improve its processes and the
         channels available to staff members for raising concerns. This includes a bespoke
         “Business Integrity & Investigations” team that manages its whistleblowing programme.
         It also operates a “Speak Up” hotline, run by an external charity which allows employees
         to anonymously make reports regarding unethical behaviour within the company. In
         addition, a mandatory training module on honest and ethical behaviour has been
         introduced to encourage the reporting of any environmental compliance concerns.

3.148    Based on the evidence that Thames Water has provided during the course of our
         investigation, it is clear that it is now taking steps to improve its processes and controls
         and has enhanced significantly its use of, and access to, data to allow its to monitor
         trends and to have better oversight over compliance at its WWTW. However, the
         evidence we have seen also points to significant deficiencies in its previous processes
         and controls 179. Indeed, a number of documents provided by the company in response to
         our information requests suggest that the company was aware of, at least some of, the
         deficiencies and shortcomings in its systems and controls that it is now seeking to
         address.

3.149    In September 2019, for example, a paper presented to Thames Water's Audit and Risk
         Committee (ARRC) 180 set out the findings of an internal review into the potential risks

         faced by the company as a result of Ofwat's enforcement action against Southern

3.150    As part of its review, the company examined a range of different risk categories to reflect
         the range of its operational and management activities and rated each category as red,
         amber or green. This included Operations, Asset Management, Reporting, Culture and
         Governance amongst others. Under Operations, for example, which covered operational
         staff as well as management and technical support (both of which were amber rated),
         the review noted the competence and skills of staff and the strong staff ethos to protect
         the environment, but also found that operating awareness of legal requirements and
         permit compliance obligations was immature, with a focus on numeric effluent
         parameters (that is, "end of pipe" metrics), and that, in many areas, delivery of
         compliant activities was reliant on operator instinct.

3.151    In terms of asset management too (which included on-site alarms and documentation
         and which the review rated as red), the review noted that site operating manuals were
         on a programme of renewal for sites with a new EPR discharge permit only and that
         some manuals and diagrams were out of data and not reflective of the site log books;
         that alarms were not responded to (so increasing the risk of prolonged pollution); and
         site documentation could not be used in operation or in mitigation if required. The
         report recommended a review of the alarm strategy and the promotion of digital
         opportunities to prevent pollutions, building it into site documentation, maintenance
         and training regimes.

3.152    A further Internal Audit Report on Pollutions Reduction and Prevention Risk, Audit &
         Assurance from 17 May 2021 182 also highlighted the risk that a lack of defined
         competencies for both internal employees and external contractors could lead to an
         inability to ensure that wastewater teams had the skills and knowledge to perform
         operations that meet the company's legal obligations and priorities on pollutions. The
         report found that beyond locating the site’s EPR permit, site operators were not
         expected to know permit parameters; that outside of the inspection process there was
         no formal training programme and/or onboarding of employees into specific roles to
         address this gap 183. It concluded that the absence of a formal training programme, built

       In its January 2024 response, Thames Water told us that it recognised Ofwat’s concerns regarding
   the adequacy of training across its network and WWTW estate. However, it also pointed to the upgrades
   and improvements it has made as part of its Turnaround Plan. This includes (i) updates within the PIRP
   to address mandatory training for front-line managers in relation to the management of WWTW
   (including incidents at WWTW); (ii) mandatory training on pollution and permits for new starters; and
   (iii) mandatory pollution awareness training across its waste treatment site teams. It also noted that a
