        on key technical competencies to enable employees to reduce and prevent pollutions,
        could result in ineffective decisions making impacting on the environment and increase
        the risk of Thames Water being in breach of legal and regulatory obligations.

3.153   The audit also considered Thames Water's environmental inspection regime, noting that
        this was performed by the EMS Team and assessed operational sites for compliance to
        the EMS standard, which in turn addressed compliance to the EPR permits. The report
        pointed out that this regime could be considered as a preventative control activity which
        could potentially drive a reduction in pollution. Having reviewed the process at a number
        of Thames Water's sites, however, the audit team noted that they were unable to verify
        that the inspection regime was sufficient to monitor the entire estate and highlighted
        that higher risk sites might not be reviewed at the frequency that they needed to be.
        The audit team also highlighted an absence of Senior Leadership Team oversight on
        environmental inspection performance that would enable informed decision making on
        mitigating or reducing pollution. As a result, they pointed to the risk that the company's
        processes might fail or might not effectively keep pace with changes in the legislative
        and/or complex regulatory landscape and thereby lead to instances of non-compliance
        with the company's obligations.

3.154   As a result of the 2021 review by Thames Water's Internal Audit team, a number of
        actions were agreed to strengthen its environmental inspection regime. These included
        checks to verify that site operators had received the necessary awareness training of
        EPR permits and where to access information for the site (including in relation to storm
        tanks); checks that site operators were aware of the Operations Plan schedules for their
        site (which contain key elements to maintain compliance on a daily basis); that process
        documents were updated. The review also recommended the implementation of a
        performance monitoring and reporting process of environmental inspections to be
        presented to the EGB on a monthly basis.

3.155   The audit also pointed to the need for a refresh of the company's EMS house-keeping
        checklist intended to encourage site operators to monitor the assets on their site with
        the aim of driving compliance to EPR permits. In reviewing the existing process, the
        audit noted that the EMS house-keeping exercise was intended to be performed once a
        month on a large site and every quarter on a smaller site with the site operators self-
        declaring that their sites were compliant to the EMS standards which in turn address
        the EPR permits. The output from this exercise was then used to report internally on


   relevant training for compliance with storm discharge permit conditions at its WWTW). It also pointed
   out that training on environmental permit requirements (including training on the UWWTR and the
   requirements for effluent compliance) is mandatory for all new technician starters, with a mandatory
   refresher programme for technicians commencing which will run every three years. In addition, it
   noted that there are multiple training programmes for the operational team and for technicians at
   WWTW, including, for example, sessions for Thames Water's management on the “Use of Storm Tank”.
   This session was part of the EMS briefings held when regulations change, or business processes are
   updated.


                                                    103
          Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                              on Thames Water

        performance and drive improvements in operational compliance. The audit report found,
        however, that the EMS reports from the completed house-keeping exercises were not
        kept up to date on the EMS system. This meant that it was not possible to evidence that
        housekeeping activities were being completed and therefore, opportunities were being
        missed to drive positive behaviour towards the up-keep of sites with the aim of reducing
        pollutions.

3.156   The reports cited above date from 2019 and 2021 respectively, and clearly indicate that,
        contrary to Thames Water's claims about the robustness of its processes and
        procedures, significant weaknesses existed. It would also appear that many of the issues
        and risks identified in the 2019 reports were still being highlighted in the 2021 report,
        suggesting that the company had failed to address them in a timely manner or with
        sufficient urgency.

3.157   While recognising the steps that Thames Water has taken, and is taking, to improve its
        access to, and use of, data on spills from storm overflows across its WWTW estate and to
        ensure that the issues encountered with the installation of EDMs during AMP6 are not
        repeated, it is clear that further work is needed. The fact that in its 2022 annual return
        to the Environment Agency the company identified 312 storm overflows which had not
        previously been listed, and which did not have monitors in place, and many of which
        were also unpermitted 184, points to ongoing problems with its management and
        oversight of its wastewater assets.



   Thames Water's governance arrangements and the role of the
   Board in ensuring the company is meetings its environmental
   obligations
3.158   During the course of our investigation, Thames Water stressed that its Board and
        Executive Team are fully committed to addressing any potential non-compliance with all
        aspects of its environmental obligations. While acknowledging that legal compliance
        must be the baseline for any organisation, it also told us that it is aware that its
        customers and communities expect Thames Water to go beyond this, to protect and
        improve environmental quality and take account of the value that its communities place
        on its watercourses.

3.159   It explained that organisationally, governance and oversight of its environmental
        permitting requirements had been separated within the business from its Operations
        directorate, with its Engineering and Asset directorate assurance function providing
        independent assessment of its performance and compliance. This function oversaw the
        annual quality monitoring programme for wastewater discharges (operator self-


   184 As highlighted previously, Thames Water told us that, following the introduction on its unpermitted

   CSO programme, this number had now been reduced.

                                                     104
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

        monitoring) as well as the self-monitoring of final effluent flow, both of which are
        audited annually by the Environment Agency.

3.160   In order to keep the Executive team and Board informed regarding Thames Water's
        performance with respect to its price review Performance Commitments (PCs) and the
        Environment Agency's EPA rating, its Engineering and Asset team reported to it on the
        company's numeric discharge performance (that is, its "end of pipe" compliance), as
        well as compliance with its FE permitted flow conditions, including DWF and Missing
        Data performance. The FE total daily flows were reviewed on a monthly basis at an
        operational team level and non-compliances investigated, initially at a local level but
        also with subsequent escalation via the assurance function, and reports made to the
        Environment Agency at year-end in line with permitted requirements.

3.161   As outlined previously, Thames Water told us that as an escalation point, its monthly
        senior management level EGB has, since November 2019, provided oversight, assurance
        and challenge on operational performance and environmental compliance. These EGB
        meetings are chaired by the Head of Environmental Regulation and Quality. Members of
        the Executive receive details of the agreed actions from this meeting to provide
        oversight and attend on a quarterly basis. Insights into the company's environmental
        performance and compliance, including in relation to flow matters, have also been
        reported via the Operations Director at Board level via the quarterly Health, Safety and
        Environment (HSEC) committee meetings, which are chaired by an independent non-
        Executive Director. The Chair reports back to the Board with an update from the HSEC.

3.162   In its September 2024 response to our consultation Thames Water also told us that it had
        created a new “Environmental Matters” Steering Group which had its first meeting at the
        beginning of 2024. Whilst initially informal, the Group now has formal terms of reference
        which is to provide an overview of existing and future environmental compliance
        performance and risk, ensure upcoming legislation or changes are identified across the
        business with key risks escalated, and with risk owners assigned as required. The Group
        reports into, and escalates matters upwards to, the EGB. In addition, Thames Water has
        also appointed a senior lawyer, who has been in post since July 2024, to support in
        providing oversight and assurance of environmental compliance.

3.163   In its responses to our section 203 notices Thames Water has acknowledged that,
        historically, its oversight and management could have been better 185. However, it has
        also claimed that, overall, its systems of planning, risk management, internal control,
        and performance management, coupled with its information systems and improvement
        actions, have enabled the Executive and Board to broadly assess the company’s overall
        compliance with its licence and regulatory obligations - including its obligations under
        section 94 WIA91. It outlined the following list of fora and activities which, it claims,

         the Ring-fencing Certificate (RFC). To enable the Board to sign the RFC, and as outlined
         above, Thames Water also prepares a Risk and Compliance Statement (RCS). This is
         designed to identify risks across the organisation and assess the extent to which any
         risks identified might impact on the ability of the company to carry out its regulated
         activities.

   189
       More recently too, it has included data on spills to the environment in its Board reporting with the
   intention of using this to track its progress on spills reduction.
   190
       As part of the price review process Ofwat sets performance commitments to incentivise companies
   to deliver on the outcomes that matter to their customers and the environment. Performance
   commitments have either financial or reputational incentives (‘Outcome Delivery Incentives (ODIs)’)
   associated with them. The majority of these incentives are financial which means that if a company
   performs better than the performance commitment level, it may get an outperformance payment
   (which means that it recovers more revenue from customers) and if it performs below the commitment
   level it may have to make an underperformance payment (which means that customers pay less). In
   this way, each company’s management and its investors are encouraged to deliver what customers
   want. They incur outperformance payments for doing better than their performance commitment
   target; and underperformance payments if fail to meet those targets. The underperformance payments
   incurred by companies for not meeting performance levels set at a price review are distinct from
   enforcement penalties that Ofwat may impose under section 22A of the WIA91. The former is part of
   mechanisms used at price reviews to incentivise good performance and the achievement of agreed
   performance standards; the latter are penalties for breaching enforceable statutory or licence
   requirements.
   All water companies in England and Wales are also required to publish information about their
   performance in an Annual Performance Report. This includes information about how they have done in
   meeting their performance commitments; whether they have earned any out or underperformance
   payments for their delivery against these commitments; and if companies are failing to deliver, how
   they will put things right.

                                                     108
          Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                              on Thames Water

              operational asset failure, and the controls established to prevent (or mitigate the
              impact of) asset failure, including current risk exposures;

          •   A report from the Internal Audit team covering ‘Pollutions prevention and controls’
              which was circulated to the Executive team and ARRC members in May 2021 which
              identified improvements required to reduce the risk of non-compliance;

          •   A report to the Board in September 2021 on the approach to address storm
              discharges and ensure compliance with the company's legal obligation to treat
              enough flow at its sites before discharging to the environment (flow non-
              compliance).

3.171   The information provided to us by Thames Water does appear to show that since 2020,
        the Executive and the Board have begun to focus more on the company's performance in
        terms of spills and pollution incidents and, more recently (since the launch of the
        Environment Agency and Ofwat investigations), on other aspects of environmental
        permit compliance, including flow compliance. However, prior to this, and based on the
        evidence provided to us, Executive and Board engagement on these issues appears to
        have been very limited.

3.172   This appears to have been the case in relation to the G2G programme and the decisions
        about the prioritisation and speed with which compliance risks would be addressed. In
        its response, Thames Water told us that its work on identifying and resolving FFT issues
        through its G2G programme had been conducted "with the awareness of the Board". The
        documents we have seen confirm that the Executive and Board were made aware of the
        findings of the G2G risk assessment and so were aware of the potential scale of non-
        compliance risk across the WWTW estate (mainly via heatmaps, showing the number of
        sites rated red, amber, green). However, those documents also seem to indicate that the
        main focus of the discussions with Board about the G2G programme related to the level
        of funding included in the company's business plans, rather than any real consideration
        of what the true scale of compliance risk, identified by the G2G assessment, might mean
        in terms of the company's compliance with its environmental permits more generally,
        and ultimately, its compliance with its duties under the UWWTR and section 94 WIA91.

3.173   Thames Water also acknowledged that, in the past, there had been no specific line item
        in its regular reports to Board addressing compliance with the company's duties under
        section 94 WIA91 / UWWTR and it appears to have failed to make the connection between
        complying with its environmental permits, including its FFT permit conditions, and its
        wider regulatory obligations.

3.174   This is surprising as Thames Water should have been aware of, and should have been
        alert to, the risk that a failure to meet FFT permit conditions might mean in terms of the
        company's wider compliance position. Not only had the company been the subject of
        previous Environment Agency prosecutions due to the environmental impact of the


                                                   109
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

         failure of a number of its sites to treat sufficient FFT, but in June 2019 Ofwat had issued
         our decision 191 in our Southern Water enforcement case for breaches of its licence
         conditions and its statutory duties. The Southern Water case clearly set out the
         importance of companies meeting FFT permit conditions and explained the link between
         failures to meet FFT permit conditions and a company's wider duties.

3.175    Moreover, when issuing our decision for the Southern Water case, we also wrote to the
         CEOs of all water companies 192 to ensure that they were aware of our findings against
         Southern Water and that these findings were widely understood across their
         organisations. In that letter we asked them to consider whether they were confident
         that the events which occurred at Southern Water could not happen in their own
         organisations. In addition, we used these letters as an opportunity to reinforce our
         message to the sector that we will not tolerate companies that "show scant regards to
         their responsibilities to customers and the environment" and noted that "[c]ompanies
         should expect us to step up the sanctions we impose for breaches of this nature". 193

3.176    It is clear from information provided to us by Thames Water, that the implications of the
         Southern Water case had been considered by the company. Indeed, it would appear that
         it was described, by the company, as an opportunity to learn about Ofwat’s expectations
         in terms of demonstrating compliance with Section 94, Licence Condition P and its
         Environment Agency permit requirements.

3.177    In a paper discussed by its ARRC 194 in September 2019, referred to above, Ofwat’s
         approach to section 94 was highlighted as was the central duty of companies to ensure
         full permit compliance at WWTW and to ensure that discharges of untreated wastewater
         only occur in accordance with permit conditions. The paper also noted the fact that in
         the Southern Water case Ofwat had taken into account levels of timely investment and
         expectations of a proactive approach to maintenance.

3.178    This paper also considered what the Southern Water case might mean for Thames Water
         in terms of its own compliance regime; highlighted risks with its current position; and
         recommended ways of mitigating those risks. This included a review of its compliance
         commitments and performance; its site-based systems and processes to ensure that
         WWTW and network assets were operated in a compliant manner; the independence of
         its sampling programme (for final effluent quality or 'end-of-pipe' compliance); its

         monitoring, reporting and intervention approach; its asset maintenance investment

3.179    Previously, we set out the review's 195 findings in terms of the company's operation of its
         WWTW; its asset management and staff training. In addition, the review also highlighted
         a number of other risks. On reporting, for example, it noted that the process in relation
         to 'end-of-pipe' effluent compliance was mature, but it rated reporting on all other EPR
         and legislation requirements as red. This reflected the fact that while information on
         "end of pipe" compliance, pollutions and any ongoing enforcement action by the
         Environment Agency was reported monthly to the Board (with tracked pollution updates
         provided to senior management and daily updates provided from operational control),
         parameters other than ‘end-of-pipe' (such as flow compliance and descriptive permit
         breaches) were not reported. This, according to the review findings, raised the risk that
         these compliance failures were potentially unknown. It recommended, therefore, the
         establishment of a full legal requirements tracker with performance reported monthly
         and prioritised on the basis of environmental harm.

3.180    In relation to corporate culture and governance too, the review concluded that
         culturally, there was some evidence that a small but significant proportion of staff (14%)
         were not comfortable in raising environmental compliance concerns with management.
         It noted that, in the past, this had meant that these risks had not reached senior
         management attention and pointed to the need for better oversight to enable the
         company to satisfy itself that behaviours throughout the business were aligned with its
         environmental policy and corporate commitments 196.

3.181    Having assessed the different aspects of the company's compliance regime, the review
         also considered the extent to which "[t]he actions of the company were the actions to be
         expected of a reasonable company". 197 It concluded that historic prioritisation of capital
         maintenance had not put robust and resilient environmental compliance of all
         permit/consent conditions as a high priority, rather concentrating on 'end-of-pipe'
         compliance. It also noted that current asset condition had historically not been
         considered in the context of the development of future legislative requirements, even
         with issues being highlighted through the company's formal systems. It recommended,
         therefore, that the governance behind capital spend and investment should formally

3.183    The shortcomings in its systems and processes identified in the 2019 report to ARRC
         were also reflected in other documents provided by Thames Water in response to our

         Permitting Regulations (EPRs) Risk, Audit & Assurance from January 2019 199, had also
         noted, for example, that "The control environment is unsatisfactory and needs major
         improvements to reduce the organisation's exposure to risk to an acceptable level for
         ensuring compliance with Environmental Permit Regulations. We have found that
         governance and ownership is unclear and incomplete, performance reporting has a bias
         towards pollutions and is not designed to pick up other compliance areas".

3.184    That 2019 audit report also noted that governance arrangements for environmental
         permitting were unevenly spread across the various permitting requirements and that
         the company's existing policy was not effective in setting direction or monitoring
         performance. In relation to performance reporting, the report found that performance of
         permitting was monitored via a number of processes including alarms, monitors and
         sampling regimes with the results being reported both internally to business unit
         performance meetings and externally to the Environment Agency. However, it also found
         that, as with governance and oversight, monitoring of performance was mainly aligned
         to EPA requirements and processes that existed under the previous consent regime that
         focus on the quality of wastewater treatment rather than flow management.

3.200      Having considered Thames Water business plan, Ofwat issued its draft determination for
           in July 2024. 211 In that we rejected Thames Water's request for £677 million under the
           WAAP in full because we considered that "any improvements required to address current
           non-compliance against existing permits must be addressed by companies, and we will
           not allow customers to pay for this". We noted that "Thames Water did not provide
           sufficient and convincing evidence to demonstrate that the enhancement is being
           sought in relation to a new compliance measure and not an issue on a pre-existing
           permit or to meet growth at these sites that has already been funded".

3.201      Following the publication of our PR24 draft determination, Thames Water submitted a
           further enhancement case for funding for the WAAP 212. In doing so, it argued that the
           funding was needed not just to address permit compliance issue at its sites of concern
           but also address population growth, changes to Environment Agency guidance and to
           statutory requirements. As a result of these drivers, and a better understanding of the
           likely costs needed to address the issues it had identified at its sites of concern, Thames
           Water also increased its request for funding for the WAAP to £1,044 million.

3.202      In our Final Determination for PR24 which was published in December 2024 213, we were
           not convinced by the new arguments and evidence that Thames Water had submitted to
           support its enhancement case, so its request for £1,044 million funding was not allowed.
           However, we also noted that "despite the issues with the evidence Thames Water has
           provided, we are concerned that Thames Water may not have sufficient capacity in its
           sewage treatment works [STW] to accommodate growth. Given the potential impact of
           growth in the Thames Water region, it is not in the best interests of customers and the
           environment to wait another five years for Thames Water to provide better evidence,
           given the potential wider impacts on housing and economic growth." In our Final
           Determination we therefore provided Thames Water with a £250 million growth at STWs
           gated allowance to allow it to double growth at sewage treatment expenditure over the

   Enhanced governance and oversight of environmental compliance

3.210    In its January 2024 and September 2024 responses, Thames Water told us that it accepts
         that historically, its processes could have been better. However, it also stressed that as
         part of its Turnaround Plan it had made substantial enhancements to its governance and
         now claims to have sophisticated processes to address and remediate compliance issues
         at sites across its network. It also told us that within these processes there are now clear
         escalation routes to the Board and Executive (who remain apprised of compliance in any
         case), allowing for both reactive and proactive interventions.

3.211    Thames Water pointed, in particular, to its improved monitoring capability which now
         allows it to monitor the live performance of its assets, and to actively identify potential
         compliance issues. It noted that this data is subject to verification (for example, through
         the DAM tool) to identify any anomalies and is then assessed by the company's
         operational teams. Following risk (and cost) assessments, sites are then either
         remediated through “business as usual” asset maintenance, or escalated to the (now
         revamped) EGB for consideration 218. If escalated, the EGB will then either allocate the

   218
      Thames Water told us that its operational team assess the scale of potential non-compliance,

   issues can fall into one of two categories for remediation. These are lower cost, “business as usual”
   asset maintenance issues which are managed and financed through its “Minor Capital Works” or

        site to one of Thames Water's existing investment programmes (such as its Wastewater

        Board. Thames Water also noted that, in addition to reviewing escalated items of non-
        compliance, the Executive and Board, are also kept regularly appraised of Thames
        Water's remediation programmes.

3.212   In relation to its reporting and governance arrangements, Thames Water had previously
        informed us that it had introduced changes to ensure a clearer line of sight between the
        company's environmental permits non-compliance and the Executive Risk Committee,
        ARRC and the Board as part of the company's eight-year Turnaround Plan. 219

3.213   These changes to its internal governance arrangements included:

          •   Weekly Business Updates: to enable the Executive Committee to escalate key
              issues or insights from the day to day running of the business.
          •   Strategic Decision Making: monthly sessions to enable critical cross-cutting
              decisions on areas such as Strategy, Governance, Innovation, Growth, Resilience in
              the round and Culture to be considered.
          •   Business Performance Review: monthly review of performance and improvement
              activities, monitoring performance against PCs, ODIs, key risk metrics, strategic
              credibility metrics and budget. This, Thames Water told us, will improve line of
              sight between performance goals and delivery and allow for timely cross-functional
              trade-offs and interventions.

3.214   As part of these new governance arrangements, on a quarterly basis the Executive Risk
        Committee also now reviews the top current risks from across the business, any material
        improvements or deteriorations in existing risks, and any new risks and changes in the
        internal and external risk climate. Where they meet the threshold, these include non-
        compliance with environmental permits and other regulations. Thames Water states
        that the Executive Risk Committee now reviews and challenges the assessment and
        management of these risks. These risks are also now reported to the Board through its
        ARRC.

           Group 221 (previously known as the “Programme Board”), chaired by the Assets and

           group of the EGB which oversees compliance and reports into the Executive and the
           Board.

3.220      In a further update from January 2024, Thames Water told us that the EGB had
           undergone further improvements to its governance structure which were finalised in
           April 2023. These improvements included a series of centrally governed monthly
           meetings (including Thames Water's steering groups), co-chaired by the Asset and
           Engineering Director and Operations Director. Within these meetings, escalated items
           from the operational teams are discussed with a resolution approved. Where possible
           (and commensurate to risk) assets are then progressed by the relevant sponsoring
           group (for example, the WAAP or WINEP sponsoring group) which will include the asset
           within the scope of their programme. The operational team will implement temporary
           mitigations in relation to these assets (to minimise the risk of any potential non-
           compliance) until remediation under the respective programme.

3.221      Where a failing asset is sufficiently great in risk or cost, there are now multiple
           escalation routes from the EGB to committees such as the HSEC, ARRC, and ultimately
           the Executive Committee and Board. For example, at each meeting the HSEC reviews an

3.222   In addition to the substantive enhancements to its investigation and resolution
        pathways, Thames Water also told us that it had also enhanced the synergy between its
        main investment programmes, the WAAP (which is aimed at addressing potential permit
        compliance issues at WWTW) and the WINEP (which implements, amongst other things,
        future legal requirements and the objectives of the SODRP). This new approach aims
        towards holistic solutions to compliance risk and required asset upgrades/remediation,
        where potential concerns are not considered in isolation. For example, if network
        permitted sites and WWTW are addressed within the same remedial system, solutions
        tackling flow at source will gain greater weight when considering remedial or
        investment options, because such solutions will have wider holistic system benefits as
        opposed to a narrow site-by site assessment. Thames Water explained that, where
        possible, the scope of both WAAP and WINEP have been grouped together to drive
        efficiencies as well as ensuring that improvements at a particular site are not solely
        designed to address the issue triggering intervention, but instead consider all projects
        required to be undertaken at the relevant site during the AMP and deliver them as one
        combined project.

3.223   By combining WAAP and WINEP, Thames Water claims that this ensures a robust,
        forward looking and proactive approach to compliance and remediation, and ensures
        that its assets can meet both current and future compliance needs. Thames Water also
        told us that it is confident that this process now delivers a system-wide, synergistic
        approach to assessing and remedying compliance concerns.

3.224   The Sponsoring Group now meets monthly to discuss and endorse programme decisions
        and to provide direction to the business. This includes, for example, the prioritisation of
        funding for FFT sites, strategic business risks around potential non-compliance and how
        funding should be prioritised, and the performance of its delivery programmes (delivery
        versus baseline, expected cost outturns, risks, and other areas). Should issues within
        this Sponsoring Group fail to be resolved, they are escalated to the EGB (with further
        escalation to the Executive as described above).

3.225   More widely, WAAP updates are now provided to the EGB every two months. Thames
        Water also noted that WAAP is also discussed at a high level monthly meeting with the
        Environment Agency and a quarterly update on WAAP is provided directly to the Board
        ensuring direct oversight of the programme at Board level.

3.226   Furthermore, Thames Water told us that the legal requirements tracker (which was
        recommended as part of the company's 2019 “Lines of Defence” audit) to identify

                                                   124
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

         potential compliance failures is operational with a monthly performance programme. As
         part of this process, there are weekly flow and pollution meetings to track flow
         compliance data and corrective actions identified in the tracker. Thames Water noted
         that while actions from these meetings can mostly be resolved locally without the need
         for escalation to the EGB, if further action is necessary, the Chair of these meetings can
         escalate matters directly to the Operations Director (the Chair of the EGB).

3.227    Finally, Thames Water told us in January 2024 that in order to ensure that it has
         sufficient governance oversight over the company’s compliance position, there is now a
         dedicated role for this which includes responsibility for oversight over the Environmental
         Management System 222.

   Measures to reduce spills to the environment

3.228    Thames Water told us that the use of data from its EDM monitors to have near real-time
         visibility of discharges from its storm overflows is part of an overarching plan which goes
         beyond addressing compliance risk and aims to achieve zero spills by 2050 by tackling
         the primary root causes. This, according to Thames Water, means going beyond the
         improvement of its own assets, to tackle a number of other issues that cause spills,
         including ground water infiltration and surface water ingress – which account for a
         significant proportion of the spills from its overflow assets.

3.229    Its ambitions for reducing spills, pollutions incidents and improving river quality in its
         region have been set out in a number of published plans. These include its PIRP 223 with
         an ambition to reduce the number of pollution incidents in its area by 30% by 2025, as

         an integral part of our DWMP and wider strategic and delivery programmes. We have set
         ambitious targets in our delivery programme to ensure our storm overflow discharges do

   Addressing stakeholder concerns

3.230      During the course of our investigation, Thames Water told us that it is determined to
           rebuild trust with its local communities and stakeholders. In addition to the measures
           set out in Compliance First and WAAP and in its SODRP and DWMP, it is also taking steps
           to improve transparency and make data on discharges from its sites more openly
           available. This, it is hoped, will enable river users to make informed judgements about
           the advisability of taking part in recreational activities on their local river. More widely, it
           will allow customers and stakeholders to have an up-to-date picture of what is
           happening across Thames Water's infrastructure and to ask questions or hold it to
           account where necessary. Thames Water told us that it recognises that in some cases,
           particularly where there is a lack of trust, ‘seeing is believing’. This has led the company
           to install web cams covering storm discharge locations at a number of its WWTW, with
           live feeds provided via the internet to interested stakeholders.

3.231      Thames Water also told us that once its plans for eliminating risks of non-compliance
           are finalised it will publish them on its website and explain them to its Catchment
           Partnerships and other local stakeholders. It will report progress against its plan at least
           annually, setting out where it has made improvements in both its assets and monitoring
           systems, as well as reporting progress at regular meetings, to enable stakeholders to
           comment on progress. Where there are particular concerns, it will also work with local
           councils to deliver roadshows, explaining what it is planning to do, why and by when.

3.232      Thames Water acknowledged that engaging in ongoing discussions with its diverse array
           of environmental stakeholders has proved challenging and resource intensive. It noted,
           however, that the establishment of the Catchment Partnerships, under the
           Government’s Catchment Based Approach 228 initiative, had given the company a
           mechanism to work with the majority of local environmental stakeholders in its region.
           According to Thames Water it has a dedicated representative on each of the 27
           Catchment Partnerships in its area but also noted that these partnerships are at
           differing levels of maturity, with some having dedicated offices and employing more
           than ten full time staff, while others are composed entirely of voluntary organisations
           and individuals working in their spare time. As a result, its engagement model varies
           with each partnership depending on their capacity and needs.

3.233      Thames Water explained that this collaborative approach is particularly important where
           compliance issues are caused, or exacerbated, by excess surface water arriving at the
           WWTW. Working with local stakeholder groups can help to identify the sources of such

3.235      In addition to its work with local environmental groups and stakeholders, Thames Water
           also told us it recognised how concerned its customers are about the environment and
           the impact of its activities on it. Between July and September 2021, it commissioned
           customer research into the relative preferences across ten public value initiatives. River
           health and Thames Water's ambition to achieve zero pollution incidents were the topics
           of greatest interest to participants. This, it told us, was consistent with the conclusions
           from other customer research the company had undertaken, including its December
           2021 research of a sample of customers from its Customers' Voice panel to enable it to
           understand what actions they would see as being appropriate ‘to put things right’.

3.236      That research found that most respondents wanted greater transparency from Thames
           Water as to how much damage was being done by its non-compliance and what it was
           doing to prevent it happening in the future. The majority also believed that Thames
           Water was not currently doing enough to rectify this issue, and that its response was too
           slow. The findings also showed that customers were clear that a fine should act as a
           deterrent and motivate Thames Water to put things right but also expressed the view
           that companies can regard fines as being a ‘cost of doing business’ rather than a
           deterrent to non-compliance. Thames Water also noted that a minority of those who
           participated in the research were sympathetic to the situation Thames Water was in and
           thought that, on this occasion, it should not be fined but should work with Ofwat to
           resolve the issue. However, irrespective of action taken, participants in the customer
           research were almost unanimous in wanting Thames Water to be compliant as soon as
           possible.

4. Our assessment and findings

Introduction and overview
4.1   As required by section 22A(6) of the WIA91, we set out the statutory provisions and
      relevant licence conditions that we consider Thames Water has breached and explain
      our assessment of the evidence we have considered in reaching our conclusion. We
      have also taken account of the responses that we received to the consultation on our
      draft decision.

4.2   For the reasons set out further below, we are satisfied that, from at least 2017, Thames
      Water has contravened Regulations 4(4)(a) and Regulation 4(2) and Schedule 2
      UWWTR by failing to ensure that its WWTW and collecting systems are designed,
      constructed, operated and maintained adequately according to the standards and
      requirements set out in these regulations. These breach are in themselves breaches of
      section 94 WIA91.

4.3   The magnitude and extent of Thames Water’s contraventions of the UWWTR is
      significant, widespread and has persisted over an extended period. Accordingly, we
      are satisfied that in addition to these UWWTR breaches, this indicates a systemic
      failure by the company to make appropriate provision for effectually providing
      drainage and dealing with the contents of sewers and, therefore, itself constitutes a
      breach of section 94 WIA91.

4.4   We are also satisfied that, from at least 2017, Thames Water has contravened
      paragraph 12 of Condition P by failing to ensure it had adequate financial and
      management resources and systems of planning and internal control in place to carry
      out its regulated activities and, in particular, to manage and operate its WWTW to
      ensure compliance with UWWTR and section 94 WIA91.

4.5   As outlined in Section 3, this view has been reached following a lengthy investigation
      during which we made multiple detailed requests for information and data to Thames
      Water. In addition to the hundreds of documents provided by the company, we
      reviewed environmental performance and spills data published by the Environment
      Agency and considered relevant information provided to Ofwat by stakeholders,
      including eNGOs, in Thames Water's area. We have also taken into account the
      responses that we received to the consultation on our draft decision.

4.6   As noted previously, our investigation broadened from issues related to FFT to wider
      concerns about Thames Water's management of its wastewater treatment network as
      more evidence was uncovered. This evidence has revealed a failure by the company to
      have a clear view or understanding about the state of its wastewater assets (including
      the actual number of storm overflows on its network); about how its assets were

                                               129
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       actually performing; and, therefore, about whether these assets were being managed
       and operated in a manner which was compliant with the company's obligations under
       UWWTR and section 94 WIA91. Moreover, even when Thames Water was aware of
       compliance risks, it failed to address these risks in an appropriate and timely manner
       and, therefore, essentially chose to accept ongoing non-compliance across a
       significant portion of its estate.

4.7    In the following sections, we consider each of the statutory provisions and relevant
       licence conditions that we consider Thames Water has breached.

Findings in relation to a breach of the UWWTR
4.8    As set out in Section 2, Regulation 4 UWWTR requires a water company to ensure that
       its WWTW plants are designed, constructed, operated and maintained to ensure that
       spills only occur in exceptional or unforeseeable circumstances in all local climatic
       conditions, subject to the BTKNEEC test. As explained previously, we have considered
       both the extent to which:

           a) the company's assets comply with the requirements of Regulation 4 UWWTR
              (that is, in terms of whether they are providing sufficient performance), and

           b) the extent to which the company has met its obligation under Regulation 4
              UWWTR to ensure that its assets continue to meet the relevant standards (that
              is, how it has gone about ensuring that they provide sufficient performance).

4.9    While Ofwat is not responsible for assessing and enforcing breaches of environmental
       permits, non-compliance with the conditions of those permits is a strong indicator
       that duties under Regulation 4 UWWTR are not being met, as it might point to the
       asset no longer having the capacity to meet sufficient performance or to inadequate
       operation and maintenance of the asset to enable sufficient performance. These are
       not the only indicators we have looked at, however, and compliance with a permit does
       not necessarily equal compliance with the requirements of Regulation 4.

Regulation 4(4) UWWTR: Failure to meet local demand in normal climatic
conditions at specific WWTW

Failure to meet FFT permit levels

4.10   As explained in Section 3 above, as a result of our investigation and Thames Water's
       assessment of its FFT compliance position, it has identified 157 sites of concern (that
       is, sites potentially non-compliant with FFT or other environmental permit conditions).
       These sites are listed in Appendix A3.




                                                130
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

4.11   While we appreciate that in carrying out its assessment Thames Water adopted a
       cautious approach and also included sites where underperformance may have been
       marginal, the fact that these sites of concern accounted for almost two-thirds of all its
       sites with FFT permits or TDCs indicates significant compliance issues across its
       estate. The breakdown provided by Thames Water showed that 88 WWTW, as it
       acknowledges, were clearly underperforming (and by implication were not capable of,
       or did not have the capacity to, meet the FFT requirement in its permit). In addition,
       Thames Water identified 41 sites which it said demonstrated a capability of meeting
       FFT at times but were not doing so consistently; a further 16 sites which Thames Water
       categorised as marginally underperforming sites (but which it also noted were
       typically achieving only 80-92% FFT on average); and 12 sites which it had assessed as
       being generally compliant, but which had signs of a one-off, potentially non-
       compliant, event.

4.12   Thames Water has sought to argue that as it has now determined that 48 of its 157
       sites of concern serve agglomerations with a population equivalent of less than
       2,000 231, that these sites should be excluded from the scope of our investigation and
       our assessment of the company's compliance with Regulation 4 UWWTR. However, as
       we have explained previously, while Regulation 4(2) imposes obligations in respect of
       the provision of collecting systems “for every agglomeration with a population
       equivalent of 2,000 or more”, Regulation 4(4) does apply to smaller agglomerations,
       because, (i) the obligation is to ensure that water is, before discharge, “subject to
       treatment provided in accordance with regulation 5”, and (ii) although regulation 5
       imposes specific obligations as regards the treatment plants that must be in place in
       respect of larger agglomerations, it also requires “appropriate treatment” of water
       from agglomerations of less than 2,000. In our view, therefore, all 157 of Thames
       Water's WWTW which it has identified as sites of concern are also covered by
       Regulation 4(4).

4.13   The scale of the potential FFT non-compliance that Thames Water has identified
       across its WWTW estate is extremely concerning. It is also concerning that during the
       course of our investigation it became clear that the company did not have a complete
       picture of its actual compliance position as it found more sites of concern as our
       investigation progressed. It also acknowledged in its responses to our section 203
       notices that the picture could change again as it was not confident that it had a full
       understanding of its true compliance position. This in itself points to the company
       failing to ensure that it was providing for, operating and maintaining its assets
       consistent with the requirements of Regulation 4(4) UWWTR. The company was also

concern serving agglomerations with a PE of less than 2,000 was now 48. These sites are identified in

       initially unable to identify the root cause of potential FFT non-compliance at many of
       those sites and it was only after several further information requests that the company
       was finally able to do so.

4.14   As set out in Section 3 above (Table 3.3), the results of Thames Water's analysis
       showed that almost all of the sites of concern had multiple root causes for failing FFT
       permit requirements. Data quality issues arising from monitor installation and
       maintenance were identified at close to 90% of the sites (and are seen as ongoing
       issues until U_MON3 and U_MON4 monitors are fully commissioned, tested, certified
       and in operational use). Outside of these data quality issues, poor flow control and
       management was found at 83% of sites and a lack of treatment resilience at 72% of
       sites. Hydraulic capacity restrictions were identified at almost 50% of the sites of
       concern and almost 50% were found to also have issues with site and storm tank
       returns. As we explained in Section 3 and further below, Thames Water has also
       consistently failed to address a number of compliance issues for sites it has identified
       as part of its G2G programme established in 2017.

4.15   We recognise that Thames Water has begun to take steps to address FFT compliance
       issues at its sites, and improve its ability to monitor and manage potential compliance
       risks in a more effective and timely manner. As part of its revised plan for PR24232, for
       example, it included proposals to invest £1,044 million under its WAAP to address
       compliance issues at its sites of concern 233. This was increased from the £677 million
       included in its initial business plan as, according to Thames Water, it also included
       measures to address population grown and climate change, not just restoring
       compliance with permit conditions.

4.16   However, the scale of the investment that Thames Water has estimated that will now
       be needed to address compliance issues across its estate also serves to underline the
       scale and scope of the issues that the company has identified during the course of our
       investigation. It represents a further revision of the investment that it had previously
       estimated would be needed and also represents a further delay in the delivery of
       solutions at these sites. As noted previously, Thames Water had originally told us that
       it had planned to restore compliance at the majority of its sites of concern by the end
       of AMP7 under its Compliance First plan. However, in its September 2024 response to
       our draft decision, Thames Water told us that, at that stage, 24 of the 157 sites of
       concern had completed schemes to resolve root causes which have been prioritised
       under Tranche 1 of the WAAP and that, "subject to confirmation around funding for
       AMP8" (which as noted previously, was not allowed in Thames Water's Final
       Determination for PR24), the estimated delivery for remediation for its sites of concern
       was now the end of AMP8, that is, by 2030. This means, therefore, that compliance

232 Thames Water's PR24 Enhancement Case Sewage Treatment Waste Asset Assurance Programme

(WAAP).
  As noted earlier, Thames Water's Final Determination for PR24 also includes an allowance of £784

million to enable it to reduce the number of spills from storm overflows by 29% by 2029-30.

                                                 132
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       issues at many of Thames Water's sites of concern will have persisted for a very
       extended period of time.

4.17   As explained in Section 2, Regulation 4(4) UWWTR requires that water companies
       ensure that their WWTW assets are designed, constructed, operated and maintained
       to ensure sufficient performance under all normal local climatic conditions, subject to
       exceptional circumstances and BTKNEEC assessment. This means that Thames Water
       needs to ensure that its WWTW assets are able to deal with the normal flows from the
       collecting systems in their catchment in order and have processes to identify and
       respond to changes in the catchment and the resulting flows in its collecting systems,
       to ensure their assets remain fit for purpose for handling these.

4.18   Given the extent of the infrastructural, operational and management issues which
       Thames Water has identified at so many of its WWTW, it is clear that the company's
       processes have not been effective in ensuring its assets are able to meet local demand
       in normal climatic conditions and/or perform sufficiently. It will take a number of years
       for Thames Water to address the issues at these sites of concern and it has not
       provided evidence to show that these issues did not lead to environmental harm or
       that it would be disproportionately costly to address them under BTKNEEC. We are
       therefore satisfied that Thames Water has breached and, until such time as the
       solutions it has identified to address the causes of non-compliance at its sites of
       concern have been implemented, it continues to be in breach of Regulation 4(4)
       UWWTR.

Failure to meet other environmental permit conditions

4.19   While the root cause analysis outlined above relates specifically to FFT non-
       compliance, data provided by Thames Water also indicated compliance concerns in
       respect to other aspects of how its WWTW are designed, constructed, operated and
       maintained. As set out in Section 3, data obtained during the course of our
       investigation indicated a deterioration in the company's DWF compliance position with
       increasing numbers of sites failing to meet DWF permit conditions on at least one
       occasion and an increasing number of sites seeing an erosion in headroom and
       therefore at greater risk of failing. In addition, of its 157 sites of concern, it identified
       issues with storm tank capacity at 57 of these sites.

4.20   Data provided by Thames Water indicated that 78 (28%) of the sites with a DWF permit
       requirement for which it was able to provide information had failed to meet their Q80
       DWF permit conditions at least once in the five-year period from 2017 to 2021; 46 sites
       (16%) had failed Q90 at least once in those five years; 23 sites (8%) had failed Q90
       twice in those five years; and ten sites (4%) had failed to meet Q90 at least three times




                                                133
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       in that five-year period.234 Failure to meet the DWF level in permits can indicate that a
       WWTW no longer has the capacity required to deliver sufficient performance under
       Regulation 4 (4) UWWTR under normal local climatic conditions.

4.21   This evidence on the number of sites failing, or at risk of failing, to meet Q80 and Q90
       DWF permit limits was known and was being flagged within the company and was
       acknowledged as being due, at least in part, to investment not having kept pace with
       changes in local catchments. In a February 2022 report, for example, Thames Water
       noted regarding compliance with its Q90 flow levels that "currently we have 46 failing
       sites in total. If any of these sites fail for a second year then we could have at least 21
       sites at “failed” status by end of 2025 (unless issues fully resolved). 235 This position was
       also discussed at an Executive Performance Review meeting. In that instance, Thames
       Water also noted that “as investment has not kept up with growth” the company could
       have more than 20 failing sites by 2025236.

4.22   With respect to the ten sites which failed to meet Q90 at least three times between
       2017 and 2021, it is clear to us that there are unresolved design, construction,
       maintenance and/or operational issues and that Thames Water has therefore failed to
       ensure that these sites are meeting the requirements of the UWWTR. Accordingly, it
       has breached and continues to be in breach of Regulation 4(4) UWWTR for those sites.

4.23   The fact that a site fails to meet its Q80 permit limit (especially if this happens on a
       persistent or recurring basis), and/or it fails to meet its Q90 permit limit fewer than
       three times in five years, may be a warning sign that the site's performance is
       deteriorating. It should act as a trigger to prompt a company to investigate the
       relevant assets to check their operation and consider if they have sufficient capacity
       to deal with the local demand and whether it needs to, for example, upgrade them or
       contact the Environment Agency to ask for an update to the associated permit
       conditions. In Thames Water's case, as shown in Figure 1 in Section 3 above, it is clear
       that a significant proportion of its WWTW have seen an erosion in headroom and with
       many operating between 90%-100% and >100% of their DWF Q80 levels

4.24   The fact that 57 of the sites of concern that Thames Water has identified also have
       storm tank capacity issues reinforces our finding of Regulation 4(4) UWWTR breach.

4.25   In arriving at our findings we also considered whether there were mitigating
       circumstances that should be taken into account in our assessment of Thames Water's
       compliance with Regulation 4(4) UWWTR (that is, if there were exceptional

          circumstances, no environmental harm or a BTKNEEC exception). However, Thames
          Water was unable to demonstrate that any of these exceptions applied. In fact,
          Thames Water is now taking steps to address these compliance issues and risks as
          part of its WAAP programme. It is proposing to increase investment to ensure that its
          WWTW meet the stipulated storm storage and overflow requirements. In addition,
          outside of the WAAP programme, it proposed investment of £355 million as part of its
          business plan for AMP8 to increase capacity at 15 WWTW. According to the company,
          this investment is intended to "provide treatment capacity to a population equivalent
          to 96,700 people or 40,292 homes. This investment will allow us to remain compliant
          with our discharge permits." 237

4.26      While we welcome these proposed steps, we consider that Thames Water should have
          acted sooner to remedy the issues identified. There will be a number of years before
          these issues are fully resolved and that this, in turn, means that compliance issues
          and risks at many of Thames Water's WWTW will have persisted for an extended period
          of time.

Failure to limit discharges from storm overflows at WWTW

4.27      As discussed in Section 2, the purpose of the UWWTR is to ensure that under all
          normal weather conditions, the wastewater collected by a catchment area's sewer
          system is treated to a required standard before discharge. Untreated discharges to
          the environment should therefore only occur in exceptional circumstances or where
          the costs of reducing harm are disproportionate to the environmental benefit that
          would be gained (the BTKNEEC test), and companies should minimise any adverse
          impacts to the environment arising from these discharges.

4.28      We consider that companies should treat 20 spills per year as a material indicator of a
          breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR which requires further investigation to ensure
          compliance. In the absence of the relevant evidence that there has been exceptional
          circumstances, that no environmental harm has arisen or a BTKNEEC exception exists,
          we will consider storm overflows having 20 or more spills a year as evidence that they
          are probably in breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR. We consider that storm overflows that
          are discharging 20 times or more, where there is no evidence of exceptional
          circumstances, and it is clear there is a BTKNEEC solution (including operational
          solutions) which has not been implemented are clearly in breach of Regulation 4
          UWWTR. Likewise if a company cannot evidence that it has investigated apparent
          routine discharges and taken appropriate remedial action in a timely way, or is in the
          process of investigating such, we consider a company to have breached or to be in
          breach of Regulation 4.




237
      Thames Water's business plan for PR24, page 115.

                                                    135
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

4.29   As set out in Table 3.6, analysis of Thames Water's EDM data shows that the majority of
       the storm overflows at its WWTW have been spilling on such a regular basis that the
       circumstances cannot be considered to be exceptional. In 2021 238 171 storm overflows
       associated with Thames Water's WWTW, (that is 73%), recorded 20 or more spills. In
       2022 (which is considered to have been a relatively dry year) the proportion of storm
       overflows associated with Thames Water's WWTW that spilled 20 or more times
       reduced to 46.5%, but it increased again in 2023, to 69.2%.

4.30   Absent evidence of exceptional circumstances or a BTKNEEC exemption, the fact that
       storm overflows at the WWTW in question are being used so regularly would indicate
       either that Thames Water lacks sufficient capacity at the WWTW to treat the sewage
       being received by the works under normal climatic conditions, or that sufficient
       capacity does exist but that the WWTW is not being operated properly so as to
       appropriately utilise it. In either scenario, Thames Water would be contravening the
       requirements of Regulation 4(4) UWWTR.

4.31   However, as we explained in Section 2, where a company can demonstrate that it has
       reasonably applied the legal tests of Regulation 4 UWWTR and concluded that spills
       were due to exceptional circumstances, that there was no resulting environmental
       harm, or that there is no BTNKEEC solution available to reduce harm from discharges,
       the issues identified would likely not amount to a breach of Regulation 4(4) with
       respect to that site. We have therefore also considered the information contained in
       the company's annual EDM returns to the Environment Agency as well as the
       additional information provided by Thames Water in respect of its investigations of
       sites which were triggered under SOAF (this is, the sites which spilled more than 60
       times). As highlighted in Table 3.6, of the 171 storm overflows associated with Thames
       Water's WWTW which had spilled more than 20 times in 2021, 70 had recorded over 60
       spills. (This reduced to 11% in 2022 but increased again, to 41%, in 2023).

4.32   We have considered the evidence put forward by Thames Water of the SOAF
       investigations it has carried out to assess whether any exceptional circumstances
       existed or whether appropriate BTKNEEC exemptions exist for its highest spilling sites.
       The information that Thames Water provided does not map across consistently to the
       data on the individual sites which are reported as spilling more than 60 times in the
       Environment Agency's published reports. As a result, it is not altogether clear how
       advanced the investigations of other high spilling sites are (particularly as it can take
       a number of years for all stages of the SOAF process to be completed and some sites
       which were high spilling in 2021 may have already had a SOAF investigation underway
       because they had also been high spilling in a previous year.) Nevertheless, we have




238
   The data that Thames Water provided in response to our section 203 notices on the performance of
its wastewater assets related primarily to 2021 so this has been used as the "base year" for our
assessment of its performance against a number of other indicators too.

                                                136
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       reviewed this information and have taken it into account in our penalty assessment.
       We set out the approach that we have adopted below.

Sites spilling frequently due to asset maintenance issues

4.33   For sites triggered for investigation under SOAF, where the root cause of high spills
       was identified as being operational and maintenance (asset maintenance) issues, we
       have taken this into account in our penalty assessment if the cause of the spill has not
       been rectified within the calendar year (as is required under SOAF) so that it
       continues to record high spills in the following year. For example, for sites which
       spilled more than 60 times in 2021 due to asset maintenance reasons we consider that
       site to have not only breached UWWTR but have also included in in our penalty
       assessment if that site also spilled 20 or more times in 2022 as this indicates that the
       cause of the spill has not been resolved as is required under SOAF.

4.34   As noted in Section 3, over 60% of Thames Water's sites were which were triggered for
       investigation under SOAF in 2021 (that is, had spilled more than 60 times) were found
       to have done so because of asset maintenance issues. Similarly, 63% of all the sites
       that Thames Water had told us it had assessed as part of the SOAF process, did not
       progress beyond Stage 1 as they were found to be due to asset maintenance and
       operational issues. The fact that spills at so many of the company's highest spilling
       sites are due to asset maintenance issues is concerning. In our view, a company
       should be able to operate and manage its assets to prevent issues occurring in the
       first place. Spills due to asset maintenance issues should be the exception and not, as
       appears to be the case with Thames Water, the norm.

4.35   Furthermore, if and when a site has recorded high spill numbers due to asset
       maintenance reasons, we would expect the company to act swiftly to resolve those
       issues to prevent the site also recording high spills in subsequent periods: where
       there is an available operational or maintenance fix, carrying out that work would
       almost inevitably constitute a viable BTKNEEC solution, in which case the failure to
       carry out the work would constitute a breach of Regulation 4(4). It appears that
       Thames Water has failed to carry out such operational and maintenance fixes in
       relation to several of its assets with the result that a significant number of sites which
       had spilled more than 60 times in 2021 continued to record 20 or more spills in 2022.

4.36   In its response to the consultation on our draft decision, Thames Water argued that we
       had "likely overstated" the number of its storm overflows which we had included in our
       penalty assessment. This is because, under SOAF, while sites which are high spilling
       due to asset maintenance issues are expected to be remediated within the calendar
       year, where infiltration is identified as the reason for the spill, "it is recognised that
       investigation and resolution of infiltration issues can be difficult, that solutions may be




                                                137
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       iterative, and that infiltration reduction plans may only succeed over the medium to
       long-term".239

4.37   To ensure that we considered this point fully, therefore, we sought further information
       from Thames Water on the number of its storm overflows where infiltration was
       identified as the main cause of the high spills. We also requested details of whether an
       infiltration reduction plan was in place for the particular storm overflow (as is required
       under SOAF); the date the plan had originally been developed and when it was last
       reviewed; whether the plan had been agreed with the Environment Agency 240; and
       information on the company's progress in delivering that plan and the actions it has
       taken to resolve infiltration issues at its sites.

4.38   In its response to our information request, Thames Water also told us that a number of
       sites which were initially recorded as spilling due to infiltration were subsequently
       found to have hydraulic capacity or other asset maintenance issues and were
       therefore re-categorised under the SOAF process. We have also taken this into
       account as part of our updated assessment which is summarised in Table 4.1 below.

4.39 Based on Thames Water's updated information, 45 storm overflows associated with
       Thames Water's WWTW were found to have spilled more than 60 times due to asset
       maintenance issues, including infiltration.

4.40 In terms of our penalty assessment, and the treatment of sites under SOAF, we have
       considered high spilling sites where infiltration or other asset maintenance issues
       were identified as the reason for the spills separately. Our assessment shows that 28 of
       the 45 storm overflow sites were found to have spilled due to asset maintenance issues
       (excluding infiltration). At 5 of these sites, Thames Water appears to have taken
       adequate steps to resolve the issues within the calendar year so that in the following
       year, 2022, there were fewer than 20 spills recorded at those sites. We consider these
       sites were in breach of Regulation 4(4) UWWTR in the past, but as the underlying
       issues were addressed, they were no longer in breach. For the remaining 23 sites,
       however, all recorded 20 or more spills in 2022, indicating that Thames Water failed to
       resolve the cause of the issue within a reasonable timeframe. Therefore, these sites
       have been included in our penalty assessment.

4.41   Of the 45 sites where asset maintenance was identified as the reason the site recorded
       more than 60 spills in 2021, Thames Water told us that at 17 sites infiltration was the
       underlying cause. As noted earlier, while we recognise that under SOAF it is
       acknowledged that it may take longer to address issues at those sites, we nevertheless

239
   SOAF, page 6 (water.org.uk).
240
   Thames Water has Infiltration Reduction Plans (IRPs) or Groundwater Impacted System
