to Thames Water these have been developed in conjunction with the Environment Agency; are
reviewed and updated annually and published in its website.

                                                138
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       expect to see evidence that the company has developed plans to identify and resolve
       the causes of infiltration at these sites as well as evidence that the plans are being
       progressed and the company is actively taking steps to ensure that its assets provide
       sufficient performance.

4.42   Thames Water has developed Infiltration Reduction Plans (IRPs) or Groundwater
       Impacted System Management Plans (GISMPs) for those sites which spilled more than
       60 times due to infiltration in 2021. However, having reviewed these plans 241, it is clear
       that at eight sites (which continued to record 20 or more spills in 2022), while Thames
       Water has various investigations ongoing, there is no evidence of any remedial work
       being undertaken to address the cause of the infiltration. These sites have, therefore,
       also been taken into account as part of our assessment of the appropriate level of
       penalty in this case.

Sites spilling frequently due to hydraulic capacity issues

4.43   We have also revisited our assessment of the number of Thames Water's sites which
       were high spilling due to hydraulic capacity issues to reflect the fact that in the
       information it provided, a number of sites which had originally been thought to have
       been high spilling due to infiltration were subsequently found to be due to hydraulic
       capacity issues. In addition, we have also taken account of more recent information on
       Thames Water's plans to address compliance issues across its wastewater treatment
       network.

4.44   Under SOAF, where the root cause of high spills is identified as being hydraulic
       capacity, further investigation is required to assess whether a BTKNEEC solution
       exists. In our assessment, a failure to carry out a further investigation in a timely
       manner is considered to be a breach. Where an investigation has been completed and
       no BTKNEEC solution was identified or where the investigation is still ongoing (and
       therefore it is not yet clear that a BTKNEEC solution actually exists), the site is
       excluded from both the breach finding and from our penalty assessment. Where a site
       has been investigated under the SOAF and a BTKNEEC solution has been identified, the
       site is considered to be in breach until that solution has been implemented. However,
       we have not included the site in our penalty assessment if we have evidence to
       indicate that the work to implement the BTKNEEC solution is being or will be carried
       out in a timely manner.


241
   In terms of our assessment of Thames Water's IRPs and GISMPs and whether Thames Water has
taken sufficient action to warrant excluding the site from our penalty calculation, we have adopted the
following approach:
• If the only remedial work undertaken by Thames Water has been to investigate the sewer network
     to identify potential sources of infiltration (e.g. via CCTV surveys) and no further action has been
     taken, those sites have been included as part of our penalty calculation.
• If there is evidence that the company has taken remedial action, such as sealing manholes or
     lining sewers, those sites have not be included in our penalty calculation.

                                                  139
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

   4.45    In Thames Water's case, the information contained in its annual EDM returns to the
           Environment Agency and in the separate information it provided on the sites that had
           been triggered for investigation under SOAF 242 indicated that it was investigating the
           causes of high spills at its high spilling sites. The information also showed that in
           many instances, it had also identified and costed solutions to address these causes
           (which in many instances were found to be due to FFT or storm tank capacity issues).
           However, given the further slippage that has been seen in relation to the delivery of
           Thames Water's WAAP investment programme and the fact that many of the steps to
           address issues across WWTW and network which had originally been scheduled to take
           place during AMP7 (under Thames Water's Compliance First plan and now its WAAP)
           have been deferred to AMP8 means that we do not have sufficient evidence to
           demonstrate that Thames Water will implement the BTKNEEC solutions it has already
           identified within a reasonable timescale.

   4.46    As a result, we now consider that 19 sites which were high spilling due to hydraulic
           capacity issues should be included in our penalty calculations under Regulation 4(4).
           Our revised assessment is summarised in Table 4.1 below.

4.47   In summary, therefore, while the frequency with which so many of the storm overflows
       associated with Thames Water's WWTW have spilled points to a serious contravention
       of the company's obligations under Regulation 4(4) UWWTR, we have also taken
       account of the steps that the company has been taking to identify and address the
       causes of high spills under the SOAF framework as part of our penalty assessment. The
       information provided by Thames Water does appear to demonstrate that the company
       has been engaging with the SOAF process and where the reason for high spills at a
       site has been found to be due to hydraulic capacity issues, it followed up by
       considering the potential environmental impact of the high spills from those sites, to

       identified that a BTKNEEC solution does exist to address the reasons for the high spills
       at a site, that site is considered to be non-compliant with Regulation 4(4) until that
       solution is implemented.

4.48   Similarly, the fact that over 60% of sites which spilled more than 60 times were due to
       operational and asset maintenance issues in itself points to a contravention of the
       company's obligations under Regulation 4(4) UWWTR and the company's failure to take
       appropriate action to mitigate or remedy the issues at so many of these sites (as it is
       required to do under SOAF) indicates a significant ongoing breach of these
       obligations.

4.49   On the basis of the evidence we have gathered during our investigation, therefore, we
       have concluded that Thames Water has contravened and is continuing to contravene
       the requirements of Regulation 4(4) UWWTR by failing to ensure that its WWTW are
       designed, constructed, operated and maintained to ensure sufficient performance
       under all normal local climatic conditions.

4.50   Our analysis of storm overflow EDM data shows that over 70% (171) of Thames Water's
       WWTW have storm overflows associated with them that have spilled on such a regular
       basis that these spills could not be considered to be exceptional. At its 70 highest
       spilling sites, which have been investigated under SOAF, it failed to address asset
       performance issues at 31 of these overflows. Where it identified potential solutions to
       address hydraulic capacity problems at these sites, it has failed to implement them in
       a timely manner at 19 of the storm overflows.

Regulation 4(2) and Schedule 2: Failure to limit discharges from storm
overflows on its network (collecting systems)

4.51   While our investigation has focused primarily on compliance at Thames Water's
       WWTW, it is also clear from the evidence that we have obtained that there are issues in
       relation to the design, construction and maintenance of the systems which are used to
       collect and bring wastewater to its WWTW.

4.52   As set out in in Section 3 the number of recorded spills at storm overflows at Thames
       Water's sewage pumping stations and on its network were significantly lower than
       those recorded at storm overflows associated with its WWTW. Nevertheless, in 2021,
       30% of those storm overflows (69) recorded 20 or more spills (reducing to 16% and
       24% in 2022 and 2023 respectively). In line with the approach we have set out above
       on our assessment of spills at the storm overflows associated with Thames Water's
       WWTW, these storm overflows are considered to be operating in breach of Regulation
       4(2) UWWTR unless there is evidence that no environmental harm occurred as a result



                                                143
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       of the spills and that, having investigated, Thames Water has determined that a
       BTKNEEC solution to address the cause of the spills is not available.

4.53   However, as explained in Section 3, we consider that the true scale of spills from storm
       overflows on Thames Water's network is likely to be much higher than that currently
       reported. This is because in its annual return to the Environment Agency for 2022, it
       listed 312 additional storm overflows which it had not previously identified and which
       did not have monitors in place. The vast majority of these storm overflows (307) were
       identified as being on its network and Thames Water also revealed that 196 252 of these
       overflows did not have permits in place.

4.54   In terms of our detailed findings, we note again that Thames Water did not attribute
       the relevant spills to exceptional circumstances. In terms of our penalty assessment,
       for the 14 storm overflows which spilled more than 60 times and were triggered for
       investigation under SOAF, all 14 had SOAF Stage 1 assessment completed. The root
       cause of spills for three 253 of those was attributed to asset maintenance issues and at
       two of the three sites, the underlying cause was identified as infiltration.

       associated with Thames Water's WWTW we have considered whether Thames Water

             of Regulation 4(2) UWWTR.

       b)    For the two sites where infiltration was the reason for the high spills 255, while
             Thames Water does have IRPs/GISMPs in place, at one of those sites 256 there is

4.57   While the number of storm overflows on Thames Water's network that breached, and
       continued to breach, Regulation 4(2) is relatively small based on our assessment of the
       information contained in the Environment Agency's annual EDM reports and the
       additional information provided by Thames Water on sites which have been
       investigated as part of the SOAF process, in our view, Thames Water's breach is far
       more significant than this.

4.58   As noted previously, in its 2022 return to the Environment Agency Thames Water
       included 307 storm overflows on its network which it had not previously been
       identified. These overflows were unmonitored and in many cases were also
       unpermitted. As we explained in Section 2, a prevalence of unpermitted and
       unmonitored storm overflows is indicative of a company's failure to ensure that its
       network has not been constructed or maintained in accordance with the BTKNEEC
       standard to ensure limitation of pollution from storm overflows. In this case, the
       number of such storm overflows is significant (that is, 307 or 57.2% of all the storm
       overflows that Thames Water had identified on its network) and we consider that the
       company's conduct amounted to a breach of Regulation 4(2) and Schedule 2 UWWTR.
       While Thames Water is in the process of investigating its unpermitted overflows as
       part of its uCSO programme 264, we consider that absent evidence that these overflows

       did not result in environmental harm or it would be disproportionately costly to remedy

       UWWTR.

Thames Water's failure to act to ensure its wastewater assets are
designed, constructed, operated and maintained to meet its UWWTR
obligations

4.59   As already explained above, Regulation 4 UWWTR not only sets out the requirements
       for the design, construction, maintenance and operation of wastewater assets (with
       the underlying goal of preventing environmental harm), but also requires the
       companies to ensure that its assets are UWWTR compliant.

4.60   The scale and duration of potential non-compliance that our investigation has found
       at so many of Thames Water's sites, and across so many aspects of its operation and
       management of its wastewater infrastructure, is extremely concerning.

4.61   This is particularly so given the fact that the company was already aware of the
       consequences of failing to operate its assets to meet environmental permit conditions
       and the impact that these failings can have on the environment. As noted in Section 3,
       in 2017 Thames Water was fined £20.3 million by the Environment Agency as a result of
       serious and prolonged pollution incidents on the river Thames due to unpermitted
       discharges of untreated sewage from its WWTW. The company's failure to manage its
       FFT compliance was a key aspect of those prosecutions.

4.62   Following those prosecutions, and engagement with the Environment Agency, in 2017
       Thames Water undertook an estate-wide assessment of its compliance risks. While
       this focused primarily on FFT compliance, it also considered a number of other metrics
       (which are relevant indicators of UWWTR compliance) to reflect asset health, as well
       as factors such as the sensitivity of the watercourse that the WWTW was discharging
       into. While this assessment had rated more than 100 sites as red or amber in terms of
       potential compliance risks, Thames Water chose to prioritise action at fewer than 30 of
       these under its G2G programme. This also meant that the bulk of its WWTW, which it
       knew had compliance risks or which were at heightened risk of non-compliance, were
       not prioritised for resolution and so, were not addressed with any urgency.



However, if it does locate a storm or emergency overflow that is in use, Thames Water seeks a permit
from the Environment Agency.
Thames Water told us that it was targeting the end November 2024 to have all existing known
unconsented CSOs either permitted or sealed.
In a further update provided as part of its September 2024 response to the consultation on our
proposed findings, it told us that at that point, as a result of its investigations, the number of
unconsented CSOs had further reduced to 82, with 26 of these having had permit applications
submitted to the Environment Agency, with the remainder still under investigation.


                                                 148
              Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                                  on Thames Water

      4.63    Thames Water claimed that it had always intended G2G to be a multi-AMP investment
              programme so that compliance risks at other WWTW would have been prioritised in
              subsequent investment rounds (or that sites may have obtained funding from other
              capital investment programmes which were designed to fund planned or reactive
              works at treatment sites). However, based on the evidence we have seen, it is clear
              that many of the sites which had a compliance risk rating of red or amber under the
              original 2017 G2G assessment are sites which were also rated red or amber when the
              company repeated its G2G risk assessment in 2020 and are also sites which are now
              amongst Thames Water's 157 sites of concern.

      4.64    Even for those sites which Thames Water had identified as of the highest priority
              because of their compliance risk and even where investment needs were clearly
              identified, the evidence we have seen indicates that measures to address compliance
              risk were not progressed with sufficient urgency and compliance issues at many of
              these sites are yet to be resolved.

      4.65    As we have set out in this document, our main concern is that, despite its awareness
              of long-standing compliance risks across its WWTW, Thames Water failed to address
              these risks with sufficient urgency. We have not seen evidence to indicate that it had
              sought additional funding (either via the price control or via its own shareholders) to
              enable it to resolve known compliance risks more rapidly. Indeed, even when other
              sources of funding became available under the Green Recovery programme 265 or via
              the Accelerated Infrastructure Delivery Project, Thames Water did not seek to secure
              additional investment to improve its WWTW assets or the performance of its storm
              overflows.

      4.66    Thames Water has acknowledged that for those sites which it had identified as being
              of the highest priority under the G2G programme, and for which funding had been
              obtained, progress was slow in the early stages of AMP7. This, it claims, was due in
              part to the impact that Covid 19 had had on the company – an impact that was outside
              its control. We accept that Covid 19 did impact on Thames Water's business, as it did
              on other water companies. However, it was not the only factor cited by the company as
              a reason for the delay in the early stages of AMP7. The other factor was the
              introduction of a new capital delivery model and this was a factor that the company
              should have anticipated, planned for and managed to ensure that it did not have a
              negative impact on delivery.

      4.67    Furthermore, while we recognise that Thames Water is now taking steps to address its
              compliance risks across its WWTW estate and network and is proposing significant

265
      Thames Water did obtain funding via the Green Recovery programme to support the roll-out of smart
       water meters. It failed to obtain any funding under the Accelerated Infrastructure Delivery Project (due
       to lack of sufficient detail) and does not appear to have requested any funding for projects to address
       discharges from storm overflows (its proposals related primarily to water resilience).


                                                         149
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       investment during AMP8 under its WAAP, we also note that previously it had told us
       that (as part of what was originally its Compliance First plan) its plans to address
       issues at a significant number of its sites of concern would be delivered in AMP7. This
       means, therefore, a further delay in the company's progress to resolve its compliance
       issues. It also means that compliance issues at many of its sites will have continued,
       unresolved, for a very extended period of time.

4.68   In light of the above, we consider that Thames Water failed to adequately ensure that
       its assets were compliant with the UWWTR, which itself amounts to a breach of
       Regulation 4(4) UWWTR and Regulation 4(2) UWWTR.

Section 94 WIA91: Failure to effectually deal with the contents of sewers

4.69   As explained in Section 2, each breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR itself constitutes a
       breach of section 94 WIA91. However, in addition to the UWWTR breaches identified
       above, we have also considered whether the company has breached its general duties
       under section 94 WIA91. The magnitude and extent of Thames Water’s contraventions
       of the UWWTR is significant, widespread and has persisted over an extended period.
       Accordingly, we are satisfied that this indicates a systemic failure to make appropriate
       provision for effectually dealing with the contents of sewers and, therefore,
       constitutes a breach of the general duty under section 94 WIA91.

4.70   In coming to this conclusion, we have relied on the following conduct:

        •   Thames Water identified 157 potentially FFT non-compliant sites. This equates to
            almost two thirds of all its sites which have FFT permit conditions or TDCs. Of
            these sites, Thames Water’s analysis demonstrated that virtually all of these
            potentially non-compliant sites have multiple root causes for failing FFT
            performance, including data quality issues, poor flow control and management,
            and lack of treatment resilience such as inadequate maintenance or the capacity
            of assets no longer being sufficient to meet local demand. In addition, Thames
            Water identified issues with storm tank capacity at more than a third of its sites
            of concern as well as an increasing number of sites which had failed, or were at
            risk of failing, to meet DWF permit conditions. In our assessment, this is
            representative of a widespread and persistent failure to meet Thames Water’s
            obligations under Regulation 4(4) UWWTR.

        •   The magnitude and duration of spills from storm overflows associated with its
            WWTW, the majority of which appear to be due to operational and asset
            maintenance issues which should be within Thames Water's control, also point to
            a breach of the company's obligations under Regulation 4(4) UWWTR. In 2021, for
            example, over 70% of storm overflows associated with its WWTW had spilled 20 or
            more times. While recognising the steps that Thames Water has taken at its high
            spilling sites under the SOAF process, our overall assessment is that Thames


                                                150
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

            Water has failed to limit discharges from its storm overflows and this failure has
            been widespread and persistent.

        •   The identification of over 300 storm overflows on its collecting systems, which
            have been unmonitored and, in many cases, also unpermitted, indicates a large-
            scale failure by the company to ensure its wastewater assets are managed,
            operated and maintained to meet its regulatory obligations and, in our
            assessment, constitutes a failure of its obligations under Regulation 4(2) UWWTR.

        •   Thames Water continued to operate with the knowledge that failing to prioritise
            investment at a significant number of WWTW with known compliance risks would
            mean that these WWTW would continue to be non-compliant or at greater risk of
            non-compliance and did not take sufficient action. As highlighted above, many of
            the sites with a red or amber rating under the original 2017 G2G assessment are
            sites which were also rated red or amber when the company repeated its G2G risk
            assessment in 2020 and are also sites which are amongst Thames Water's 157
            sites of concern. These issues were clearly widespread, and the evidence
            available to us indicates that Thames Water persistently failed to ensure that its
            WWTW met its UWWTR obligations and its obligations under section 94 WIA91.

4.71   In summary, therefore, based on the evidence obtained during the course of our
       investigation, and for the reasons outlined above, we have concluded that, from at
       least 2017, Thames Water failed, and continues to fail, to comply with its obligations
       under Regulation 4(4), Regulations 4(2) and Schedule 2 of the UWWTR and its duties
       under section 94 WIA91.

4.72   While Thames Water has acknowledged that, historically, its processes and
       governance arrangement could have been better, to date, it has not acknowledged
       that the scale and duration of compliance issues across its WWTW estate (and the
       likely impact that this non-compliance has had on the environment) constitutes a
       breach of its duties under the UWWTR and section 94 WIA91.

4.73   We have considered Thames Water's response to our draft decision and have taken
       account of the new information and evidence it has provided. However, in reaching
       our findings in relation to the extent to which the company has complied, and
       continues to comply with, its obligations under section 94, we have based our
       assessment on the magnitude and extent of Thames Water's potential non-compliance
       in relation to FFT permit conditions; the evidence on the increasing number of its sites
       which have failed (or are at risk of failing) to meet DWF permit conditions as well as
       the significant number of its sites that are spilling to the environment frequently. In
       our view, this evidence points to a systemic failure to make appropriate provision for
       effectually dealing with the contents of sewers and constitutes an ongoing breach of
       section 94 WIA91.



                                                151
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

4.74   We also consider that, despite Thames Water's repeated claims to the contrary, it has
       not behaved in a manner we would expect of a reasonable company. In our view, it
       should have been aware of, and alert to, the risk that a failure to meet FFT and other
       permit conditions and its spills and pollution incidents performance might mean for
       the company's wider regulatory obligations. Not only had the company been the
       subject of previous prosecutions due to the environmental impact of the failure of a
       number of its sites to treat sufficient FFT, but it was also aware of, and had considered,
       the implications for the company as a result of Ofwat's decision in the Southern Water
       enforcement case. In doing so, it had identified a number of risks which it failed to
       address with sufficient urgency. As a result of those failures, and despite the fact that
       companies were funded to meet existing environmental permits under the price
       control, Thames Water is now in a position where almost two thirds of all its sites with
       FFT permit conditions and TDCs are potentially non-compliant. This means that sites
       may have been discharging untreated wastewater when they should not be doing so
       and, in circumstances which were not due to exceptional or unforeseeable events.

Findings in respect of Condition P: Failure to have adequate financial
and management resources and systems of planning and internal
control to enable it to secure the carrying out of its regulated activities

4.75   The breaches we have identified above demonstrate that Thames Water has failed to
       properly carry out its regulated activities, in particular, to design, construct, operate
       and maintain its WWTW and the collecting systems that supply its WWTW in order to
       minimise harm to the environment. We consider that this is due to the failures in, and
       the inadequacy of, the financial and management resources and systems of planning
       and internal controls that it put towards these regulated activities, contrary to the
       company's obligations under Condition P of its Licence (as explained in Section 2).

4.76   Condition P means that Thames Water needs to have understood its legal and
       regulatory obligations and have put in place appropriate processes and controls to
       ensure that it was able to operate its wastewater assets in a manner that complied
       with those obligations.

4.77   It should also have in place systems to ensure that any non-compliance (and the risk
       of non-compliance) is escalated within the company and that prompt, timely
       investigations are carried out and remedial action taken to secure compliance. It
       should also have internal controls in place that follow-up these interventions to ensure
       that compliance is achieved in a timely and effective manner.

4.78   Thames Water should have in place appropriate processes to ensure that its
       operational staff have the necessary information to identify potential issues and that it
       has the right culture and processes in place to ensure that remediation of these issues
       is prioritised. This includes making sure that it has appropriate checks and balances
       to assure monitoring and reporting information is accurate. Thames Water should also

                                                152
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       have in place processes to ensure that its Executive and Board request and receive the
       information they need to monitor the company's compliance with its environmental
       obligations and that sufficient time and resource are put to engaging meaningfully
       with this.

4.79   Based on the evidence we have seen, we have concluded that Thames Water has had:

       •   inadequate systems of planning and internal controls and management resources
           in place to enable it to understand and effectively manage its compliance with its
           environmental obligations (both with respect to its environmental permits and the
           UWWTR / section 94) at its WTTW and on its network;

       •   inadequate financial and management resources in place to adequately ensure
           that its WWTW and its network continue to be designed, constructed, operated and
           maintained to meet local conditions, and that solutions to non-compliance are
           identified and addressed in a timely way; and

       •   inadequate processes of assurance, controls and governance to ensure and
           challenge the completeness and integrity of WWTW and network asset compliance
           reporting, including at Board and Executive level.

4.80   As set out in Section 3, during the course of our investigation, Thames Water identified
       more and more sites which were potentially non-compliant with their FFT permit
       conditions. This was despite the previous compliance risk assessments that the
       company had undertaken under its G2G programme and despite the robust processes
       and procedures that it claimed to have had in place to identify and manage its
       compliance risks.

4.81   When asked to provide an explanation or root cause of what had gone wrong to result
       in the company ending up with potential FFT non-compliance on the scale it has now
       identified, Thames Water repeatedly cited a lack of accurate data. It told us that this
       will be resolved by the roll-out of U_MON3 and U_MON4 monitors and this will improve
       its ability to manage compliance across its WWTW estate.

4.82   However, the duties and obligations imposed on companies by the UWWTR and section
       94 WIA91 have existed for many years and the onus is, and always has been, on the
       company to ensure its compliance with these obligations. How it does that, and the
       processes and procedures it has in place, to assure itself that it is complying is up to
       the company. The absence of data from U_MON3 and U_MON4 monitors does not
       explain (or excuse) how, and why, Thames Water has ended up with so many
       potentially non-compliant sites. Rather, the scale of the potential non-compliance
       now identified points to a failure of the company's systems of planning and control to
       enable it to assure itself of its actual compliance position and a failure by the company



                                                153
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       to be more diligent in its enquiries as to what was actually going on across its WWTW
       and network estate.

4.83   It is obvious from the evidence we have seen that Thames Water did not have a clear
       view or understanding about the state of its wastewater assets; about how those
       assets were actually performing; and, therefore, about whether its WWTW and the
       networks supplying those sites were being managed and operated in a manner which
       was compliant, not only with environmental permits, but also with the company's
       overarching obligations under the UWWTR and section 94 WIA91. These are not the
       actions of a reasonable company.

4.84   Furthermore, while Thames Water has cited a lack of data from flow monitors as a key
       factor in the potential non-compliance it has now identified, it is also evident that its
       inability to access data which could have helped it to have a better understanding of
       the performance of its wastewater assets was due, at least in part, to failings of its own
       systems of management and control. As set out previously, the company began
       installing EDMs in AMP6, but in Autumn 2018 it discovered problems with the quality of
       the data being collected from these monitors. The ultimate root cause of these
       problems was found to be a new capital delivery model the company introduced in
       AMP6 which, according to Thames Water, had led to a lack of assurance,
       communication and governance of installation requirements. While Thames Water has
       informed us that it has now resolved these issues and has introduced a range of new
       processes and procedures to avoid problems arising again, these issues did have a
       significant impact on the company's ability to access reliable data on spills to the
       environment and arose from its own inadequate management of its supplier for this
       work. This in turn impacted on the ability of the company to use this data, in
       conjunction with the data from its TDV/FE monitors, to obtain a more accurate picture
       of what was happening at its sites in terms of flow compliance.

4.85   There were also other sources of information and other indicators that should have
       alerted Thames Water that things might be going wrong and that should have
       prompted further, more diligent enquiry by its management into the company's true
       compliance position. This included, for example, evidence about the number of
       serious pollution incidents being recorded by the company; evidence that an
       increasing number of its WWTW were operating with little or no headroom and at risk
       of failing to meet DWF permit limits and what this might also mean in terms of FFT
       permit compliance and the increased risk of spills of untreated wastewater to the
       environment; and evidence from the mounting concerns being raised, and analysis
       being provided, by local action groups and citizen scientists about the scale and
       duration of discharges of untreated wastewater from its WWTW and the impact that
       these discharges were having on local watercourses .




                                                154
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

4.86   It is also clear that, despite Thames Water's claims about the robustness of its
       processes and procedures, a number of its own internal reviews 266 paint a different
       picture and suggest that the company was aware of, at least some of, the
       shortcomings in its systems and controls. They also indicate a failure of the company
       to learn the lessons from previous enforcement action (both against Thames Water
       and other companies), and a failure to address the issues identified with sufficient
       urgency.

4.87   These internal reviews are described in more detail in Section 3 but, in general, point
       to significant issues and risks in the company's compliance regime and across many
       areas of the business, from operations and asset management, to staff training and
       awareness, to reporting, oversight and governance. In a September 2019 report to
       Thames Water's Audit, Risk and Reporting Committee (ARRC) following the publication
       of our Southern Water enforcement decision, for example, it was noted that operating
       awareness of legal requirements and permit compliance obligations was immature,
       with a focus on numeric effluent parameters (that is, "end of pipe" or final effluent
       quality metrics 267), and that, in many areas, the delivery of compliant activities was
       reliant on operator instinct. Even in 2021, another internal audit report highlighted
       that there were no formal training programmes to deliver key technical competencies
       for staff which would consistently provide employees with greater knowledge and
       competence to reduce and prevent pollutions, reduce the risk of ineffective decision
       making which could impact on the environment, and reduce the risk of Thames Water
       being in breach of legal and regulatory obligations.

4.88   These risks in the company's systems and controls were known to the company's
       Executive and Board, as was the fact that the Board did not have full sight of other
       aspects of the company's compliance with its environmental obligations. The
       September 2019 ARRC paper concluded, for example, that in relation to Condition P
       "we believe there are strong controls for end-of-pipe parameters, but there is more we
       can do to improve our control of processes and culture to be compliant with the full
       remit of environmental permits and other environmental legislation". This conclusion
       was based on the finding that while information on end-of-pipe compliance, pollutions
       and enforcement was reported monthly to the Board, parameters other than ‘end-of-
       pipe’ were not reported. This, according to the review findings, raised the risk that
       these compliance failures were potentially unknown.

4.89   Despite this, the company does not appear to have acted in a manner we would expect
       of a reasonable and diligent company or responded with any urgency to address these

       known risks. The papers we have seen from 2019 onwards indicate, that until recently,

       fed into the company's EPA report or were relevant to the company's price review PCs
       and ODIs. It would also appear from the evidence provided to us that even when issues
       around FFT compliance were discussed by Board, the focus was on the level and
       timing of investment under the G2G programme rather than on the need for the
       company to restore compliance at all of its sites as a matter of urgency rather than
       deferring action to address known non-compliance risks, which is what the company
       actually did 268.

4.90   As a result, there was a lack of awareness of, and insufficient challenge applied to,
       WWTW and network performance data at Board level. Moreover, throughout this time,
       and despite the risks highlighted above, the Board continued to provide an annual
       ring-fencing certificate to Ofwat to assure us that the company had adequate

       carrying out of its regulated activities.

4.91   While the company did highlight a number of material instances of non-compliance
       and missed targets and commitments in its annual Risk and Compliance Statement
       (RCS), as explained earlier, it continued to focus on indicators or parameters which
       fed into its EPA report or were relevant to the company's PCs and ODIs such as number
       of pollution incidents or number of fines or Environment Agency prosecutions for
       breaches under the Environmental Permitting Regulations. In its 2019 RCS, for
       example, Thames Water noted that it had failed to meet its PC on WWTW discharge
       compliance. This largely relates to compliance with "end-of-pipe" effluent quality.
       There was no reference to the company's performance in relation to flow compliance
       and none of the documents which were provided to Ofwat as part of the annual
       certification process over the period 2019 to 2021 highlighted the ongoing compliance
       risk that the company was exposed to by the failure of a significant number of its
       WWTW to meet their FFT permit conditions, despite internal reviews during that period
       demonstrating that this was known to the company.

4.92   While more recently the company has begun to focus more on its performance in
       terms of pollution and spills to the environment, it would appear that it was only as a
       result of our initial request for information for our investigation in November 2021 that
       the company fully acknowledged, and took steps to address, the fact that there were
       other material areas where it was non-compliant with its environmental obligations

   Thames Water has told us that its work on identifying and resolving flow to full treatment issues

       (including non-compliance with FFT permit conditions) which should be included in

4.93   Even for the sites which Thames Water had identified as being of the highest priority
       because of their compliance risks, progress to address and resolve issues has been
       slow. Thames Water has acknowledged this but cited a change in its capital delivery
       model and Covid 19 as factors for delivery delays in the early part of AMP7. While
       recognising that Covid did impact on all companies within the sector, any potential
       delivery risks due to the introduction of a new capital delivery model is something that
       the company should have anticipated, planned for and managed to ensure that it did
       not negatively impact on its ability to meet its regulatory obligations. Moreover, while
       Thames Water has denied that this was the case, a number of internal documents also
       suggest that the speed with which the company chose to address compliance risks
       was also impacted by its decisions to prioritise spending on other areas of the
       business or to focus on those areas which had, historically, been subject to greater
       regulatory scrutiny 270. In our view, addressing compliance risks was not sufficiently
       prioritised in Thames Water's investment decisions.

4.94   Moreover, while Thames Water has sought to challenge our finding (including in its
       response to our draft decision), as noted previously, this mirrors the findings of its own
       internal review following the publication of our Southern Water case. That review
       concluded that historic prioritisation of capital maintenance had not put robust and
       resilient environmental compliance with all permit/consent conditions as a high
       priority, instead concentrating on 'end-of-pipe' compliance, and recommended that
       the governance behind capital spend and investment should formally consider and
       prioritise compliance requirements moving forward.

4.95   When questioned about its decision making on the level and timing of investment to
       address compliance risk, Thames Water told us that it had sought to balance a
       number of the company's risks and competing priorities when making these decisions,
       including tackling leakage, addressing pollutions, improving service and reducing the
       risk of asset failure. It noted too that in line with its systems and procedures for
       making investment decisions, priorities were driven by an assessment of available
       funding and outcomes, based on the data available.

4.96   Even when assessing the potential legal and regulatory implications of Ofwat's
       Southern Water case for the company and, whilst recognising the risk of enforcement
       action by the Environment Agency ("if we do not deliver on our commitment to
       improve, such as through ‘Go to Green’" and from Ofwat due to the fact that "there are
       some areas where it is not known whether we are fully compliant with our Section 94
       obligations"), the company, nevertheless, considered that "its actions were those of a
       reasonable company within the prioritisation of limited investment funds" 271.

4.97   This indicates that even when Thames Water was aware of its compliance risks, it
       failed to address these risks in an appropriate and timely manner and therefore, in
       effect, chose to accept ongoing non-compliance across a significant portion of its
       WWTW estate.

4.98   As highlighted previously, Thames Water has claimed repeatedly that it has always
       acted reasonably within the constraints of the price review process and always
       intended its G2G programme to be multi-AMP so compliance risks at other sites would
       have been prioritised in subsequent investment rounds. However, as we have made
       clear in our determinations for PR24, companies, including Thames Water, were
       already funded to meet environmental permit levels as part of the price review
       process. Moreover, compliance with legal and regulatory obligations is something
       which companies are required to meet as a minimum. It is not a choice that
       companies can make or an obligation they can decide to "de-prioritise".

4.99   Based on the evidence that we have seen, therefore, we have concluded that, from at
       least March 2017, Thames Water failed to act in the manner best calculated to ensure
       it had adequate financial and management resources and systems of planning and
       internal control in place to carry out its regulated activities, and in particular to
       manage and effectively operate its WWTW and networks to meet their environmental
       obligations under their permits and the UWWTR/Section 94.

4.100 As required by Condition P, we would have expected a company to have in place
      adequate systems and controls in order to prevent, identify and address the issues of
      the type outlined in this case (including, for example, ensuring that it was operating
      and managing its assets to ensure that "asset maintenance" was not the reason for so
      many of its storm overflows spilling so frequently). It is also clear that the company
      was aware of at least some of these shortcomings and did not respond swiftly enough
      to address them. It could also have done more to have a better understanding of those
      aspects of its performance it was not yet aware of until revealed during our
      investigation.




  Lines of Defence for Regulatory Wastewater Compliance: Paper discussed by ARRC, 11 September
271

2019.

                                                158
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

4.101 Ofwat considers that Thames Water neglected its obligations in this case. The Board of
      Thames Water placed reliance on a variety of teams and systems to understand the
      performance of its WWTW and networks. These systems and processes were ineffective
      and focused on a relatively narrow set of indicators or parameters which fed into the
      company's EPA report or were relevant to the company's PCs and ODIs. As a result, the
      Board did not seek sufficient information to adequately assure itself, or Ofwat via its
      annual certificate, as to the company's full compliance position, nor to sufficiently
      scrutinise performance in order to drive improvements and secure compliance.

4.102 Thames Water has challenged our assessment that its management team and Board
      did not receive, nor seek, sufficient information and assurance on the operational
      performance of the company’s wastewater assets and their compliance with relevant
      legal obligations. It also rejected any suggestion that it had improperly assured Ofwat
      in relation to the adequacy of its resources and systems of planning and internal
      control in its RFC and its RCS. In its view, the company has, at all time, followed Ofwat
      guidance in relation to the preparation of the RFC and has made suitable disclosures –
      including disclosures relating to permit compliance in its RFC for 2021 -2022 and
      2022-23.

4.103 However, despite Thames Water's challenges and claims to the contrary, as noted
      previously, these are issues and risks that were highlighted by its own internal reviews
      and reports. In 2019, for example, an internal audit report272 had found that "the
      control environment is unsatisfactory and needs major improvements to reduce the
      organisation's exposure to risk to an acceptable level for ensuring compliance with
      Environmental Permit Regulations. We have found that governance and ownership is
      unclear and incomplete, performance reporting has a bias towards pollutions and is
      not designed to pick up other compliance areas". Similarly it concluded that "as with
      governance and oversight, monitoring of performance was mainly aligned to EPA
      requirements and processes that existed under the previous consent regime that
      focus on the quality of wastewater treatment rather than flow management".273

4.104 We have set out the evidence we have relied on in reaching our findings in relation to
      Thames Water's compliance with Condition P in Section 3. This evidence is drawn
      mainly from Thames Water's Board papers and minutes and, in our assessment,
      indicates that, until relatively recently, the focus of the management team and Board

      was on metrics relevant to the Environment Agency's EPA and the company's PCs and
      ODIs under the price control and not on its wider legal obligations. Despite Thames
      Water's challenge, our conclusion also mirrors the findings of the company's own
      internal audit reports which highlighted the risks arising from the company's narrow
      focus on a limited number of metrics.

4.105 Similarly, in relation to Thames Water's RFCs, while we recognise that the company
      has now taken steps to report on risks associated with its wider regulatory obligations,
      it only began to do so for the 2021-22 RFC – which post-dates the opening of our
      investigation. Prior to that, and despite the compliance risks which had been
      identified under the company's G2G programme, these were not referred to in earlier
      RFCs.

4.106 Furthermore, while we also recognise the steps that Thames Water has taken and is
      taking to improve its processes, oversight and governance, the fact that the company
      considers that these changes are needed also serves to underline the fact that there
      were deficiencies in its previous processes and systems.

4.107 While Thames Water has sought to argue that even if it had breached Condition P in
      the past, because of the changes and range of measures it had now introduced to
      improve its processes, oversight and governance, there is no ongoing breach.
      However, whilst we welcome the changes that Thames Water has made and the steps
      that has taken/is taking to improve its processes, oversight and governance, it is too
      early to conclude with any degree of confidence that the changes that it has now put
      in place are sufficient to enable us to conclude that there is no ongoing breach of
      Condition P. This is because of the fact that the company appears to be still
      uncovering new potential issues and its understanding of the steps that it needs to
      take, and the investment that this will require, is still evolving.

5. Our decision to issue an enforcement order

Background
5.1       Under section 18 WIA91, if we are satisfied that a regulated company is contravening
          or likely to contravene a condition of its Licence or a statutory requirement we are
          under the duty to make an enforcement order containing such provisions as are
          requisite to secure compliance with that condition or requirement.

5.2       Under section 19 WIA91, we are not required to make an enforcement order if we are
          satisfied that the contravention in question is of a trivial nature, or is contrary to our
          duties in Part I WIA91, including our duty to have regard to best regulatory practice
          and our other duties under section 2 WIA91. We may also instead or in part accept
          formal undertakings from a company to take remedial steps as long as we consider
          them appropriate to secure compliance. A company's obligation to comply with an
          undertaking is treated as a statutory requirement which is enforceable by Ofwat under
          section 18 WIA91.

5.3       We do not consider that any of the exceptions in section 19 WIA91 apply and we are
          therefore under a duty to make an enforcement order in this case. The breaches
          identified are significant. We are disappointed that Thames Water has not offered
          formal undertakings which would displace our duty to make an enforcement order. In
          making this decision, we have taken account of Thames Water's financial and
          operational issues alongside our section 2 WIA91 duties more generally including the
          interests of customers and the environment in a restoration of compliance as quickly
          and efficiently as reasonably possible. Our enforcement order is specifically designed
          to take account of issues of affordability, deliverability and financeability in the steps
          and timescales for delivering compliance.

5.4       Under section 108 of the Deregulation Act 2014, in carrying out our enforcement duty
          under section 18 WIA we must have regard to the desirability of promoting economic
          growth, and in particular in a way which ensures that regulatory action is only taken
          where needed; and any action is proportionate. We are also required to have regard to
          any guidance that the secretary of state may issue in relation to the application of this
          duty.

5.5       We note that in the introduction to the duty 274 the current guidance states that the
          duty does not legitimise non-compliance with other duties or objectives, and its
          purpose is not to achieve or pursue economic growth at the expense of necessary
          protections. We also note that it recognises that enforcement action can be necessary
          to protect public health or the environment although care should be taken in relation


274
      Department for Business & Trade, Growth Duty: Statutory Guidance – Refresh, 21 May 2024, page 4.

                                                    161
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       to the actions employed and remedies imposed to ensure that they are proportionate,
       reasonable, not dissuasive to investment and take account of willingness to comply.

5.6    In pursuance of the duties described above, and having regard to the growth duty
       guidance, we have therefore sought to ensure our enforcement action in this case is
       proportionate to the extent of non-compliance we have found and in order that the
       company returns to compliance as quickly and efficiently as reasonably possible,
       consistent with what the company can reasonably deliver.

5.7    Under section 22 WIA91, where a company fails to comply with an enforcement order,
       Ofwat may apply to the court to seek an injunction to compel a company to do so or for
       other forms of appropriate relief. Section 22 also makes clear that the obligation to
       comply with an enforcement order constitutes a duty owed to any person who may be
       affected by a contravention of the order. That person may bring legal proceedings
       against a company if a breach of that duty causes them to sustain loss or damage.

Competition Act 1998

5.8    As mentioned in Section 2, under sections 19(1A) and 19(2A) WIA91, before deciding to
       make an enforcement order or confirm a provisional enforcement order, Ofwat is
       required to consider whether it would be more appropriate for us to proceed under the
       CA98. Ofwat is satisfied that it is more appropriate to make an order pursuant to
       section 18 WIA91 in this case because there are specific statutory and Llicence
       provisions closely related to the issues identified in this investigation which we can
       enforce under that section.

The enforcement order in this case
5.9    As explained in detail in Section 4 above, we have found that Thames Water had acted
       in breach of its duties under the UWWTR, section 94 WIA91 and Condition P of its
       Licence. Accordingly, at Appendix A1 we enclose an enforcement order (the Order)
       which sets out the terms we consider necessary to ensure compliance with these
       provisions. As section 18(1)(b) WIA91 requires us to act also when a company is likely
       to contravene the relevant obligations and we have identified a number of behaviours
       which are likely to give rise to breaches in the future, the Order also sets out a list of
       factors which we consider should prompt the companies to assess their compliance
       going forward.


Effects of the proposed Order

5.10   The Order imposes six broad requirements on Thames Water that require it to address
       the ongoing breaches of its legal and regulatory obligations that we have identified in
       Section 4, and which should avoid the risk of future breaches arising. The company
       will be required to report its progress to us every six months.

                                                162
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

5.11   To support these requirements, Thames Water will be required to produce, and agree
       with us, a series of action plans that detail the steps it will take and the timescales
       within which these will be taken.

5.12   We summarise below the six main requirements and their associated actions plans.

5.13   Requirement 1 relates to WWTW and requires Thames Water to ensure and be able to
       demonstrate that all aspects of its WWTW are, and for the foreseeable future will
       continue to be, designed, constructed, operated and maintained in order to receive
       and treat the volumes of received wastewater and DWF storm sewage they receive
       from their catchment area under normal local climatic conditions, ensuring assets
       have appropriate FFT and storage levels to enable this. To support this requirement,
       Thames Water will be required to:

       •   Put in place an action plan (the WWTW Remediation Plan) that will require it to
           review its WWTW asset base for potential compliance issues, conduct root cause
           analysis and remedy any issues identified as quickly and efficiently as possible. It
           will also require Thames Water to detail the processes and procedures that it has
           in place to identify assets that are operating below their design capacity,
           investigate these and take action appropriate remedial action.

       •   Ensure and demonstrate that it has operational planning and systems in place to
           regularly review and proactively respond to catchment changes that may result in
           the design, construction, operation and/or maintenance of the WWTW becoming
           insufficient for the volumes of sewage received.

       •   Demonstrate that it has ensured that all relevant assets have environmental
           permits and that these are adequate for the volumes of sewage received at the
           WWTW. Where permits require revision, Thames Water will be required to engage
           proactively with the Environment Agency to update those permits.

5.14   Requirement 2 relates to the wastewater network and requires Thames Water to
       ensure and be able to demonstrate that all aspects of its wastewater network assets
       are, and for the foreseeable future will continue to be, designed, constructed,
       operated and maintained in order to transport to WWTW for treatment the volumes of
       received wastewater and storm sewage they receive from their catchment area under
       normal local climatic conditions. To support this requirement, Thames Water will be
       required to:

       •   Put in place an action plan (the Network Remediation Plan) that will require it to
           review its wastewater asset base for potential compliance issues, conduct root
           cause analysis and remedy any issues identified as quickly and efficiently as
           possible. It will also require Thames Water to detail the processes and procedures



                                                163
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

           that it has in place to identify assets that are operating below their design
           capacity, investigate these and take action appropriate remedial action.

       •   Ensure and demonstrate that it has operational planning and systems in place to
           regularly review and proactively respond to catchment changes that may result in
           the design, construction, operation and/or maintenance of its sewerage network
           becoming insufficient for the volumes of sewage received.

       •   Demonstrate that it has ensured that all relevant assets have environmental
           permits and that these are adequate for the volumes of sewage received in the
           sewerage network. Where permits require revision, Thames Water will be required
           to engage proactively with the Environment Agency to update those permits.

5.15   Requirement 3 requires Thames Water to ensure and be able to demonstrate that
       discharges to the environment of wastewater that have not been fully treated only
       arise as a result of exceptional circumstances or in situations where no cost beneficial
       solution exists to avoid discharges from occurring. To support this requirement,
       Thames Water will be required to:

       •   Put in place an action plan (the Overflow Plan) setting out the processes and
           procedures that it has in place to investigate and take remedial action in relation
           to high spilling storm overflows and emergency overflows.

       •   Apply the BTKNEEC test when considering solutions to minimise environmental
           harm from discharges, ensuring that the full range of remedial options have been
           assessed, that all relevant issues are considered when assessing the costs of
           environmental harm and that account is taken of the latest technological
           advances.

       •   Demonstrate that it has ensured that all storm overflows have environmental
           permits. Where storm overflows lack permits or these require revision, Thames
           Water will be required to engage proactively with the Environment Agency.

       •   Work with the Environmental Agency to develop and agree a protocol for assessing
           the environmental impact of discharges waterways that are polluted from multiple
           locations.

5.16   Requirement 4 requires Thames Water to ensure and be able to demonstrate that its
       senior management and Board actively review and challenge the company's
       compliance with its environmental permits, the UWWTR and section 94 WIA91
       obligations on a regular basis, and that they receive all the information they need to
       be able to do this. To support this requirement, Thames Water will be required to:




                                                164
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       •   Create an action plan (the Management Plan) for putting in place measures to
           ensure that the necessary compliance information is provided to its senior
           management and Board and that this information will be used to review and
           challenge the company's compliance performance.

       •   Demonstrate that it has implemented the Management Plan and is using relevant
           information to monitor and address compliance issues within the company and to
           plan for the long-term needs of its catchment.

       •   Develop and implement an annual training programme for operational staff and
           senior management to ensure that they understand the compliance requirements
           of the UWWTR and section 94 WIA91, and are familiar with the processes that need
           to be followed to report and escalate potential non-compliance within the
           company.

5.17   Requirement 5 requires Thames Water to deliver the actions set out in the plans
       agreed under Requirements 1-4 in accordance with their deadlines and terms. We
       have reserved the right to require amendments to be made to one or more of the
       agreed plans should circumstances change.

5.18   Requirement 6 requires Thames Water to report to us on a six-monthly basis on its
       progress against the requirements of the Order and to publish on its website details of
       its progress and a summary of the actions it has taken.

5.19   Thames Water will be required to present and obtain our agreement to the action
       plans discussed above within six months of the date of the Order. We will not
       unreasonably withhold our agreement to these plans but reserve the right to require
       amendments be made to one or more of the plans should circumstances change.

5.20   By virtue of section 22 WIA91, the Order would also have the following effects:

       •   The obligation to comply with an enforcement order shall be a duty owed to any
           person who may be affected by a contravention of the Order.

       •   In any proceedings brought against any company in the pursuance of paragraph
           5.6 above, other than proceedings in respect of so much of a contravention of any
           order as consists in a breach of a duty imposed by virtue of section 68(1)(a) or
           (1A)(a) WIA91, it shall be a defence for the person to show that the person took all
           reasonable steps and exercised all due diligence to avoid contravening the order.

       •   Without prejudice to any right which any person may have by virtue of paragraph
           5.6 above to bring civil proceedings in respect of any contravention or
           apprehended contravention of an enforcement order, compliance with any such
