6. Our decision to impose a financial penalty and the
broad level of that penalty

Relevant provisions of the WIA91
6.1    Under sections 22A(1)(a) and 22A(2)(a) of the WIA91, Ofwat may impose on a water or
       sewerage company a penalty of such amount (not exceeding 10% of the regulated
       company's relevant turnover in a relevant year) as is reasonable in all the
       circumstances of the case, where it is satisfied that the company has contravened or
       is contravening any condition of its Licence, or any statutory requirement Ofwat is
       responsible for enforcing.

6.2    Under section 22A(11) of the WIA91, no penalty imposed by Ofwat under section 22A
       may exceed 10% of the turnover of the company, determined in accordance with the
       Water Industry (Determination of Turnover for Penalties) Order 2005. Article 3 of that
       Order provides that for the purposes of section 22A(11) of the WIA91, the relevant
       turnover of a company is the applicable turnover for the preceding business year net
       of tax (that is, the last business year preceding the date on which Ofwat gives notice
       under section 22A(6) of the WIA91). Article 2(2) defines “applicable turnover" as “the
       amounts, determined in conformity with normal accounting practice in the United
       Kingdom, which are derived by a company from the provision of goods and services in
       the course of the company's regulated activities in respect of which the penalty is
       imposed” and Article 2(1) defines “regulated activities" as:

               “(a) in the case of a company holding an appointment as a water undertaker,
               its functions as a water undertaker;

               (b) in the case of a company holding an appointment as a sewerage
               undertaker, its functions as a sewerage undertaker;”

6.3    For the purposes of the penalty in this case we have considered the business year from
       1 April 2023 to 31 March 2024. Thames Water's applicable turnover derived from its
       activities as a sewerage company in that business year was £1,160.998 million. 275
       Accordingly, the maximum penalty that we could impose on the company would be
       10% of this number, that is, £116.10 million.


275
   Thames Water's relevant turnover of £1,160.998m was calculated as the sum of revenues from retail
wastewater services (£59.058m), wastewater network+ (£893.995m), bioresources (£201.139m), bulk
wastewater supplies (£3.110m) and other third-party wastewater revenue (£3.696m). Source: Thames
Water Annual Performance Report 2023-24, pages 104 and 107. As the reported revenues from retail
wastewater services relate to both water and wastewater services, we have estimated the proportion
for wastewater services on the basis of the ratio of Wastewater Network+ revenues to Water Network+
revenues. We have adopted the same approach in apportioning other third-party revenue.

                                                167
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

6.4       Under section 22C(1) of the WIA91 Ofwat may only impose a penalty for breaches
          which occurred within five years of the penalty being imposed, unless, before the end
          of that period, a notice under section 203 WIA91 is issued or a provisional or final
          enforcement order is issued. In this case this means that we can only apply a penalty
          in respect of a breach that occurred after 8 March 2017. 276

6.5       Ofwat is satisfied that the breaches for which a penalty is being applied fall within the
          permitted time period for imposing a penalty under section 22C(1) WIA91.

6.6       Under section 22A(6) WIA91, we must specify the acts or omissions and any “other
          facts” which we consider justify imposing a penalty for a relevant contravention, and of
          the amount proposed.

6.7       Pursuant to section 22B(2) WIA91, Ofwat must have regard to its most recently
          published statement of policy with respect to financial penalties (Statement of Policy)
          when deciding whether to impose a penalty, and its amount. The growth duty in
          section 108 of the Deregulation Act 2015 (and subsequent sections) also applies to the
          imposition of a penalty. The factors set out in our Statement of Policy have guided us
          in considering whether a penalty is necessary and proportionate consistent with that
          duty.

6.8       Section 22A(9) WIA91 provides that penalties imposed under section 22A WIA91 are
          paid into the HM Treasury's Consolidated Fund.



Competition Act 1998

6.9       As mentioned in Section 2, sections 22A(13) and 22A(14) WIA91 provide that before
          imposing a financial penalty Ofwat must consider whether it would be more
          appropriate to proceed under CA98. As stated in Section 5 above, Ofwat is satisfied
          that it is more appropriate to proceed under section 22A WIA91 given that it enables
          Ofwat to impose penalties in respect of specific statutory and Licence provisions
          closely related to the issues identified in this investigation.



Ofwat's reasons for considering it appropriate to impose a
penalty and the proposed broad level of that penalty

6.10      We are satisfied that Thames Water has contravened Regulations 4(4)(a), Regulations
          4(2) and Schedule 2 UWWTR, section 94 WIA91, and Condition P of its licence in




276
      We issued the notice under section 203(2) WIA91 on 8 March 2022.

                                                    168
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       relation to how it managed and operated its WWTW and the collecting systems that
       flow to its WWTW.

6.11   In determining whether to impose penalties for these contraventions, and their broad
       level, we have had regard to Ofwat’s Statement of Policy and in particular the following
       factors, as further detailed below:

            •   The seriousness and duration of the contraventions or failure;

            •   If the contravention or failure has damaged the interests of customers, the
                degree of harm caused and also any increased costs incurred by customers;
            •   If the application of a penalty would be likely to create an incentive to comply
                and deter future contraventions or failures;
            •   Any gains made by the company (financial or otherwise);
            •   Any damage to other market participants;
            •   Any damage caused to the environment;
            •   Whether the contraventions or failure was or is of a trivial nature;
            •   Whether the contravention or possibility of a contravention would have been
                apparent to a diligent company; and
            •   Precedents set under equivalent provisions for other utilities.

The seriousness and duration of the contraventions

6.12   Thames Water must provide sewerage services across its area of appointment in line
       with its statutory duties and licence obligations. Under the UWWTR it is required to
       ensure that its WWTW are designed, constructed, operated and maintained in
       accordance with BTKNEEC so that, under normal local climatic conditions, all
       wastewater is collected and treated sufficiently in order to minimise adverse effects
       on the environment. As a general duty, section 94 WIA91 is one of the core obligations
       of a sewerage company. Condition P is a fundamental requirement of an efficient and
       effectively operated company. We therefore consider a contravention of these
       provisions to be serious.

6.13   In considering whether breaches of the UWWTR are sufficiently serious to warrant a
       penalty, we have focused on those breaches which are also inconsistent with the
       environmental permitting regime, or which involve assets spilling above the 60-spill
       SOAF threshold, and have also considered how Thames Water has engaged with the
       expectations of the SOAF.

6.14   We note, for example, that, in relation to breaches of Regulation 4(4) Thames Water
       recorded over 60 spills at almost 30% of the storm overflows associated with its WWTW
       in 2021 which surpasses the SOAF spill thresholds. At the same time, however, we also

                                                169
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       recognise that the company has engaged with the SOAF process and is in the process
       of completing, or has completed, investigations to identify and address potential root
       causes of spills at a significant number of sites where infiltration or hydraulic capacity
       have been identified as the reason for the high spills. We have taken this into account
       as part of our overall assessment of whether the company has breached its obligations
       in this case, including its obligations under UWWTR, and whether a breach is
       sufficiently serious to warrant the imposition of a penalty in this case.

6.15   We also note, however, that a significant number of the spills at Thames Water's high
       spilling sites have been due to asset maintenance issues (other than infiltration), such
       that (absent unusual circumstances) a BTKNEEC solution for the spill, that is, the
       proper maintenance of the existing assets, was readily available. In our assessment,
       these are issues that a diligent and reasonable company should be taking proactive
       steps to prevent and, if issues still arise, should be taking urgent remedial action to
       address.

6.16   On balance, therefore, and given the range of other factors that we considered when
       arriving at our decision, we remain of the view that Thames Water's has breached
       Regulation 4(4) and Regulation 4(2) of UWWTR, Section 94 WIA91 and Condition P of its
       licence. We also consider that nature and scale of these breaches warrant a
       significant penalty.

6.17   We also note that, despite the Board's awareness of issues at its WWTW and on its
       network and shortcomings in its compliance regime, it continued until relatively
       recently to assure us about the adequacy of its resources and systems of planning and
       internal control in the annual Ring-Fencing Certificate (RFC) and Risk and Compliance
       Statement (RCS).

6.18   We are satisfied that the breaches identified in this investigation occurred from at
       least March 2017 onwards. Thames Water has been aware of potential compliance
       risks at a significant number of its WWTW since at least 2017 when it established its
       G2G programme following prosecution by the Environment Agency. While we cannot
       be certain of the duration of issues at individual WWTW sites and spills from individual
       storm overflows on the network, the evidence shows that a number of WWTW sites
       were rated red or amber in the company's 2017 and that the failure to limit pollution
       from storm overflows has persisted for several years.

If the contravention or failure has damaged the interests of customers,
the degree of harm caused, and also any increased costs incurred by
customers

6.19   The evidence obtained during the course of our investigation indicates that Thames
       Water's customers have not received the level of service that they are entitled to from
       a statutory provider of sewerage services.

                                                170
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

6.20   Our decision is not based on any conclusions as to the degree of harm caused to the
       environment or customers as a result of the failures we have identified. As spills
       arising from Thames Water's failures occurred in the past, it is difficult for us to
       definitively establish whether there was customer harm at the time as a result of
       those spills 277. However, as we explain in our Statement of Policy, when assessing the
       appropriate level of penalty we consider factors relevant to each case. In this case, we
       consider it appropriate to take into account the risk of customer harm and are
       satisfied that spilling more frequently than permitted by the UWWTR increased the
       risk that customers suffered indirect harm, for example, through the loss of ability to
       swim or fish in rivers or streams.

If the application of a penalty would be likely to create an incentive to
comply and deter future contraventions or failures

6.21   We consider that a financial penalty issued under section 22A of the WIA91 will act as
       an incentive to deter future contraventions or failures by Thames Water and/or other
       companies.

Any gains made by the company (financial or otherwise) from the
contraventions

6.22   We have not found evidence that Thames Water underspent on wastewater relative to
       the funding allocated to it under AMP6, or that the company made financial gains from
       the contraventions identified, but it does appear that its spending on wastewater was
       slower than planned in the early stages of AMP7. The company told us that this was
       due, in part, to the impact of Covid 19 and the change in its delivery model for AMP7.
       While recognising that the impact of Covid was outside the company's control, the
       change to its delivery model was certainly within its control and it should have been
       able to anticipate, plan for, and manage any delivery risks arising. Moreover, it would
       also appear that in the early part of AMP7 the options to delay and defer spending on
       wastewater were actively considered by the Executive and Board because of a desire
       to prioritise other areas of the business which were subject to greater regulatory
       scrutiny.

6.23   It is also clear from the evidence that we have seen that Thames Water has failed to
       make necessary investment at a number of its WWTW and on its network. While it was
       aware of potential compliance risks at a significant number of its sites, it chose to




   The information that Thames Water provided on its sites which were triggered for investigation

 environmental and water quality impacts), at the vast majority of those (52 or 84%) the spills had had
 an environmental impact and at 23 sites (37%) the spills had had a severe impact on water quality.

       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       prioritise investment at higher risk sites only – so deferring action, and investment, to
       address compliance risks at other sites.

6.24   During the course of our investigation, the company identified 157 sites of concern and
       estimated that it would require a further [CONFIDENTIAL – REDACTED] on top of the
       investment previously allocated under its G2G programme to bring all 157 sites of
       concern back into compliance by the end of AMP8. As noted previously, in its business
       plans for PR24, Thames Water's estimate of the level of investment needed to restore
       compliance at these sites had increased again (to a total of £677 million under its
       WAAP in its original business plan but this was later increased to £1,044 million as part
       of an further case for enhanced expenditure submitted by Thames Water.). While
       Thames Water had sought funding for these under PR24, its request was rejected as
       Ofwat considered that Thames Water had already been funded to comply with its
       permits as part of its base expenditure allowance in previous price controls and
       therefore, any improvements required to address current non-compliance against
       existing permits must be funded by companies as we would not allow customers to pay
       twice.

6.25   Thames Water has claimed it has always acted as a reasonable company within the
       financial constraints imposed on it by the price review process, and also spent/will
       have spent all the funds allocated to it in AMP6 and AMP7. However, given the degree
       of non-compliance with environmental permits that Thames Water has now identified,
       and the scale of investment that it has estimated will be needed to address this non-
       compliance, it would appear that those funds were not directed effectively. We have
       also seen no evidence to indicate that it sought further investment to enable it to
       address compliance risks over this period, nor is there evidence that its shareholders
       offered to fund any remedial work needed. Indeed, even when further opportunities
       arose to enable companies to access additional funding for schemes aimed at
       delivering lasting environmental improvements under the Green Recovery
       Programme 278, Thames Water failed, for example, to request funding to address issues
       associated with its high spilling sites 279. It also failed to obtain any funding under the
       Accelerated Infrastructure Delivery Project280, which, amongst other things, had
       invited submissions from companies for accelerated investment to tackle spills from
       storm overflows. In that case, Thames Water's proposals for funding related primarily
       to schemes aimed at improving water resilience, but here its proposals were rejected
       due to a lack of sufficient detail.

                                                  172
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

Any damage to other market participants

6.26   It is reasonable to conclude that customers’ confidence in the sector as a whole has
       been damaged by Thames Water's failure to fulfil its duties under the UWWTR, section
       94 WIA91 and Condition P. It is also reasonable to conclude that it has lead to
       reputational damage, damage to confidence in the regulatory regime and damage to
       investors' confidence in the sector.

Any damage caused to the environment

6.27   The evidence available to us indicates that Thames Water has persistently failed to
       ensure that its WWTW and networks are designed, constructed, operated and
       maintained so that, under the normal local climatic conditions, all wastewater is
       collected and treated sufficiently in order to minimise adverse effects on the
       environment.

6.28   As set out in Section 3, the poor performance and management of so many of Thames
       Water's WWTW and its failure to have proper oversight of storm overflows on its
       network has meant that spills of untreated wastewater have occurred in
       circumstances which could not be classed as exceptional. In 2021, for example, 73% of
       the storm overflows associated with Thames Water's WWTW (171 overflows) recorded
       20 or more spills and almost 30% recorded more than 60 spills (the majority of which
       due to asset maintenance issues). In addition, while the number of recorded spills
       from storm overflows on Thames Water's network were lower, the fact that in its return
       to the Environment Agency for 2022 it identified 312 additional storm overflows (the
       vast majority on its network) which had not previously been listed; which did not have
       monitors in place; and a significant number of which were also unpermitted 281 –
       suggests that the true scale of spills from Thames Water's storm overflows is likely to
       be much higher. While Thames Water has now told us that as a result of its
       investigations, it now believes that the number of 'genuine' overflows is lower than
       originally estimated, and that it has already applied for permits for a number of those
       overflows, the fact remains that Thames Water failed to ensure that all its assets are
       appropriately managed and monitored to meet the relevant standards and provide
       sufficient performance to comply with the UWWTR.

6.29   As noted above, our decision is not based on any conclusions as to the degree of harm
       caused to the environment or customers as a result of the failures we have identified.
       As spills arising from Thames Water's failures occurred in the past, it is difficult for us
       to definitively establish whether there was environmental harm at the time as a result
       of those spills (though Thames Water's own assessment indicates that at the majority
       of the sites which progressed to Stage 2 of the SOAF, the spills had had an


281 Thames Water has told us that it has now begun to reduce this number as part of its unpermitted

CSO programme.

                                                 173
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       environmental impact). However, as we explain in our Statement of Policy, when
       assessing the appropriate level of penalty we consider factors relevant to each case. In
       this case, we consider it appropriate to take into account the risk of environmental
       harm and are satisfied that spilling more frequently than permitted by the UWWTR
       increased the risk of damage to the natural environment and ecology; poorer
       aesthetics and amenity value in the local area; and public nuisance.

Whether the contraventions or failure was or is of a trivial nature

6.30   We have outlined above the serious nature of Thames Water's contraventions, not only
       in terms of their scale and scope but also in terms of their duration.

Whether the contravention or possibility of a contravention would have
been apparent to a diligent company

6.31   Thames Water’s poor performance has resulted, at least in part, from the
       shortcomings in the internal controls and management of its WWTW and network,
       which we consider would have been apparent to a diligent company. Moreover, even
       when the company was aware of actual non-compliance with environmental permits
       or the risk of non-compliance, it failed to act with sufficient urgency.

6.32   While Thames Water has repeatedly told us that its inability to identify potential non-
       compliance on the scale it now has is due, primarily, to a lack of accurate data, that
       lack of data in itself was due in part to its own failings to properly install and maintain
       EDM monitors at its WWTW (which in turn was failings to properly oversee its capital
       delivery partners). Regardless of the absence of data from monitors on its sites,
       Thames Water's processes and controls should have been robust enough to ensure
       that it had a full understanding of its assets and how those assets were performing –
       particularly given the fact that other sources of information, including mounting
       complaints from stakeholders, was clearly suggesting that things might be going
       wrong across is WWTW estate and network.

6.33   Instead of focusing on its true compliance position, the management and Board of
       Thames Water concentrated on a relatively narrow set of indicators or parameters
       which fed into the company's EPA report or were relevant to the company's PCs and
       ODIs. As a result, and despite the fact that this narrow focus was highlighted as a risk
       in its own internal reviews, there was a lack of sufficient challenge applied to WWTW
       and network performance and compliance information at Board level.

6.34   Thames Water remains ultimately responsible for ensuring that it has met its
       obligations. Given the scale of the potential non-compliance that Thames Water has
       reported and the frequency with which so many of its storm overflows were spilling,
       Ofwat considers that Thames Water did not have appropriate systems in place to
       enable it to identify and address potential non-compliance across its WWTW estate

                                                174
          Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                              on Thames Water

          and network and to ensure that that its WWTW are designed, constructed, operated
          and maintained so that, under the range of typical local climatic conditions, all
          wastewater is collected and treated sufficiently in order to minimise adverse effects
          on the environment.

6.35      In addition, in 2019 Ofwat imposed a penalty on Southern Water for a breach of its
          duties under section 94 WIA91 and Condition P. In our decision document in that case,
          we sent a clear signal to the sector about the importance of ensuring that WWTW were
          effectively managed and were meeting FFT permit conditions. Thames Water was
          aware of this case and had considered the implications of the approach that Ofwat
          had adopted for its own compliance risks, under section 94 WIA91 and Condition P.
          And yet, despite this, and despite the fact that Thames Water itself had been the
          subject of record fines relating to serious pollution incidents at its WWTW because of
          shortcomings in its management of FFT, it failed to act with any real urgency.

6.36      When issuing our determination in the Southern Water case, we also wrote to the CEOs
          of all companies 282 to ensure that they were aware of our findings against Southern
          Water and asked them to consider whether they were confident that the events which
          occurred at Southern Water could not happen in their own organisation. In addition,
          we used these letters as an opportunity to reinforce our message to the sector that we
          will not tolerate companies that "show scant regards to their responsibilities to
          customers and the environment" and noted that "Companies should expect us to step
          up the sanctions we impose for breaches of this nature".

6.37      In 2018 Ofwat also imposed a financial penalty on Thames Water itself for a failure to
          meet its obligations under section 37 WIA91 (the equivalent of its general duty under
          section 94 WIA91 but relating to the provision of water services) and Condition F (now
          Condition P).283 As a result of our case, Thames Water agreed formal undertakings
          under section 19 WIA91, which included undertakings regarding the adequacy of its
          systems of planning and control to meet its regulatory obligations.

6.38      The failings of Thames Water identified in this investigation, occurred despite the
          existence of these precedents. This further shows that Thames Water did not act like a
          diligent company.

Precedents set under equivalent provisions for other utilities

6.39      We are not aware of any relevant precedent decisions by other regulators in respect of
          provisions equivalent to the UWWTR, section 94 of the WIA91 and Condition P.

      Letter from Ofwat Chief Executive to all CEOs regarding Southern Water, 28 June 2019.
283
      Investigation into Thames Water’s failure to meet its leakage performance commitments - Ofwat

Conclusion on the appropriateness of financial penalty and its broad
level

6.40   Having considered the factors above, we are satisfied that a financial penalty is
       appropriate in Thames Water's case. As discussed in detail, we have found that
       Thames Water contravened several of its duties and obligations. These are interrelated
       to an extent and their combined effect impacts on the seriousness of Thames Water's
       conduct as a whole. Therefore, we consider it appropriate to issue a single penalty,
       rather than separate penalties in respect of each breach. The broad framework that
       we have used to assess the seriousness of the breaches we have identified, to
       establish the appropriate level of penalty is set out in Appendix A7.

6.41   In setting the broad penalty level, we took the factors above into account, as well as
       both the cumulative effect of all breaches and the degree of overlap between them.
       We have had particular regard to the serious and widespread nature of the breaches.

6.42   Against this background, therefore, we consider that the broad level of penalty should
       be set at 8.5% of the company's relevant turnover from its wastewater activities.



Aggravating and mitigating factors

6.43   As set out in our Statement of Policy, other aggravating and mitigating factors may be
       taken into consideration to make further adjustments to the penalty level. We are
       satisfied that certain further adjustments are warranted in Thames Water's case. We
       explain this in detail below in relation to the following factors we have considered:

            •   repeated contraventions or failures;
            •   the continuation of a contravention or failure;
            •   any involvement of senior management;
            •   the level of cooperation with any investigation carried out;
            •   any attempts to conceal the contravention or failure;
            •   the proactive reporting of the contravention or failure to the enforcement
                authority;
            •   taking appropriate action to acknowledge and rectify the contravention or
                failure; and
            •   activities to provide restitution and compensation.




                                                176
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

Aggravating factors

Repeated contraventions or failures

6.44   Ofwat has not previously taken enforcement action against Thames Water under the
       UWWTR so the breaches of Regulation 4(4)(a) and Regulation 4(2) and Schedule 2 of
       the UWWTR identified in this investigation do not amount to repeated breaches.

6.45   In 2018, however, we found that Thames Water had breached section 37 WIA91 (an
       equivalent of section 94 WIA91 but for water) and Condition F (now Condition P) of its
       Licence. In this case, we have also concluded that the company has breached its
       obligations under Condition P. We appreciate that the breaches in the 2018 case and
       this investigation relate to different types of conduct by Thames Water and, to an
       extent, different obligations. However, the underlying issues surrounding the
       adequacy of Thames Water's financial and management resources and systems of
       planning and control to ensure it is able to fulfil its statutory obligations which gave
       rise to the breach of Condition P are similar in nature in both cases, and have also
       contributed to this breach. We therefore consider this to be an aggravating factor in
       determining an appropriate penalty level.

The continuation of a breach

6.46   As set out above, while we have previously taken enforcement action against the
       company in respect of a breach of its Licence condition (now Condition P), we have
       considered this as a repeated breach rather than a continuation of a previous breach
       in our assessment of potentially aggravating factors.

6.47   As we have not taken enforcement action against Thames Water previously for a
       breach of UWWTR so our findings in relation to Regulation 4(4)(a) and Regulation 4(2)
       and Schedule 2 UWWTR are not considered to be a continuation of a breach and
       therefore have not factored in our determination of an appropriate penalty level.

Any involvement of senior management

6.48   The company's senior management and Board were aware of at least some of the
       compliance risks at Thames Water's WWTW and failed to act to address those risks
       with sufficient urgency (even when the initial impetus for the company to undertake
       the compliance risk assessment was the fact that it had been the subject of a record
       fine for a number of serious pollution incidents at its WWTW and where a failure to
       manage its FFT permit compliance had been identified as a critical factor). The
       company also chose to defer investment on addressing known compliance risks and
       focus resources on areas which, it acknowledges, were, historically, subject to greater
       regulatory scrutiny.



                                                177
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

6.49   It is also evident from the company's own internal audit reports that management had
       been alerted to the risks arising from the company's historic focus on metrics such as
       "end of pipe" compliance rather than on how the company's WWTW were being
       managed in terms of flow compliance and what this might mean for the company's
       compliance regime. These reports also highlighted issues with the company's
       systems, processes and reporting arrangement. Despite this, and despite the fact that
       Thames Water already had section 19 undertaking in place which covered Condition P
       as a result of previous enforcement action we had taken against it, the senior
       management and Board failed to act to address these issues. We consider this to be an
       aggravating factor.

Any attempts to conceal the contravention or failure

6.50   While Thames Water has failed to identify and report the issues concerned in this
       investigation, we have seen no evidence that the company sought to deliberately
       conceal the contraventions or failures identified by our investigation. We therefore do
       not propose to make any adjustments to the penalty level in this respect.



Mitigating factors

The level of co-operation with any investigation carried out

6.51   In general, we expect the regulated companies to cooperate with our investigations. In
       the case of Thames Water, since the opening our investigation it has provided us with
       a large amount of material, shared with us the details of its own assessments and
       proactively engaged with us to identify the steps it might take to address the concerns
       identified. We note, however, that in some instances it failed to answer questions fully
       or chose to interpret our questions very narrowly. This meant that the case team had
       to issue further information requests, which in turn, added to the length of time of the
       information gathering phase of the investigation.

6.52   More specifically we acknowledge the cautious and inclusive approach Thames Water
       adopted in assessing the scale of potential non-compliance with FFT permit
       conditions. This meant that in addition to sites which are clearly underperforming, it
       also included sites where under-performance relative to FFT permit levels may have
       been marginal or skewed by a one-off event. On balance we consider this approach to
       be a mitigating factor in our determination of the penalty level.

The proactive reporting of the contravention or failure to the
enforcement authority

6.53   Thames Water has acknowledged that there have been historic failures of its
       processes in relation to its wastewater management and has also acknowledged the

                                                178
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       scale of its potential non-compliance with its environmental permits. It has also
       pointed to the significant steps that it is now takin to "rectify its accepted failings" 284,
       including proposing to invest over £1,044 million in AMP8 in wastewater compliance.
       However, it has not, to date, acknowledged that the failings identified during the
       course of our investigation, in turn, constitute breaches of the UWWTR, section 94
       WIA91 or Condition P. It has not been proactive in reporting these to Ofwat.

6.54   Indeed, the company has, until recently, continued to provide assurances to us, via its
       annual RFC and RCS, about the adequacy of its systems, processes and resources to
       enable it to meet its statutory and Licence obligations. We therefore do not propose to
       make any adjustments to the penalty level in this respect.

Taking appropriate action to acknowledge and rectify the contravention
or failure.

6.55   As above, Thames Water has not, to date, acknowledged that the issues identified in
       our investigation constitute breaches of the UWWTR, section 94 WIA91 or Condition P.
       However, as also highlighted above, it is now taking action to acknowledge and rectify
       its failings in relation to FFT permit conditions, DWF permit conditions and storm tank
       capacity issues. It has also engaged with the SOAF process and sought to investigate
       its high spilling storm overflows condition and to identify potential solutions at a
       significant number of these sites.

6.56   We also recognise that it has begun to take action to improve its processes and
       procedures to prevent similar issues arising in future and has also put new reporting
       structures in place to ensure a clearer line of sight between environmental permit
       compliance, the Executive and the Board. These steps are welcome. While it is not
       clear, at this stage, whether these steps will be sufficient to address all the issues
       identified as part of our investigation, we have, nevertheless, viewed this as a
       mitigating factor in relation to the determination of an appropriate penalty level.

Activities to provide restitution and compensation

6.57   Thames Water has not provided any assessment of the potential harm that its
       breaches may have caused. Whilst it has begun to take steps to improve engagement
       with local community groups and eNGOs to address their concerns and is seeking to
       be more transparent about the performance of its wastewater assets and the steps it
       is taking to improve that performance, we do not consider these steps to be sufficient
       to constitute a mitigating factor.




284 Thames Water response to Ofwat's provisional findings, January 2024.


                                                 179
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water


The penalty
6.58   Having considered each of the aggravating and mitigating factors outlined above, we
       have concluded that a further upward adjustment to the broad level starting point for
       the penalty is appropriate in this case, and that the final penalty level should be 9.0%
       of Thames Water's relevant turnover of £1,160.998 million. This is equivalent to a
       penalty of £104.5 million.

6.59   In reaching this decision, we have taken account of Thames Water's wider financial
       issues, but consider the fine to be proportionate within the context of Thames Water's
       compliance issues and its ability to finance its operations. Moreover, it is open to
       Thames Water to propose alternative payment terms for the financial penalty, that we
       must consider under section 22A(7) WIA91. We are disappointed that Thames Water
       has not proposed a settlement package that removes or reduces the need for this
       penalty and by providing suitable redress to customers and the environment.

       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

A1: Enforcement Order

OFWAT HEREBY MAKES A FINAL ENFORCEMENT ORDER UNDER SECTION 18(1) WIA91 IN
RELATION TO THAMES WATER UTILITIES LIMITED AS SET OUT BELOW

Enforcement Order in relation to Thames Water

This document constitutes a final enforcement order (the Order) given by the Water Services
Regulation Authority (the Authority) to Thames Water Services Limited (the Licensee)
under section 18(1) of the Water Industry Act 1991, as amended (WIA91).

Except as otherwise provided in this Order, words and expressions used in this Order have the
same meaning as they have in the WIA91.

On 28 May 2025, the Authority gave notice under section 20 WIA91 of its proposal to make an
order under section 18(1) WIA91 in relation to the Licensee (the 'Notice').

The purpose of this Order is to secure compliance by the Licensee with its statutory
obligations under Regulation 4 and Schedule 2 of the Urban Waste Water Treatment (England
and Wales) Regulations 1994 (UWWTR), section 94 WIA91, and its Instrument of Appointment
(the Licence).


As set out in the Notice:

•   The Licensee has contravened its statutory obligations by failing to ensure that its
    wastewater treatment works (WWTW) and collecting systems were operated, and the
    contents of its sewers treated, in accordance with the requirements of Regulation 4 and
    Schedule 2 UWWTR and section 94 WIA91; and

•   The Licensee has contravened its Licence by failing to comply with the requirements of
    Condition P12.

This Order takes effect as of 28 May 2025.

Requirements
Requirement 1: Securing compliance: Sufficient capacity and performance at
wastewater treatment works (WWTW)

To the extent required by the UWWTR and Section 94 WIA, the Licensee shall ensure and be
able to demonstrate that all aspects of its WWTW are, and for the foreseeable future will
continue to be, designed, constructed, operated and maintained in order to receive and treat
the volumes of received wastewater (dry weather flow (DWF)) and storm sewage they receive


                                                181
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

from their catchment area under usual circumstances (including in normal local climatic
conditions) so as to minimise pollution of the environment, ensuring relevant assets have
appropriate flow to full treatment (FFT) / pass forward flow (PFF) and storage levels to enable
this.

In support of Requirement 1 the Licensee shall:

   a) By 28 November 2025 present and obtain the Authority's agreement (such agreement
      not to be unreasonably withheld) to a plan ("the WWTW Remediation Plan") for (i)
      reviewing its asset base for issues related to compliance with UWWTR and Section 94
      WIA91, (ii) conducting root cause analysis of any potential non-compliance, and (iii)
      remediating non-compliance, and any other identified issues which may result in
      non-compliance, as quickly and efficiently as reasonably possible (such timescales to
      be provided in detail in the plan), taking account of deliverability, affordability and
      financeability. The plan must cover all relevant assets and specify reasonable
      deadlines by which actions will be taken, subject to paragraph 2(b) below. The
      Licensee understands that through future price reviews the Authority will continue to
      determine funding allowances to enable companies to comply with their legal
      obligations as required by the Order and otherwise, recognising the underlying
      principle that customers will not be expected to pay twice by paying again for
      previously funded compliance with legal obligations.

   b) The Licensee will not be held to be in breach of Requirement 1(a) to the extent that it
      can demonstrate that the cause of the delay was reasonably outside its control and
      that it had taken all reasonable steps to minimise that delay.

   c) The WWTW Remediation Plan will include the processes and procedures that the
      Licensee has in place or will put in place to alert it to any concerns over non-
      compliance, so as to enable the Licensee to respond as quickly and as is reasonably
      possible, taking account of deliverability, affordability and financeability, to WWTW
      assets operating below their intended design capacity. This includes identifying and
      swiftly investigating (such investigations to be completed to understand the basis for
      the asset performance and timescales to be provided in detail in the plan), where any
      relevant data or insight indicates that the asset is not, or is at risk of, operating in a
      manner that is not in compliance with UWWTR or section 94 WIA, including where:

          (i)     an asset has spilled more than 20 times on average in a 12-month period (or
                  any tighter threshold established and identified to the Licensee by the
                  Environment Agency or Government);
          (ii)    an asset falls below 108% of the FFT level set out in its environmental permit;
          (iii)   an asset fails to achieve the Q80 DWF level set out in its environmental
                  permit;




                                                182
   Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                       on Thames Water

       (iv)   a storm tank at a WWTW falls below a capacity of 68 litres per head of
              population/2 hours storage (unless this has otherwise been agreed with the
              Environment Agency);
       (v)    an event duration monitor has failed to accurately record data for at least
              90% of a calendar 12-month reporting period;
       (vi) an asset has a reported pollution incident of Category 1-3;
       (vii) an emergency overflow has discharged more than once in any calendar year
              12-month period; and/or
       (viii) an asset which has previously received enhancement funding to meet legal
              obligations, for example to protect bathing waters, RAMSAR, SSSI sites,
              where insufficient operation and management of the asset (rather than new
              demands placed upon it) is identified to now be contributing to the failure of
              the water body to meet legal obligations.
   RAMSAR means Ramsar Convention on Wetlands. RAMSAR sites are wetlands of
   international importance that have been designated under the criteria of that
   Convention for containing representative, rare or unique wetland types or for their
   importance in conserving biological diversity.
   SSSI means Sites of Special Scientific Interest.

d) By 28 November 2025 the Licensee will ensure and be able to demonstrate that it has
   operational planning and systems in place to review, on an annual basis, catchment
   changes (for example in relation to population, changing normal climatic conditions,
   infiltration) that may result in the design, construction, operation and maintenance of
   its WWTW becoming insufficient. This relates to the volumes of wastewater and storm
   sewage the WWTW receive, so that the capacity and performance of those assets
   remains sufficient so as to minimise environmental impact. The Licensee will
   proactively respond (such timescales to be set out in detail in the plan) and remediate
   (taking account of deliverability, affordability and financeability) where it identifies
   potential issues.

e) By 28 November 2025 ensure and be able to demonstrate that it has reviewed the
   requirements of all of its environmental permits to check that all relevant WWTW
   assets are permitted and to check whether the permits' metrics for DWF, FFT/PFF,
   storm tank capacity and other discharge restriction requirements (other than treated
   effluent conditions and any other factors which are outside the scope of the
   investigation) are adequate for the volumes of received wastewater and storm sewage
   and the ecological and environmental needs of the related catchment (in conjunction
   with the Environment Agency), and are consistent with its obligations under the
   UWWTR. Where this is not the case, the WWTW Remediation Plan should set out the
   steps that the Licensee will take, including reasonable deadlines, to engage
   proactively with the Environment Agency to seek for these permits to be amended as
   quickly as reasonably practicable (excluding those permits where such applications
   have already been made and the Licensee is awaiting the Environment Agency


                                            183
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

      response), and to comply with the revised permits. It is accepted that the timescales
      from engaging with the Environment Agency in relation to a permit under this
      paragraph 1(e) to receipt of a revised permit may vary and the Licensee will not be
      penalised for any delays which are reasonably outside of its control.

Requirement 2: Securing compliance: Sufficient capacity and performance of
wastewater network assets

To the extent required by the UWWTR and section 94 WIA, the Licensee shall ensure and be
able to demonstrate that all aspects of its wastewater network assets are, and for the
foreseeable future will continue to be, designed, constructed, operated and maintained in
order to transport to WWTW for treatment the volumes of received wastewater and storm
sewage they receive from their catchment area under usual circumstances (including in
normal local climatic conditions) so as to minimise pollution of the environment, ensuring
relevant assets have appropriate PFF and storage levels to enable this.

In support of Requirement 2 the Licensee shall:

   a) By 28 November 2025 present and obtain the Authority's agreement to a plan (such
      agreement not to be unreasonably withheld) ("the Network Remediation Plan") for
      reviewing its asset base for issues related to compliance with UWWTR and section 94
      WIA, conducting root cause analysis of any compliance concerns and remediating
      identified issues as quickly and efficiently (such timescales to be provided in detail in
      the plan) as reasonably possible, taking account of deliverability, affordability and
      financeability. The plan must cover all relevant assets and specify reasonable
      deadlines by which actions will be taken, subject to paragraph 2(b) below. The
      Licensee understands that through future price reviews the Authority will continue to
      determine funding allowances to enable companies to comply with their legal
      obligations as required by the Order and otherwise, recognising the underlying
      principle that customers will not be expected to pay twice by paying again for
      previously funded compliance with legal obligations.

   b) The Licensee will not be held to be in breach of Requirement 2(a) to the extent that it
      can demonstrate that the cause of the delay was reasonably outside its control and
      that it had taken all reasonable steps to minimise that delay.

   c) The Network Remediation Plan should include the processes and procedures that the
      Licensee has in place or will put in place to alert it to any concerns, so as to enable the
      Licensee to respond as quickly and efficiently as reasonably possible, taking account
      of deliverability, affordability and financeability, to sewerage network assets operating
      below their intended design capacity. This includes identifying and swiftly
      investigating (such investigations to be completed to understand the basis for the
      asset performance and timescales to be provided in detail in the plan), where any



                                                184
   Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                       on Thames Water

   relevant data or insight indicates that the asset is not, or is at risk of, operating in a
   manner that is not in compliance with UWWTR or section 94 WIA, including where:

       (i)   an asset has spilled more than 20 times on average in a 12-month period (or
             any tighter threshold established and identified to the Licensee by the
             Environment Agency or Government);
       (ii) an asset fails to achieve 108% of the PFF level set out in its environmental
             permit;
       (iii) the capacity of a storage tank in the sewerage network has not been agreed
             with the Environment Agency;
       (iv) an event duration monitor has failed to accurately record data for at least 90%
             of a calendar 12-month period;
       (v) an asset has a reported pollution incident of Category 1-3;
       (vi) an emergency overflow has discharged more than once in any calendar year
             12-month period; and/or
       (vii) an asset which has previously received enhancement funding to meet legal
             obligations, for example to protect bathing waters, RAMSAR, SSSI sites, where
             insufficient operation and management of the asset (rather than new
             demands placed upon it) is identified to now be contributing to the failure of
             the water body to meet legal obligations.


   RAMSAR means Ramsar Convention on Wetlands. RAMSAR sites are wetlands of
   international importance that have been designated under the criteria of that
   Convention for containing representative, rare or unique wetland types or for their
   importance in conserving biological diversity.
   SSSI means Sites of Special Scientific Interest.

d) By 28 November 2025 the Licensee will be able to demonstrate that it has appropriate
   operational planning and systems in place to regularly review and proactively respond
   (taking account of deliverability, affordability and financeability) to catchment
   changes (for example in relation to population, changing normal climatic conditions,
   infiltration) that may result in the design, construction, operation and maintenance of
   its sewerage network becoming insufficient for the volumes of wastewater and storm
   sewage they receive, so that the capacity and performance of those assets remains
   sufficient so as to minimise impact to the environment.

e) By 28 November 2025 ensure and be able to demonstrate that it has reviewed the
   requirements of all of its environmental permits to check that all relevant sewerage
   network assets are permitted and to check whether the permits' metrics for PFF and
   storm tank capacity and other discharge restriction requirements are adequate for
   the volumes of received wastewater and storm sewage under usual circumstances
   (including in normal local climatic conditions) and the ecological and environmental
   needs of the related catchment (in conjunction with the Environment Agency), and

       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

       that they are consistent with its obligations under the UWWTR (other than treated
       effluent conditions, which are outside the scope of our investigation). Where this is
       not the case, the Network Remediation Plan should set out the steps that the Licensee
       will take, including reasonable deadlines, to engage proactively with the Environment
       Agency to seek for these permits to be amended as quickly as reasonably practicable
       and to comply with the revised permits. It is accepted that the timescales from
       engaging with the Environment Agency in relation to a permit under this paragraph
       2(e) to receipt of a revised permit may vary and the Licensee will not be penalised for
       any delays which are reasonably outside of its control.

Requirement 3: Securing compliance: Minimising environmental harm from
untreated wastewater discharges

To the extent required by the UWWTR and section 94 WIA91, the Licensee shall ensure and be
able to demonstrate to the Authority that discharges to the environment of wastewater that
have not been fully treated only arise as a result of exceptional circumstances or in situations
where no cost beneficial solution exists to minimise environmental harm from discharges.

In support of Requirement 3 the Licensee shall:

   a) By 28 November 2025, present and obtain the Authority's agreement toa plan (such
      agreement not to be unreasonably withheld) ("the Overflow Plan") setting out the
      processes and procedures that it has in place or will put in place to investigate and
      take remedial action in relation to high spilling storm and emergency overflows. This
      relates to such overflows where an equivalent investigation/remediation has not
      already taken place pursuant to Requirements 2 and 3 above and is subject to
      paragraph 3(b) below. The Licensee understands that through future price reviews
      the Authority will continue to determine funding allowances to enable companies to
      comply with their legal obligations as required by the Order and otherwise,
      recognising the underlying principle that customers will not be expected to pay twice
      by paying again for previously funded legal obligations.

   b) The Licensee will not be held to be in breach of Requirement 3(a) to the extent that it
      can demonstrate that the cause of the delay was reasonably outside its control and
      that it had taken all reasonable steps to minimise that delay.

   c) This should include ensuring and be able to demonstrate that, irrespective of whether
      an asset is compliant with its environmental permit requirements, if it registers:

          (i)    20 or more spills on average in a 12-month period (or any tighter threshold
                 established and identified to the Licensee by the Environment Agency or
                 Government);




                                                186
         Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                             on Thames Water

             (ii)    any dry day spills, as defined by the Environment Agency; 285
             (iii)   any spills which constitutes a pollution incident of Category 1-3; or
             (iv)    more than one discharge from a single emergency overflow in any calendar
                     year.

      d) Apply the BTKNEEC test when considering solutions to minimise environmental harm
         from discharges, ensuring that:

           i.    a reasonable range of remedial options have been assessed, acting diligently,
                 including steps outside the immediate catchment and on a system-wide basis
                 where these may resolve multiple concerns that individually may have been
                 prohibitive to address;
           ii.   in assessing the costs of environmental harm, all relevant issues are
                 considered, including those related to public health and amenity, as well as
                 impacts on plant health and biodiversity; and
          iii.   account is taken of the latest technological advances.

      e) By 28 November 2025 ensure and be able to demonstrate that it has reviewed all of its
         storm overflows to check whether they have the necessary environmental permits.
         Where this is not the case, the Overflow Plan should set out the steps that the
         Licensee it will take, including reasonable deadlines, to engage proactively with the
         Environment Agency to seek for these permits to be obtained as quickly as reasonably
         practicable and to comply with the revised permits. It is accepted that the timescales
         from engaging with the Environment Agency in relation to a permit under this
         condition, to receipt of a revised permit, may vary and the Licensee will not be
         penalised for any delays which are reasonably outside of its control.

      f) Work with the Environment Agency to develop and agree a protocol for assessing the
         environmental impact of discharges to coastal waters and waterways that may have
         an environmental impact from multiple locations. The Licensee will use all reasonable
         endeavours to progress this work in accordance with the Environment Agency’s
         timescales for completion.

Requirement 4: Oversight of compliance

To the extent required by Condition P.12 of its Licence, the Licensee shall ensure and be able
to demonstrate that it has in place processes to ensure that its senior management and
Board actively review and challenge the company's compliance with its environmental
permits, the UWWTR and section 94 WIA91 obligations on a regular basis, including when

considering and signing-off its annual risk and compliance statements, and that they receive

In support of Requirement 4, the Licensee shall:

   a) By 28 November 2025, present and obtain the Authority's agreement to a plan (such

Requirement 5: Delivery of the plans

The Licensee must comply with the obligations contained in the Remediation Plan, the
Network Remediation Plan, the Overflow Plan, and the Management Plan in accordance with
their deadlines and terms. Any and all amendments to the plans must be made by written
agreement between the Licensee and the Authority, such agreement not to be unreasonably
withheld by the Authority or the Licensee in all circumstances.

Requirement 6: Reporting

The Licensee will report to us on a six-monthly basis on its progress against the requirements
of this Order and publish on its website details of its progress and a summary of the actions it
has taken. The first report should be provided by 5pm on 28 November 2025.

A failure to comply with this Order may result in the Authority seeking an injunction or other
forms of relief from the court or applying to the court for a special administration order
against the Licensee.
