A2: Summary of Responses to Consultation

Introduction

1.   In August 2024, Ofwat consulted on its proposal to issue an enforcement order and
     impose a financial penalty on Thames Water for contravention of its obligations in
     relation to the management of its wastewater treatment assets. At the same time, we
     also consulted on our draft decisions in our investigations into Northumbrian Water and
     Yorkshire Water. The consultations closed on 10 September 2024.

2.   We received a total of 30 responses across all three consultations. In addition to detailed
     responses from Thames Water and the two other companies, we also received responses
     from a number of other water companies; from the Consumer Council for Water (CCW);
     from a number of local councils and local stakeholder and environmental groups in the
     different companies' areas of operation as well as responses from a number of individual
     customers. 286 The responses ranged from very detailed comments relating to the
     specifics of the individual investigations to more general comments and observations on
     water companies' management of their wastewater treatment assets and the action that
     the companies, and Ofwat, should now take.

3.   On 10 September 2024, Water UK (on behalf of the sector as a whole) also wrote to us to
     set out its views on our draft decisions and to seek clarification on our approach to the
     enforcement of the UWWTR. In doing so, it repeated many of the points and issues raised
     by the water companies themselves. We have also taken these into consideration, along
     with all the other responses we received, in reaching our final decision.

4.   In relation to Thames Water's response to our draft decision, many of the points it raised
     are points that it had previously made in its response to our provisional findings that we
     shared with the company in December 2023 and which we had already considered in
     reaching the draft decision we consulted publicly on in August 2024. Despite this Thames
     Water continues to disagree with various aspects of our investigation and how we have
     characterised its conduct and behaviour in our draft decision. It also disagrees with our
     overall findings in relation to its breaches of UWWTR, section 94 WIA91 and licence
     condition P. Where new evidence has been provided relevant to our assessment, this has
     been reflected in the relevant sections of our final decision. Similarly, where Thames
     Water has, as part of its response, provided updates to figures or updates on the changes
     it has made to its processes and procedures, these have been included in the relevant


286 Anglian Water Services Limited, Consumer Council for Water, Northumbrian Water Limited, South

West Water Limited, Thames Water Utilities Limited, United Utilities Water Limited, Water UK, Wessex
Water Services Limited and Yorkshire Water Services Limited. River Colne Catchment Action Network,
Crane Valley Community Interest Company, Henley Mermaids, Henley Town Council, River Chess
Association, River Partnerships in London, South Tyneside Council, Whitburn Neighbourhood forum
and 13 customers also provided a response.

                                                 190
         Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                             on Thames Water

     sections of our final decision. In this Summary of Responses we summarise the key
     comments received from stakeholders and set out our responses and/or explain how we
     have taken these into consideration. For the avoidance of doubt, we have considered all
     of the points raised in the responses in reaching our final decision.

5.   Many of the issues raised by Thames Water and other water companies relate to our
     expectations of what companies are required to do to ensure they are compliant with the
     UWWTR and on the steps that companies should be taking to ensure that compliance.
     Thames Water and others have, for example, considered:

     •   That the position set out in our draft decision in relation to UWWTR compliance is
         unlawful, unreasonable and disproportionate and is inconsistent with existing
         Government policy and the practices of the Environment Agency.
     •   That Ofwat is seeking to impose a new legal standard and enforce this standard
         retrospectively.
     •   That in doing so, Ofwat has failed to consult the industry, or issue guidance to the
         sector outside of our enforcement action and that this is contrary to Ofwat's duties
         under Section 2 WIA91 and is contrary to companies' legitimate expectations.
     •   That Ofwat's approach is inconsistent with the price review process and that Thames
         Water, and other companies, have not been properly funded to meet the standards
         that Ofwat is now seeking to impose.

6.   We address these points in more detail below.

The position in the draft decision is unlawful, unreasonable and
disproportionate and is inconsistent with existing practices

7.   All water company respondents, including Thames Water, claimed that the approach we
     have adopted to assessing compliance is not consistent with existing Government policy
     and the practices of the Environment Agency, most notably the Environmental Permitting
     Regime and the SOAF, which they claimed they have followed and relied on during the
     period covered by our investigation and which they say are intended to demonstrate
     compliance with Regulations 4 and 5 UWWTR. They consider Ofwat has raised no
     objection to companies following these policies and practices until now.

8.   The water companies claimed that as they have been complying with the standards
     required of them, as they understood them to be, it is unreasonable and unlawful for
     Ofwat to have departed from these settled practices and policies in the context of an
     enforcement process. It is also, in their view, unreasonable for Ofwat to seek to impose a
     new legal standard without first consulting with, or issuing guidance to, the sector.

9.   Thames Water and the other companies also challenged Ofwat's remit in relation to the
     enforcement of the UWWTR and argued that, in their view, the issues raised by Ofwat in
     the draft decisions and enforcement orders should be dealt with, and enforced by, the

                                                  191
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

    Environment Agency. This is because the Environment Agency has a duty under
    Regulation 6(2) of the UWWTR to secure compliance with the UWWTR through the setting
    and varying of environmental permits, which are negotiated individually in relation to the
    particular conditions of specific sites. As a result, in their view, Ofwat's duties towards
    the environment are indirect only.

Our response

10. As we have explained in Section 2 of this document, Ofwat has a duty under section 18
    WIA to enforce Regulation 4(2) and 4(4) UWWTR as part of section 94 WIA. This duty is
    independent of the Environment Agency and Ofwat role in relation to the enforcement of,
    and companies' obligations under Regulation 4 UWWTR are also independent of the
    Environmental Permitting Regime (EPR). The Environment Agency is responsible for
    enforcing specific permit requirements under the EPR, whereas we are responsible for
    enforcing Regulation 4 UWWTR and section 94 WIA91.

11. We are aware of the Government's and the Environment Agency's existing policies and
    practices relating to wastewater discharges and permit compliance. We are also aware
    that the companies had, erroneously, believed the SOAF, in particular, to determine the
    limits of their compliance with UWWTR with respect to spills. Ofwat is the enforcing
    authority under Regulation 4 and 5 UWWTR, independent of the Environment Agency,
    which is primarily responsible for enforcing environmental permit requirements. Ofwat
    has a statutory duty to enforce the UWWTR, and companies have their own direct duties
    to comply with the UWWTR. In reaching our decision on penalties we have recognised
    that companies, erroneously, treated the SOAF and permitting regimes as the limit of
    their storm overflow remediation obligations, and only apply penalties where companies'
    failures go beyond these. Our approach is further detailed in Appendix A6. We have used
    our judgement to reflect the fact that the companies worked to a set of pre-existing
    policies, in particular the SOAF, which we took into account in setting the penalties. We
    have also considered this in our assessment of companies' compliance with Condition
    P12.

12. Our final decision document sets out the approach we have taken to applying the
    relevant statutory provisions in practice, which we have done in accordance with the
    provisions themselves and case law. We consulted on this approach when we published
    the proposed decisions and enforcement orders on 6 August 2024. This provided water
    companies and other stakeholders the opportunity to put forward their views on our
    approach, which we have carefully considered. The three companies concerned were
    also able to provide their comments to us on the proposed decisions earlier in the
    investigation and we have similarly carefully considered these comments.




                                                192
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

Ofwat's approach risks discriminating against Yorkshire Water, Thames
Water and Northumbrian Water

13. Thames Water, Yorkshire Water and Northumbrian Water claimed that by issuing draft
    decisions against them only, when all companies are subject to the same compliance
    obligations, Ofwat was effectively discriminating against them. As such, they considered
    that requiring the three companies to take action ahead of the rest of the industry risked
    putting them at an unfair disadvantage

Our response

14. We have progressed three of the cases ahead of the others based primarily on practical
    considerations around administrative matters and resourcing. However, all 11 companies
    are under investigation, and we are following the same procedural rules and applying the
    same approach to each company to ensure that they are treated consistently.

Approach to assessing spill compliance

15. All water companies, including Thames Water, told us that they do not recognise our
    interpretation of the UWWTR and disagreed with what they view as a retrospective
    application of new requirements. In particular, the companies objected to our approach
    to assessing spill compliance, especially the use of 20 spills as a threshold for
    investigation. They noted that neither the storm overflows discharge reduction plan
    (SODRP) or the SOAF make reference to a 20 spills standard. They also noted that the 20-
    spill threshold differs from that used by the Environment Agency and that we have not
    previously mentioned the 20 spill standard nor issued any guidance on its application.

16. Thames Water and other water companies have sought to argue that by using a threshold
    of 20 spills as an indicator that spills from storm overflows may be occurring routinely
    and not in exceptional circumstances, that Ofwat has set a new legal standard and that
    this standard is inconsistent with the industry's current approach and with the standard
    that has been applied by the Environment Agency and the Government.

17. Thames Water also argued that our 'novel interpretation' of the UWWTR has been
    imposed without formal consultation or guidance; that it was not invoked in Ofwat’s prior
    investigation of Southern Water; and, that it is at tension with the framework designed to
    discharge water companies' obligations under the UWWTR and in the securing of a
    progressive reduction in storm overflows more generally.

18. Thames Water and other water companies have argued that, rather than use our
    enforcement powers, Ofwat should have consulted with, or issued guidance to, the sector
    on the standard that we expect companies to meet to ensure their compliance with the
    UWWTR. Thames Water has argued, for example, that our failure to do so is inconsistent



                                                193
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

       with our duties under Section 2 WIA91287 which, amongst other things, requires Ofwat to
       have regard to the principles of best regulatory practice. This includes ensuring that our
       regulatory activities are transparent, accountable, proportionate, consistent and
       targeted.

19. Thames Water and the other water companies have also claimed that the industry now
    finds itself in the position that the Government has (through the Environment Agency
    and Defra) established a regulatory baseline (the 60-spill standard under SOAF), which
    we have previously agreed to and endorsed through our price control activities, but is
    now told that it should have been applying a stricter standard that it did not know about
    and could not reasonably have known about.

20. All the water companies have argued that compliance with the UWWTR and the EPR are
    inextricably linked, with the EPR designed and intended to ensure compliance with the
    UWWTR. They claim that by virtue of complying with the Environment Agency's permits
    and by following the SOAF process they are in compliance with the UWWTR, and Ofwat is,
    therefore, incorrect to suggest that companies' obligations under the UWWTR are
    separate from the EPR.

Our response

21. We disagree that we are interpreting the UWWTR in a novel and unprecedented way, or
    that we are seeking to impose a new legal standard. As we explain in Section 2, our
    approach is based on the legislation and relevant caselaw.

22. We agree that the Environment Agency has a duty under Regulation 6(2) UWWTR to
    exercise its permitting functions under the UWWTR in relation to discharges from
    regulated systems so as to limit pollution to receiving waters. However, that duty does not
    supplant or override the obligation owed directly by sewerage undertakers under
    Regulation 4 UWWTR, or Ofwat’s duty to take enforcement action in respect of
    contraventions of that obligation: as Holgate J said in Wildfish at §87, “the EA is not an
    enforcing authority under the WIA 1991 for breaches of the 1994 Regulations”. And in any
    event it is concerned with “the limitation of pollution of receiving waters”, whereas the
    duty in Regulation 4 UWWTR (to ensure, among other things, that treatment plants are
    “designed […], constructed, operated and maintained to ensure sufficient performance
    under all normal local climatic conditions”) is of more general application. The
    companies continue to be directly subject to the obligations under Regulation 4 UWWTR
    notwithstanding the existence of the EPR.




287
      Our duties - Ofwat

                                                    194
        Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                            on Thames Water

23. As the body with the duty to enforce Regulation 4(2) and 4(4) UWWTR we cannot defer to
    another regulator's judgment in respect of the exercise of its separate statutory functions
    but must apply Regulation 4(2) and 4(4) consistently with legislation and case law.

24. Nevertheless, on balance, we think the fact that the companies, erroneously considered
    the SOAF and permits to set the limits of their obligations under Regulation 4 UWWTR is a
    factor to take into account when determining what punitive action Ofwat considers it
    appropriate to take. As we explain elsewhere, although we have made findings of past
    breach by reference to the 20 spills indicator, for the purpose of our penalty assessment,
    we have only included those storm overflows that have spilled in excess of 60 times per
    year, which is the upper SOAF threshold. Similarly we have only taken account of
    breaches of Regulation 4(4) which are also inconsistent with permit requirements in
    assessing penalty levels.

25. In our assessment of Condition P12, we have also considered what obligations the
    companies ought to have been aware of in light of previous legal developments and case
    law and regulatory activity.

26. In relation to our investigation into Southern Water (which we concluded in 2019), it is
    important to note that our investigations are case specific. The enforcement
    investigation into Southern Water was a different case with different information and
    data.

27. To the extent that Thames Water has argued that Ofwat should have sought to first
    consult or announce to the industry the legal framework that we have applied in arriving
    at our draft decision, and that a failure to do is contrary to Ofwat's duty under section 2
    WIA91, we note that, as part of Ofwat's established enforcement process, our draft
    decisions in our investigations, have been subject to a consultation during which
    companies 288, and other interested stakeholders, have been be able to provide their
    views. These views have been considered and have been taken into account in reaching
    our final decision in this case.

28. Furthermore, we do not consider that Ofwat, or any other regulator or enforcement body,
    should first have to consult on their interpretation of the law before they can seek to
    enforce it, particularly where that regulatory has a duty to enforce the law when a breach
    has been identified.


288 Ofwat does have a statutory duty under section 22B WIA91 to publish a statement of policy “with

respect to the imposition of penalties and the determination of their amount” and section 22B(5)
WIA91 also provides that Ofwat “shall undertake such consultation as he or it considers appropriate
when preparing or revising his or its statement of policy”. However, there is no equivalent provision in
relation to enforcement policy more generally i.e. Ofwat’s policy as to what issues to prioritise for
investigation and potential enforcement action. The publication of our draft enforcement order under
section 20 WIA91 provides an opportunity for companies and other affected persons to make
representations about it.

                                                   195
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

Ofwat's approach conflicts with companies' legitimate expectations.

29. In its response to our draft decision, Thames Water told us that our approach to the
    enforcement of the UWWTR conflicts with its, and the industry’s, legitimate expectations
    with the EPR and following the SOAF process, that by complying with the EPR and by
    following the SOAF process, it was also complying with the UWWTR and therefore that it
    should not be penalised if it had complied with these.

Our response

30. As we explain above, we have based our interpretation of Regulation 4 UWWTR on the
    pre-existing legislation and caselaw dating from 2012 (the subject matter of which
    included Thames Water assets).

The level of Thames Water's non-compliance with Regulation 4 UWWTR
is overstated has not been properly assessed

31. For the reasons outlined above, Thames Water told us that, in its views, it has complied
    with the UWWTR. It also told us that, in any event, in our assessment of whether its sites
    had breached or were in breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR, Ofwat had failed to fully reflect
    the requirements of the UWWTR and SOAF and, as a result, we have likely overstated the
    number of sites in breach and the number of sites included as part of our penalty
    assessment. While we have also addressed many of these points in Sections 3 and 4 of
    this document, we also provide a summary of the main issues raised by Thames Water
    here and our position on those issues.

Ofwat has misinterpreted the scope of the UWWTR

32. Thames Water has argued that the UWWTR is only applicable to agglomerations with a
    population equivalent (PE) of less than 2,000 people and that Ofwat has failed to take the
    necessary step of assessing the extent to which WWTW and storm overflows serve a PE
    equal to or in excess of 2,000.

Our position

33. As acknowledged in Section 2, Regulation 4(2) expressly imposes obligations in respect of
    the provision of collecting systems “for every agglomeration with a population equivalent
    of 2,000 or more”.

34. With respect to Regulation 4(4), Regulation 5 sets minimum treatment requirements
    according to the size of population served by the relevant WWTW. Regulation 5(7)
    explains that, notwithstanding the above, appropriate treatment should be provided for
    all agglomerations.



                                                196
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

35. In relation to our assessment of Thames Water's breaches under Regulation 4(2), as we
    have explained in our draft decision, and again in this final decision, our finding of
    breach relates not to the number of high spilling storm overflows on its network that the
    company failed to deal with as part of the SOAF process, but rather to the company's
    failure to monitor and manage its assets to ensure that it was complying with the UWWTR
    – as evidenced by the fact that in 2022 Thames Water included over 300 network storm
    overflows in its annual EDM return to the Environment Agency. These overflows were
    unmonitored (and so the true scale of spills from Thames Water's network storm
    overflows is likely to have been higher than recorded) and many were also unpermitted.

Ofwat has failed to understand how the BTKNEEC test is applied

36. A number of companies claimed that Ofwat’s position, that they should have conducted
    BTKNEEC assessments for high spilling overflows, runs contrary to previous sectoral
    guidance and, in particular, to the approach used under SOAF. They claim that BTKNEEC
    is an objective standard, with the test being not whether a company has applied
    BTKNEEC but whether, in objective terms, a non-exceptional discharge can be justified
    by cost-benefit analysis that satisfies BTKNEEC.

37. This argument takes two different forms. The first is that Ofwat cannot conclude that
    there has been a breach in respect of a particular site without considering BTKNEEC
    issues. The second is that Ofwat cannot conclude that there has been a breach in
    respect of a particular site unless it concludes on the balance of probabilities that there
    is a BTKNEEC-compliant solution that could be adopted at that site but has not been
    adopted.

Our position

38. Ofwat agrees that a site is only operating in breach of Regulation 4(2) UWWTR if it is both
    high spilling and there is no unimplemented BTKNEEC consistent remedy available to
    improve the position. However, Regulation 4(2) UWWTR also clearly states that companies
    must ensure that they provide collecting systems for the specified agglomerations which
    meet the requirements of Schedule 2 UWWTR including as to the limitation of pollution
    from storm overflows consistent with BTKNEEC. The ECJ made clear that this means that
    storm overflows should only spill in exceptional circumstances unless it would be
    inconsistent with BTKNEEC for those spills to be remedied, and that where BTKNEEC is
    relied upon this should be evidenced and not just asserted. If companies become aware
    that any of their storm overflows are spilling outside exceptional circumstances, their
    obligations under Regulation 4(2) UWWTR therefore require them to ensure that they are
    nevertheless operating consistently with BTKNEEC and to take remedial steps if they are
    not.




                                                197
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

Burden of proof and evidence

39. Thames Water also claimed that in our approach to UWWTR, we had improperly reversed
    the burden of proof onto it for evidencing environmental harm, BTKNEEC or exceptional
    circumstance.

Our position

40. Our approach does not reverse the burden of proof with respect to BTKNEEC. Regulation 4
    UWWTR requires the companies to ensure that they comply with BTKNEEC in the way they
    design, construct and maintain their collecting systems in non-exceptional
    circumstances. If there are non-exceptional spills, the companies are also required to
    ensure that they comply with BTKNEEC in considering how to address them.

41. In any event, the companies hold the evidence relevant to their assessment of
    environmental harm and BTKNEEC, which we note they were also required to conduct
    upon reaching the spill thresholds under the SOAF, and we have requested and
    considered evidence held by the companies relating to BTKNEEC as well as environmental
    harm in the course of our investigation.

Our assessment of sites where high spills are due to infiltration

42. In its response to our draft decision, Thames Water also challenged our findings on the
    number of its sites which had spilled more than 60 times due to asset maintenance
    reasons and claimed that we had "likely overstated" the number of its sites that we had
    found to have breached Regulation 4 UWWTR in our penalty assessment. This was
    because we had not taken into account the fact that under SOAF, where the reason for
    the high spills was identified as being due to infiltration (which is categorised under
    asset maintenance), it is recognised that it may take longer than a year to resolve the
    root cause of the spills.

Our position

43. We have considered this point and, as noted in Sections 3 and 4 of this document, we
    sought additional information from Thames Water on the sites where infiltration was
    identified as the reason the site was triggered for investigation under SOAF and on the
    company's plans to address issues at those sites. We have taken this additional evidence
    into account and have revised our assessment of the number of sites we have included as
    part of our penalty assessment. Our updated assessment is explained in Section 4.




                                                198
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

Alleged inconsistency in how remedial actions are treated as part of Ofwat's
assessment of breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR

44. As explained in Section 4, under SOAF where the root cause of a high spill is identified as
    being hydraulic capacity, further investigation is required to assess whether the spill has
    resulted in environmental harm and if a cost beneficial solutions exist to resolve the
    cause of the spill. A failure to carry out further investigation is considered to be a breach
    of Regulation 4 UWWTR. Where no cost beneficial solution is identified or the
    investigation is still ongoing (within reasonable timescales), the site is excluded from
    both the breach assessment and the penalty assessment (as it is not yet known whether
    a cost beneficial solution exists). Where a cost beneficial solution has been identified, the
    site is included in our breach assessment but is excluded from our penalty assessment –
    if these is evidence that cost beneficial solution or remedial action is being implemented
    or will be implemented within a reasonable timescales.

45. In its response to our draft decision, Thames Water welcomed our approach to assessing
    sites where hydraulic capacity was identified as the reason for the high spills under the
    SOAF process but claimed that Ofwat appeared to adopt a different, and inconsistent,
    approach when considering the remedial action that the company was taking, or was
    planning to take, outside of the SOAF process. In particular, it noted that we had not
    adopted the same approach in relation to its remedial work to address FFT, DWF and
    storm tank capacity issues at its 157 "sites of concern" under its Waste Asset Assurance
    Programme (WAAP) or WINEP. In Thames Water's view, these programmes are also steps
    it is taking to ensure its assets are maintained and operated according to BTKNEEC in
    compliance with the UWWTR and therefore, these sites should also be considered not to
    be in breach.

Our response

46. We disagree with Thames Water's claims that we are acting inconsistently in the way in
    which we are treating sites with remediation plans in our breach and penalty
    assessments. We recognise that the companies, wrongly, considered that adherence to
    the SOAF process set the limits of what was required under the UWWTR in relation to
    storm overflows and have taken those regulatory expectations into account when
    assessing penalties. In terms of breach, where a site is spilling outside exceptional
    circumstances, the company is required to assess whether remedial action needs to be
    taken consistent with BTKNEEC. If it is in the process, diligently and expeditiously, of
    carrying out that investigation, it is fulfilling its duty to ensure the operation of its
    network consistent with BTKNEEC.

47. If it transpires that there are improvements that are available consistent with BTKNEEC
    (whether operational, maintenance or improvements to hydraulic capacity), its network
    will not be operating consistently with the UWWTR until those improvements have been
    implemented. Similarly, where hydraulic capacity issues have been identified at a WWTW

                                                199
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

    that require remediation in order for the site to be compliant with the UWWTR and that
    remediation has not been implemented, the site will be non-compliant until that
    remediation has occurred.

The proposed penalty is excessive, unfair and unreasonable, lacks
adequate justification, and is not consistent with past precedent.

48. In its response to our draft decision, Thames Water told us that it does not accept that it
    had breached (and in some cases, is continuing to breach) the UWWTR, section 94 WIA91
    and Condition P of its Licence. On that basis, it argued, that Ofwat’s proposed penalty is
    unfounded, and in any event wholly disproportionate in light of the level of penalties in its
    previous enforcement cases. Similar arguments were also put forward by both Yorkshire
    Water and Northumbrian Water in their responses to their respective draft decisions.

Our position

49. In our draft decision and in our final decision we have set out the evidence that we have
    relied on in our assessment of whether Thames Water has breached UWWTR, section 94
    WIA91 and Condition P of its licence. Given the scale, scope and duration of the issues
    identified during the course of our investigation into Thames Water's management of its
    wastewater treatment assets, we have also explained the reasons why we consider a
    significant penalty is appropriate in this case.

50. In arriving at our penalty assessment, we have followed our published policy statement
    and have sought to adopt a consistent approach across all our investigations. However,
    the level of penalties that Ofwat can impose is a matter of regulatory discretion and
    whilst we have taken account of precedents (including our investigation into Southern
    Water), we are not bound by our previous decisions. Therefore, we assess each of our
    enforcement cases based on the specific circumstances of that case and while the levels
    of penalties in previous cases may provide guidance, they are not determinative of our
    future decisions.

51. Similarly, while Thames Water and other companies have sought to argue that the
    penalty in our current investigations is disproportionate in light of the penalty imposed
    on Southern Water in that case, we again stress that we consider each case individually.

There is a lack of transparency as to how the penalties have been
calculated

52. Thames Water, Yorkshire Water and Northumbrian Water have also claimed that there is
    a lack of transparency as to how the penalties have been calculated and that we have not
    explained adequately how the starting penalty in these decision was reached or how the
    mitigating and aggravating factors have been taken into account.



                                                200
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

Our response

53. Whilst we consider each investigation on a case-by-case basis, and whilst we have
    discretion about the level of penalties to impose, we have also sought to apply our
    published policy consistently across all our investigations.

54. In Section 6 we have explained in detail the factors that we have taken into account in
    arriving at our starting penalty in this case and the reasons for our assessment. We have
    also explained our assessment of all the relevant aggravating and mitigating factors in
    this case.

55. We have additionally provided Thames Water and other companies with an overview of
    the broad framework we have used to guide the decisions we have made on the
    appropriate penalty levels for all the companies that are subject to our wastewater
    investigations. This is included at Appendix A7.


Relevant revenue in the penalty calculation

56. In addition to repeating their arguments that the proposed penalties are excessive,
    disproportionate and inconsistent with those imposed in previous cases, Thames Water
    and a number of other water companies have raised concerns about the revenue streams
    that we have included as "relevant turnover" for the purposes of the penalty assessment.
    For example, Thames Water argued that retail revenue should be excluded from the
    turnover figure, whilst Yorkshire Water considered that revenue associated with
    bioresources should be excluded.

Our response

57. Relevant turnover is determined in accordance with the Water Industry (Determination of
    Turnover for Penalties) Order 2005 (Penalties Order), which in broad terms, defines it as
    revenue from "the provision of goods and services in the course of the [company's]
    regulated activities in respect of which the penalty is imposed". "Regulated activities" of a
    sewerage undertaker is defined as "its functions as a sewerage undertaker". We are
    satisfied that the revenues from the provision of bioresource services as well as a
    proportion of a company's retail revenues 289 can be reasonably considered to form part of
    the companies' functions as sewerage undertakers.




289
   As Thames Water's reported revenues from retail wastewater services relate to both water and
wastewater services, we have estimated the proportion for wastewater services on the basis of the
ratio of Wastewater Network+ revenues to Water Network+ revenues.

       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

The enforcement order is not sufficiently clear as to what is required and by
when

58. Thames Water, Yorkshire Water and Northumbrian Water raised concerns about the
    clarity of the proposed enforcement order which accompanied our draft decisions. In
    particular, the companies argued that the order was unclear on the use of the 20 spills
    threshold; on the timeframe for delivery of a number of requirements referred to in the
    order; and on some of the terminology used.

Our response

59. While we consider that the draft enforcement was clear as to our expectations of the
    companies, in light of the comments received we have updated the enforcement order.

Companies have not previously been funded to carry out all the investigations
and to take the necessary remedial action required under the enforcement
order

60. In its response to our draft decision, Thames Water told us that the regulatory standard
    imposed by the proposed Enforcement Order with respect to the UWWTR is not funded,
    exceeds the requirements of the Environmental Permitting Regime, and cuts across the
    objective of the SODRP to secure a progressive multi-amp reduction in discharges. It also
    told us that it is inconsistent with Ofwat’s duties under section 2 WIA91 (to ensure the
    financing of the proper carrying out of the company's functions, the long-term resilience
    of the company's provision of sewerage services (including by promoting appropriate
    long-term planning and investment) and to act with regards to transparency,
    accountability, proportionality and consistency).

61. A number of other companies made similar points to those raised by Thames Water and
    argued that they had not previously been funded to carry out all the required
    investigations referred to in the enforcement order and to take the necessary remedial
    action. They also sought greater clarity over how the enforcement orders will work with
    future funding requirements.

62. Some companies (for example, Yorkshire Water and Anglian Water) also argued that
    there is a clear contradiction between our expectations as set out in the enforcement
    order and the price review process and pointed out that while we recognise that
    considerable funding is required to identify and investigate high spilling sites, we have
    failed to address the tension between our expectations on companies, the funding
    required to meet those expectations, and the established process for accessing that
    funding.

63. Moreover, a number of water companies, including Thames Water, sought to argue that
    many of the alleged failures that we identified as part of our investigations can be

                                                202
          Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                              on Thames Water

       explained by the process that Ofwat, alongside the water industry, have followed for
       allocating funding for investigating and addressing issues; namely, consistent with the
       WINEP process followed at PR19, where issues are identified in one AMP and funding is
       sought such that these can be investigated further and/or remediated in the next AMP.

64. Finally, given the cost of complying with the standards included in our enforcement order
    (that is, the 20 spills threshold) Thames Water and a number of other water companies
    (for example, Yorkshire Water and Water UK) again questioned whether we had
    considered whether this was consistent with Ofwat's duty under section 2 WIA91 to
    ensure that companies are able to finance the proper carrying out of their functions.

Our response

65. As noted previously, in our enforcement order, we are not seeking to impose any new
    requirements on companies, but are enforcing pre-existing provisions in accordance
    with caselaw. It is for companies to submit business plans under the price control which
    ensure that they are able to comply with their legal obligations..

66. We do, however, recognise the expectations set by the SOAF and have not sought to
    penalise companies, where they have been properly following the SOAF process, by
    excluding those sites from our penalty calculations. Our final determinations for PR24
    explain the funding available for wastewater remediation under the price control 290.

Our findings in relation to Thames Water's compliance with section 94 WIA91

67. Thames Water told us that it did not accept that it had breached its duties under section
    94 of the WIA91 and denied that any contravention is ongoing. It claimed that our draft
    decision in this respect relied largely upon a flawed interpretation of the UWWTR and that
    we had failed to substantiate that it had breached section 94 as a stand-alone breach.

68. It argued that Ofwat is required, as set out in the 2019 Southern Water penalty decision,
    to assess whether there has been systemic failure with the company’s obligations and
    whether Thames Water's actions were the actions to be expected of a reasonable
    company. In its view, we have not considered adequately whether Thames Water failed to
    act reasonably in the circumstances and have, therefore, in its view, failed to meet the
    legal test for a finding of breach under section 94 WIA91.

69. Thames Water claimed that it had, in fact, acted reasonably in the circumstances
    according to best industry standards and in the absence of any guidance from Ofwat on
    how it proposed to interpret and enforce Regulation 4 of the UWWTR. It also claimed that
    it had relied on its legitimate expectation that compliance with the EPR and SODRP
    would mean compliance with the UWWTR and had sought funding under the various


290
      Thames Water's Final Determination for PR24.

                                                     203
         Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                             on Thames Water

      price reviews on this basis. Given these circumstances, therefore, it could not have been
      apparent to any reasonable company that it was in breach of these requirements.

Our response

  70. We disagree that our interpretation or the UWWTR is flawed for the reasons explained
      previously. We have also addressed the point about companies' legitimate expectations
      above.

  71. As explained in our draft decision document and as set out again in Section 2 of this final
      decision document, each breach of Regulation 4 UWWTR itself constitutes a breach of
      section 94 WIA91. We have applied the appropriate legal test in our findings relating to
      breach of section 94 WIA91.

  72. In reaching our findings in relation to the extent to which the company has complied,
      and continues to comply with, its obligations under section 94, we have based our
      assessment on the magnitude and extent of Thames Water's failures under the UWWTR
      which point to a systemic failure to make appropriate provision for effectually dealing
      with the contents of sewers and constitutes an ongoing breach of section 94 WIA91. In
      reaching our findings, we have considered the actions that a reasonable company would
      take.

  Our findings in relation to Thames Water's compliance with Condition P

  73. In its response to our draft decision Thames Water told us that it had carefully considered
      Ofwat’s draft decision and whilst it accepted that its governance processes could have
      been better, it did not accept that these fell below the standards required by Condition P
      such that it had breached Condition P historically, nor did it accept that any such breach
      is ongoing.

  74. In respect of our finding of historic breach of Condition P, Thames Water denied any
      intimation that it had failed to spend appropriately on compliance or that it had
      improperly preferred areas of the business which were subject to greater regulatory
      scrutiny. It noted that whilst its Board must make challenging decisions on compliance,
      it had spent all of its PR14 allowance in relation to its wastewater activities in AMP6 and
      was expected to have overspent relative to its PR19 allowance for AMP7.

  75. Thames Water also rejected any suggestion that it should have met any alleged
      compliance funding gap within AMP6 and AMP7 with shareholder funds or could have
      realistically secured more in-AMP funding from Ofwat within this period. It pointed to the
      fact that its shareholders had already injected significant new equity into the business
      and had received no external dividend for many years. It again reiterated its view that the
      regulatory standard included in the draft decision represents a new legal standard which



                                                  204
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

    Thames Water and its shareholders were not aware of and could not expected to bear
    retrospectively.

76. In relation to any continuing breach, it pointed to the enhancements it had made as part
    of its Turnaround Plan and the changes to its governance processes to improve its
    resources and systems of planning and internal control, including the advances in
    monitoring and data assessment, institutionalised training and cultural programmes and
    enhanced governance processes. These changes, in Thames Water's view, indicated that
    even if the company had breached Condition P in the past, there is no ongoing breach.

Our response

77. Under licence Condition P, companies are required to ensure that they have the
    necessary processes in place to enable them to meet all their regulatory obligations and
    to assure Ofwat annually that they have sufficient resources to enable them to do so.

78. Based on the evidence that we have seen, we have concluded that, from at least March
    2017, Thames Water failed to act in the manner best calculated to ensure it had adequate
    financial and management resources and systems of planning and internal control in
    place to carry out its regulated activities, and in particular to manage and effectively
    operate its WWTW and networks to meet their environmental obligations under their
    permits, the UWWTR and section 94 WIA91.

79. As explained in our draft decision and again in this final decision document, we would
    have expected a company to have in place adequate systems and controls in order to
    prevent, identify and address the issues of the type outlined in this case. As we also
    explained it is clear from the company's own compliance risk assessments that that the
    company was aware that it had significant compliance risks across numerous sites and
    yet, did not respond with any real urgency to address them. These risks related not to any
    new standard that Thames Water alleges that Ofwat is now seeking to enforce (and which
    it therefore could not be expected to have complied with), but rather a failure to comply
    with its existing environmental permits.

80. We therefore remain of the view that Thames Water neglected its obligations in this case.
    The Board of Thames Water placed reliance on a variety of teams and systems to
    understand the performance of its WWTW and networks. These systems and processes
    were ineffective and focused on a relatively narrow set of indicators or parameters which
    fed into the company's EPA report or were relevant to the company's PCs and ODIs. As a
    result, the Board did not seek sufficient information to adequately assure itself, or Ofwat
    via its annual certificate, as to the company's full compliance position, nor to sufficiently
    scrutinise performance in order to drive improvements and secure compliance.

81. Thames Water has challenged our assessment that its management team and Board did
    not receive, nor seek, sufficient information and assurance on the operational

                                                205
       Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                           on Thames Water

    performance of the company’s wastewater assets and their compliance with relevant
    legal obligations. It also rejected any suggestion that it had improperly assured Ofwat in
    relation to the adequacy of its resources and systems of planning and internal control in
    its RFC and its RCS. In its view, the company has, at all time, followed Ofwat guidance in
    relation to the preparation of the RFC and has made suitable disclosures – including
    disclosures relating to permit compliance in its RFC for 2021 -2022 and 2022-23.

82. We have set out the evidence we have relied on in reaching our findings in relation to
    Thames Water's compliance with Condition P in Sections 3 and 4. This evidence is drawn
    mainly from Thames Water's Board papers and minutes and, in our assessment, indicates
    that, until relatively recently, the focus of the management team and Board was on
    metrics relevant to the Environment Agency's EPA and the company's PCs and ODIs under
    the price control and not on its wider regulatory obligations. Despite Thames Water's
    challenge, our findings in relation to the limited nature of the information being
    presented to, and discussed by Board, also mirrors the findings of the company's own
    internal audit reports which also highlighted the risks arising from the company's narrow
    focus on a limited number of performance indicators and metrics.

83. Similarly, in relation to Thames Water's RFCs, while we recognise that the company has
    now taken steps to report on risks associated with its wider regulatory obligations, it only
    began to do so for the 2021-22 RFC – which post-dates the opening of our investigation.
    Prior to that, and despite the compliance risks which had been identified under the
    company's G2G programme, these were not referred to in earlier RFCs.

84. We welcome the steps that Thames Water has taken/is taking to improve its processes,
    oversight and governance. However, the fact that the company considers that these
    changes are needed also serves to underline the fact that there were deficiencies in its
    previous processes and systems. It is also too early to conclude with any degree of
    confidence that the changes that Thames Water has now put in place are sufficient to
    enable us to conclude that there is no ongoing breach of Condition P. This is because of
    the fact that the company appears to be still uncovering new potential issues and its
    understanding of the steps that it needs to take, and the investment that this will
    require, is still evolving.

85. Finally, in relation to Thames Water's challenge that as it spent all the funds it was
    allocated for its wastewater activities under the price review process in AMP6 and is
    expected to have overspent in AMP7, that it had acted as a reasonable company within
    the confines of the price review process and the need to balance competing priorities
    with a limited budget. We disagree. As explained in our draft decision and as repeated in
    this final decision, it is our assessment that Thames Water failed to act with sufficient
    urgency to address known compliance risks across its wastewater network, that is, it
    failed to act to ensure its compliance with its existing environmental permit conditions.
    Companies, including Thames Water, were funded to meet these permit conditions as
    part of the price review process and also assured us annually that they had sufficient

                                                206
         Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                             on Thames Water

      resources to enable them to do so. Despite this, during the course of our investigation,
      Thames Water identified 157 sites, or two thirds of its sites with permits or TDCs, which
      had failed to meet their FFT permit conditions. Many of its sites were also potentially non-
      compliant in relation to DWF and storm tank capacity. Moreover, Thames Water has
      claimed that had it requested additional funds to enable it to address known compliance
      issues more rapidly, the likelihood is that Ofwat would have rejected that request.
      Leaving aside this unsubstantiated assertion, Thames Water itself has admitted that its
      previous business plans and funding were based upon meeting compliance with existing
      permits. Moreover, even when opportunities did arise for companies to access additional
      investment, for example via the Green Recovery Programme and Accelerated
      Infrastructure Delivery Project, Thames Water failed to take advantage of them. In our
      view, this indicates a failure by the company to prioritise spending on compliance with
      the full range of its legal and regulatory obligations. While Thames Water has challenged
      our assessment, again, these issues and risks had been flagged by its own internal
      reports and reviews – and yet it failed to act. In the 2019 review of the company's
      compliance regime, which was undertaken following the publication of our findings in
      the Southern Water case, for example, it noted that "the governance behind capital
      spend and investment should formally consider and prioritise compliance requirements
      moving forward". This recommendation was in turn based on the fact that "compliance
      risks and resilience for all aspects of permits were not considered as a high priority in
      decisions on capital investment spend and suggested that, in order to mitigate these
      risks, the company's approach to risk prioritisation should be considered; that
      compliance issues should be ranked higher; and that awareness of all compliance
      obligations should be increased".

Key issues raised by other respondents

86. In the preceding sections, we have addressed the key themes and issues which were
    raised by Thames Water and a number of other water companies. In the following section
    we provide an overview of the issues raised by other respondents to our consultation.

Fines should be used to remedy problems in the areas affected by the
conduct

87. A number of respondents 291 called for any fines imposed to be used for the benefit of the
    local areas affected by the breaches we have identified. Where the money should be paid
    and who should be responsible for managing how it is spent varied from respondent to
    respondent (generally based on their local interests), but a common theme was that the
    fines should not simply be paid into HMT's Consolidated Fund.




  Including CCW, River Colne Catchment Area Network, River Chess Association, River Partnerships in
291

London and several members of the public.

                                                  207
          Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                              on Thames Water

88. While we acknowledge the points being made by respondents, it is also important to note
    that Ofwat does not have control over where the penalties we impose are paid to. The
    requirement for financial penalties to be paid into HM Treasury's Consolidated Fund is a
    statutory requirement set out in section 22A(9) WIA91. We are therefore not in a position
    to determine that the penalty be paid elsewhere or how the money is subsequently used.

Fines should not lead to increased customer bills or existing projects being
cancelled

89. Several community groups and members of the public raised concerns that the fines
    imposed would simply be passed on to customers in the form of higher bills and were
    clear that this should not be permitted. Others raised concerns that existing and planned
    work programmes of the water companies (including those related to the environment)
    might be impacted by the drive for cost savings that they felt would likely follow any
    penalty. They called for these programmes to be protected from the cost savings.

90. Our policy is clear that any penalties should be funded by companies and their
    shareholders and not through customer bills. We have made it clear in our decisions for
    PR24 that companies have already been funded to meet existing environmental permit
    limits through previous price controls and so, customers will not be expected to pay twice
    for any remedial work that companies are required to undertake. Where companies are
    required to go beyond existing permit conditions to meet the requirements of our
    enforcement order, however, we will consider requests for funding as part of case for
    enhanced expenditure in price reviews.

Penalties are insufficient – special administration and/or director fines
should be used

91. Several respondents called for us to petition the court to place Thames Water into special
    administration as a result of its repeated contraventions and/or for company directors or
    boards to be personally held to account by way of fines or criminal prosecution.

92. The company's wastewater performance is part of our wider monitoring of the company's
    performance, to inform any further regulatory action is required.

Infiltration is the major cause of problems that needs to be addressed

93. Several community groups 292 expressed the view that groundwater ingress and
    infiltration is the major problem behind spills. They suggested that improving storage
    capacity will not resolve this problem and that replacing, changing the design or lining
    sewers is the only solution. Some surprise was expressed at how little the impact of
    infiltration was discussed in the decision documents. One member of the public claimed


292
      River Colne Catchment Area Network and River Chess Association.

                                                   208
          Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                              on Thames Water

       that the deregulation of the building industry resulted in some sewers being
       misconnected, adding to the infiltration problems.

94. The degree to which infiltration is a problem varies between water companies. As noted
    previously in this document, Thames Water has provided us with information on those
    sites where infiltration was identified as the reason for high spills from its storm
    overflows, as well as details on the plans it has developed to address infiltration. This
    information has been taken into account in our assessment.

Concerns about the strength of Environment Agency engagement with
companies

95. One community group 293 raised concern about the number of critically important
    documents that are being drafted or amended "behind closed doors", making particular
    reference to SOAF and Temporary Discharge Permits that were agreed between the
    Environment Agency and water companies. It was suggested that these should be
    reopened with input from all stakeholders. We note that the Environment Agency recently
    completed a public consultation on a revised SOAF

96. Another group 294 raised concern about the requirement in the proposed enforcement
    order (requirement 3d) for companies to work with the Environment Agency to agree a
    protocol for assessing the impact of discharges to coastal waters and lakes (these are not
    currently covered by SOAF). They noted that their experience in relation to Whitburn
    gives them little confidence that this will be carried out in a timely manner and that it
    will not be controlled by Northumbrian Water to result in an outcome that favours them –
    they would like assurances around this.

97. We note that the way in which the Environment Agency makes policy decision is outside
    of Ofwat's remit and outside the scope of this consultation. However, we have been
    working closely with the Environment Agency on its revised SOAF to ensure our
    expectations are aligned in terms of how such processes reflect the UWWTR obligations.

A7: Penalties framework
1. We apply financial penalties on the facts and evidence in each specific enforcement case. This
   case is part of a sector-wide investigation into all water companies in England and Wales. To
   ensure consistency across the companies subject to this investigation, we have followed our
   Statement of policy with respect to financial penalties; and used a set of guiding principles which
   are described below.

2. Given the serious nature of the evidence presented to us, and in view of our statutory duties to
   enforce, it is important that Ofwat considers the full extent of its penalty setting powers.
   Consequently, we have considered levying penalties across the full range available to us, from
   zero to 10% of relevant turnover.

3. Although we have identified contraventions of separate provisions of Regulation 4 UWWTR, section
   94 WIA and Condition P, given their overlapping nature we have chosen to impose only one penalty
   in respect of all identified breaches.

4. To guide the appropriate penalty level, we have evaluated our cumulative findings, based on the
   evidence provided by the companies, to allocate the case to one of three broad categories: Minor
   (up to 3%), Serious (up to 6%) and Significant (up to 10%) each reflecting the scale and
   seriousness of the breaches. Our approach focuses on indicators of asset performance (as set out
   in the table below) and also considers the extent of relevant additional section 94 WIA91 and
   Condition P failures (considering the expectations we set out in Section 2 at paragraphs 2.124 to
   2.128).

5. Once allocated to one of the categories (Minor, Serious or Significant) we re-consider the full set
   of case evidence in the round, to determine the appropriate penalty level.


    Category           Examples of indicators and failings relevant by            Indicative penalty level
                                         Category

    Significant        With respect to WWTW:                                             Up to 10%


                       •   ≥ 30% of sites potentially non-compliant with FFT
                           requirements and/or
                       •   ≥20% of sites failing to meet Q80 DWF
                       •   Material concerns about other aspects of permit
                           compliance (e.g. storm tank capacity)
                       •   ≥ 30% of WWTW storm overflows spilling more than
                           60 times per year in 2021 (subject to factors
                           described in more detail below) and/or
                       •   A material number of storm overflows without a valid
                           permit, i.e. unpermitted (20%+ of storm overflows).


                       With respect to network:




                                                       233
            Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                                on Thames Water

                        •   ≥ 30% of network storm overflows spilling more than
                            60 times per year in 2021 (subject to factors
                            described in more detail below) and/or
                        •   a material number of unpermitted storm overflows
                            (20%+ of storm overflows).


    Serious             With respect to WWTW:                                           Up to 6%

                        •   ≥ 10% of sites potentially non-compliant with FFT
                            requirements and/or
                        •   ≥10% of sites failing to meet Q80 DWF and/or some
                            concerns about other aspects of permit compliance
                            (e.g. storm tank capacity)
                        •   ≥ 10% of WWTW storm overflows spilling more than
                            60 times per year in 2021 (subject to factors
                            described in more detail below) and/or
                        •   A small number of storm overflows without a valid
                            permit, i.e. unpermitted (10%+ of storm overflows).


                        With respect to network:


                        •   ≥ 10% of network storm overflows spilling more than
                            60 times per year in 2021 (subject to factors
                            described in more detail below) and/or
                        •   a small number of unpermitted storm overflows
                            (10%+ of storm overflows).


    Minor               With respect to WWTW:                                           Up to 3%

                        •   < 10% of sites potentially non-compliant with FFT
                            requirements and/or.
                        •   < 10% of sites failing to meet Q80 DWF.
                        •   No material concerns about other aspects of permit
                            compliance (e.g. storm tank capacity).
                        •   < 10% of WWTW storm overflows spilling more than
                            60 times per year in 2021 (subject to factors
                            described in more detail below) and/or
                        •   A non-material number of storm overflows without a
                            valid permit, i.e. unpermitted (<10% of storm
                            overflows).


                        With respect to network:


                        •   < 10% of network storm overflows spilling more than
                            60 times per year in 2021 (subject to factors
                            described in more detail below) and/or
                        •   A non-material number of unpermitted storm
                            overflows (<10% of storm overflows).


6. Subject to judgement based on wider case-specific evidence and accommodating case-specific
   factors as appropriate, we have approached the assessment of breaches within these three
   categories as follows:


                                                         234
           Notice of Ofwat’s decision to issue an enforcement order and impose a financial penalty
                                               on Thames Water

   •   We recognise that companies have, erroneously, treated the SOAF as establishing the limits of
       their storm overflow remediation obligations. Accordingly, on this occasion, we are penalising
       companies for any breaches of the UWWTR related to spills of more than 60 per year where:
       root cause analysis has not been carried out or has not been carried out in a timely manner;
       where root cause analysis identified hydraulic capacity as the problem but the company has
       not carried out a further SOAF investigation and has no such further investigation planned;
       asset operational and maintenance issues have arisen; or where a company has identified a
       BTKNEEC compatible solution which has not been implemented.

   •   In general, we would consider breaches of the UWWTR to be Significant, even when other
       indicators might be categorised as Serious or Minor. The precise penalty level for a Significant
       breach depends on the cumulative nature of those breaches, also taking into account our
       findings in relation to the general duty in section 94 WIA91 and Condition P.

   •   With respect to breaches of section 94 WIA91, the categorisation of breach has been informed
       by the extent and nature of our findings in relation to UWWTR compliance.

   •   With respect to contraventions of Condition P, we have considered the seriousness of those
       contraventions based on the root causes of the failings and whether these can be attributed to
       management failures.

7. Once the broad level of penalty has been determined in line with the approach above, we
   considered what further adjustments should be applied to reflect the relevant aggravating and
   mitigating factors, as set out in our Statement of policy, with each assigned a weighting within
   the range of 0-10% of the broad penalty level, depending on our judgement based on the
   evidence.

8. Finally, we considered, including in light of all of Ofwat's relevant duties, whether there are any
   exceptional factors which ought to be taken into account to further calibrate the penalty.
